TWENTY-FIRST REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE 
Having been authorised by the Department-Related Parliamentary Standing Committee 
on Industry, I, Ashok Mitra, hereby present the Twenty-first Report of the Committee. 
At the mid point of the fiscal year 1996-97 widespread misgivings were being expressed 
on the uncertain state of industrial growth in the country. The rates of growth of mining, 
power generation and other infrastructure industries were significantly lower compared to 
what these were in the corresponding months of the previous year; the rate of growth of 
manufacturing too was below expectations. Stock exchanges were marked by strong bearish 
sentiments, and there was few evidence of fresh investment activity. At its meeting on 
October 29, 1996 the Committee felt that it would be useful to have a wide-scale survey 
undertaken on its behalf to examine in depth the underlying factors inhibiting industrial 
growth. It was further decided that to facilitate the survey, five Sub-Committees be 
constituted which will assume responsibility for different areas of study. One Sub-Committee 
proposed to analyse the factors inhibiting industrial groups while two other Sub-Committees 
were assigned to concentrate on the problems which directly pertain to the activities of the 
Ministry of Industry, while two other Sub-Committees were assigned to cover the area of 
functioning of the Ministry of Steel and Ministry of Mines respectively. 
The reports finalised by the five Sub-Committees .are placed below. The purpose of this 
brief introduction is to draw attention to some of the more salient conclusions reached by 
the Sub-Committees at the end of their labour. Each Sub-Committee thought it useful to 
visit important metropolitan cities as well as several interior centres of the country in order 
to gain insight at first hand into the problems encountered in the different spheres of 
industrial activity, both organised and non-organised. 
Sub-Committee-III studied at some length the factors which explain the slowing down of 
industrial growth in the current year compared to the trends obtained in 1995-96. While 
arriving at its conclusions, the Sub-Committee has taken into account the generally 
depressed state of industrial growth in the years 1991-92, 1992-93 and 1993-94 as well. 
According to the Sub-Committee, the drying up of the process of investment in the different 
sectors of the economy needs to be reversed as early as possible. lnfrastructural growth in 
particular has been badly affected by the lack of capital formation. This is particularly in 
regard to the power sector. The Sub-Committee is concerned about monetary policy and the 
role in general of the Reserve Bank of India has been less than 'growth-friendly' in recent 
years. In the opinion of the Sub-Committee, the State should play a more active role in 
encouraging investment across-the-board and there must be appropriate resource allocation 
so that all major projects in the State sector should be completed within a given time frame. 
The establishment of a nodal agency which will monitor investments in the infrastructure 
industries and also coordinate between public and private sector activities is, in the view of 
the Sub-Committee, worth consideration. The Sub-Committee· has, in this connection, 
referred to the gradually disappearing role of the Planning Commission in the planning of 
,savings investment and growth in the country, which it regards as unfortunate. 
Sub-Committee-! concentrated on the problems faced by the small-scale industrial 
sector. It has a number of observations concerning the issue of raising the limits of 
investment in re-defining small and tiny sectors. The latest decisions of the government have 
partially 
1 

2 
taken care of the suggestions offered by the Sub-Committee, but perhaps a few new 
questions have reached their head. The Sub-Committee is also of the view that the 
Government has the responsibility to ensure that liberalisation carried to excess does not 
create problems of survival for the small-scale units. The Sub-Committee has comments to 
offer on the limits of commercial turnover for determining concessions with respect to excise 
duties. In this matter too, the budget for 1997-98 contained provisions which will need to be 
examined in the light of the Sub-Committees recommendations. The Sub-Committee has 
strongly U{ged for a fresh census of small-scale industrial units including those engaged in 
fulfilment of specific export obligations. It has expressed concern about the bottlenecks in 
the supply of inputs faced by the small-sector, and it wants the Government to undertake a 
s~cial review of the subsidies and other fiscal concessions to encourage further development 
in small-scale activities. The Committee has some comments to offer on the problems faced 
by the coir industry and the difficulties posed by the high interest rate structure and the 
general reluctance of the banks to offer credit. The Sub-Committee has invited. attention 'to 
the necessity to facilitate supply of gas and power, equipment to the small-scale units. 
Government agencies, in view of the Sub-Committee,_ should take an active interest in 
improving the infrastructure such, as roads, drainage, etc., in areas where the small-scale 
industries are located. It has also suggestions to offer on such issues as a more satisfactory 
integration of activities between the Centre and the State Governments. It is a matter of 
regret, the Committee feels, that several State Governments do not still have a separate 
Directorate. or Department to deal with the problems of small industries. 
Sub-Committee-IV visited the major centres of activities and operations under aegis of 
the Ministry of Mines including Hindustan Copper Limited, Hindustan Zinc Lhruted, 
National Aluminium Corporation, Bharat Gold Mines, the Geological· Survey of India and 
the Indian Bureau of Mines. It has drawn attention to the problems faced by both Hindustan 
Zinc Limited and Hindustan Copper Limited because of the progressive lowering of tariff 
barriers which have affected the marketing of products of these two major units on which 
the ·Government has invested considerable amount in the past. This issue, the Sub­
CqJ;nmittee is of the opinion must be the subject of constant review between the Ministry of 
Mines and the Mini~try of Finance. There must be augmentation of funds, the Sub­
Committee feels, both for the Geological Survey of India and the Indian Bureau ·of Mines to 
enable them expand their activities. The Sub-Committee has commented that the rates of 
duty of royalty· on non-coal items must be uniformly converted from specific to ad valorem 
as a matter of policy. It is also the Sub-Committee's point of view that the Government 
should continue to assume direct responsibility for social welfare work pertaining to the 
workers engaged in the mining areas. 
Sub-Committee-11 visited a number of major industrial centres affected by problems of 
~losure and sickness of units both in the private and public sectors. It set for itself to two 
assignments: (1) review the working of the Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) 
Act, 1985 as well as of the performance of the Board of Industrial and Financial 
Reconstruction towards the restructuring of units in difficulties; and (2) analyse the problems 
that stand in the way of using the resources of the National Renewal Fund not for payment 
of mere 'golden hand shake' to laid-off workers but for a meaningful reconstruction. of sick 
units. 
The Sub-Committee has several comments to offer on the slow pace of work by the 
BIFR. It has a number of suggestions for reforming the Sick Industrial Undertakings Act, to 
enable proceedings before the BIFR could be speeded up and surplus land available to the 
units be made easily available for purposes of the rehabilitation of the units. It has also 

3 
suggestions on how the process of consultations within the BIFR could be telescoped. The 
Sub-Committee is firmly of the view that the resources available with the NRF should be 
deployed to revive and expand the Area Employment Regeneration Schemes. The Sub­
Committee has made specific recommendations with regard to modalities for the revival of 
units belonging to the National Textile Corporation and the North-Eastern Paper Mills 
Corporation. 
It is to be hoped that the Ministries of Industry, the Ministry of SteE!l and the Ministry 
of Mines along with their relevant departments and directorates will carefully e:xamine the 
recommendations of the Sub-Committees. They should expeditiously offer their view on 
those of the recommendations of the Sub-Committees that are easily implementable and 
those they would like to examine· further. The Committee would urge the Ministry that the 
suggestions made by Sub-Committee regarding amendments to the. Sick Industrial 
Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985, reviewing the performance of BIFR and the 
restructuring of the National Renewal Fund are ex~mined in detail and the Ministry's view 
on these recommendations communicated to the Ministry of Finance. 
In any event the Committee will have a further opportunity to gc> into the issues 
covered by the reports of the Sub-Committees at the time of examination of the Demands 
for Grants for 1997~98 and the Annual Reports of the· Ministries concerned.

APPENDIX-II 
STUDY REPORTS OF 
SUB-COMMITTEES 

STUDY REPORT(S) 
OF 
SUB-COMMITTEE-I 

REPORT OF SUB-COMMITTEE-I (OF DEPARTMENT- RELATED 
PARLIAMENTARY STANDING COMMITIEE ON INDUSTRY) ON SMALL SCALE 
INDUSTRY 
The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry at its meeting 
held on the 29th October, 1996, constituted five Sub-Committees including one on Small 
Scale Industry. The Sub-Committee consisted ·:>f the following:-
!. Shrimati Jayawantiben Mehta -- Convenor 
MEMBERS 
2. Shri Maurice Kujur 
3. Shri Rajubhai A. Parmar 
4. Shrimati Anandiben Jethabai Patel 
5. Shri Anil Kumar 
6. Shri Prabhu Dayal Katheria 
7. Shri Namdevrao Diwathe 
8. Shri Shyamlal Bansiwal 
9. Shri Nawal Kishore Sharma 
10. Dr. N. Subramanyam 
11. Shri Anandrao Adsul 
2. The Sub-Committee has been assigned the job of undertaking a review of the role 
played by the small scale industries, including agro-based and other rural industries in the 
context of recent developments of the country and the problems they have encountered. The 
review covers aspects of problems including the need or otherwise of re-defining small-scale 
and tiny sector activities, the policy of reservations applied toward them, a critique of 
taxation and duty structures, appraisal of schemes of financial accommodation preffered by 
banks, financial institutions and State and Union Governments. The factors underlying 
endemic problem of sickness that often visited small-scale and rural industries also formed a 
part of the study. It is often alleged that while statutory and other measures are laid down 
for protecting small-scale industries from intrusion by outsiders, including multinational 
entities, in practice the measures are flouted with impunity, which render the entire legal 
structure of protection infructuous. The issues relevant in this connection also form a part of 
the study of the Sub-Committee. 
3. The Sub-Committee met on the 18th November, 1996 and decided that a detailed 
Questionnaire be prepared covering the area of study of the Sub-Committee. The Sub­
Committee also decided to visit Cochin, Bangalore and Chennai during January, 1997 for 
the purpose of interaction with the concerned people and an on-the-spot study. A copy of 
the Questionnaire alongwith the comments of the Ministry of Industry thereon covering the 
area of study of Sub-Committee is enclosed as ANNEXURE-I. 
4. The Sub-Committee undertook the study visit to Cochin, Bangalore and Chennai 
from the 2nd to 9th January, 1997 and had interaction with (i) the representatives of ape~ 
organisations of manufacturers, .exporters and workers engaged in main small scale industries 
in the States of Kerala, Karnataka and Tamil Nadu; (ii) the representatives of Nationalised 
Banks, Financial Institutions, Reserve Bank of India, etc. on the availability or otherwise of 
17 

18 
finances to the small scale industries and (iii) the representatives of the Government of 
Kerala, Kamataka and Tamil Nadu, respectively, in order elicit their views on the problems 
of small scale industry in their State. The list of representatives with whom the Committee 
had interaction during the course of the study visit is enclosed as ANNEXURE-II. The Sub­
Committee also visited some small scale units at Cochin, Bangalore, Mysore and Chennai 
with a view to see their functioning and for a better comprehension of their problems. The 
list of units visited by the Sub-Committee is enclosed as ANNEXURE-III. 
5. A brief summary of the points that arose during the course of the study visit are 
contained in the succeeding paragraphs. 
I. COCIDN (2ND TO 4TH JANUARY, 1997) 
A. Discussion with Representatives of Apex Organisations of manufacturers, exporters 
and workers engaged in main small scale industries in the State on 2.1.97 
(i) Small Scale Industries in Kerala were engaged in industries based on Coir, 
Coconut, Cashew, Spices, Sea Food, Rubber, etc. 
(ii) Representatives of all categories of Small Scale Industries complained about 
the higher rate of interest on term loans and the conditions precedent to their 
grant like, furnishing of collateral securities, etc. There was a demand that the 
gestation period for the term loan should be a minimum of 2 years of which 
12 inonths should be a interest holiday. The rate of interest on term loan 
should not be more that 6%. The Export Oriented Units in small scale sector 
should be provided with level playing field when compared with Multi-nationals 
that raise loans at an interest rate of 6%. 
(iii) The existing limit on investment in plant and machinery of Rs. 60 lakhs for the 
purpose of excise exemption should be raised to Rs. 1.00 to 2.00 crores. 
(iv) The limit turnover of Rs. 30 lakhs for excise exemption should be raised to 
Rs. 1.00 crore. 
(v) In order to avoid harassment at the hands of authorities collecting Sales-tax, 
Income-tax, etc. only excise duty may be collected from small scale industries. 
(vi) Keeping in view the high cost of advertisement, there should be a central 
subsidy for the purpose. 
(vii) Testing facilities should be made available in various parts of the country at 
concessional rates. 
(viii) Cost of packing for processed foods. in India is almost 65% of the cost of 
production. Concession should be provided in excise duty for packaging 
materials for new products for atleast 2 years from the commencement of 
commercial production. 
(ix) Presently, manufacture of value added products from fresh coconut is a priority 
industry only for Industry Ministry and not for the Reserve Bank of India. This 
should be re-classified as a priority industry for Reserve Bank of India as well 
to enable the Commercial Banks to provide finance on priority basis, to new 
integrated coconut processing units. 

(x) 
(xi) 
(xii) 
(xiii) 
(xiv) 
(xv) 
(xvi) 
(xvii) 
(xviii) 
(xix) 
(xx) 
19 
Workers engaged in Coir are presently unorganised, as a result of which there 
is competition among the small manufacturers who supply their products to the 
big exporters. These small manufacturers do not get reasonable price of their 
products. These small manufacturers may be encouraged to form Cooperatives 
so that their exploitation is checked. 80% of the work force engaged in coir 
industry are women. 
The exporters of Coir desired that the requirements of Minimum Export Price 
and Export Quality Inspection, should be abolished. But, the president, 
Travancore Coir Factories' Workers Union maintained that the requirement of 
Minimum Export price should be continued and the quality inspection before 
export should be strictly implemented because the minimum wages for coir 
workers are based upon the minimum export price. 
Copra processing industry in the State has not b~en able to keep pace with the 
changes ushered in other milling sectors elsewhere in the country. There is a 
need for modernisation of the plants but adequate financial assistance is not 
forthcoming either from Central Government or from the Coconut 
Development Board for converting the out-moded rotaries into expellers. 
100% Export Oriented Units (EOUs) operating outside export promotion 
zones are allowed exemption froin State Sales Tax only for a period of 5 years 
from the date of approval of the unit and,, on the other hand, EOUs situated in 
the Cochin Export Promotion Zone are allowed exemption from Sales Tax 
without any time limit. The exemption should be extended uniformly to all the 
EO Us. 
100% EOUs should get full Central Sales Tax exemption instead of Central 
Sales Tax reimbursement. 
There is a shortage of power in the State which is adversely l!ffecting the 
production of small scale industries. Efforts should be made to ensure more 
power supply to the State from the power grid. The Central Government 
should also permit setting up of more thermal and hydro power projects for the 
State. 
Reluctance on the part of financial institutions to give adequate and timely 
finance is one of the reasons for lack of development of small scale industries 
in Kerala. The Credit Deposit Ratio which is presently 45% should be raised 
to at least 75%. 
Small Scale Industries should be provided credit at lower rates of interest. 
Normally, 20% of the annual turnover should be made available as working 
capital credit. In Kerala, this is less than 5%. Immediate action should be 
taken to enhance the provision of working capital credit to enable the small 
scale units to work profitably. 
There should be an insurance scheme for the small scale industries to cover the 
risk of industrial loans given by financial institutions to small scale units and 
the provisions for collateral security should be done away. A nominal premium 
may be charged and collected by the Bank from the industrial unit and paid to 
the insurance company. If the loan gets struck up, the financial institution can 
get it from the insurance company. 
The Kerala State Small Industries Association· did not favour an abnormal rise 
in the investment limit of Small Scale Industries from the existing Rs. 60 lakhs 
because as per their assessment, 90% of the small scale units had an 
investment of less than F.s. 20 lakhs. Terefore, raising of limits may pave 

20 
the way for large business houses to peneterate and use the facility and benefits 
of this priority sector. Therefore, the investment limit in plant and machinery 
should not be raised beyond Rs. 1.00 crore. 
(xxi) The two Growth Centres sanctioned for Kerala have not yet been established. 
These may be established expeditiously. 
(xxii) The functioning of State Finance Corporations should be reviewed in order to 
improve their efficiency in providing adequate and timely finance to the small 
scale industries. 
(xxiii) There are a large number of sick small scale industries in the State. The 
financial institutions were not adhering to the guidelines given by the Reserve 
Bank of India for their revival. Government should evolve more liberal and 
practical steps for rehabilitation of these sick units. 
(xxiv) All agricultural produce including spices are not getting the sales tax exemption 
under Section 5(3) of the Central Sales Tax Act. The exemption is available if 
it is exported in the same form in which it is· purchased. This is acting as a 
disincentive for manufacturing/processing of value added products. The 
processing and manufacturing units, particularly in small scale sector, generate 
large employment opportunities mostly for women. Therefore, sales tax 
exemption may be given to units engaged in processing and manufacturing of 
value added products in spices. 
(xxv) Under EXIM Policy, a special provision exists for import of clove, cassia and 
cinnamon by traditional importers of these items for stock and sale. As these 
importers are also required to fulfil export obligation by exporting selected 
spices, they underquote prices and somehow fulfil the export obligation. This is 
very badly affecting the entire spices industry. 
(xxvi) As per the Spices Board Act, the Board is responsible only for promoting 
export of spices and spices products (except· Cardamom), whereas the total 
production/development of spices are the responsibility of Union Ministry of 
Agriculture and State Agriculture/Horticulture Departments. These two 
departments lack coordination and export focus. Without basic production 
data, the industry is finding it very difficult to plan proper strategies for 
maximising export of spices and spice products. The Committee .should look 
into this issue and recommend proper coordination with all connected agencies/ 
organisations for appropriate planning and implementation of various schemes. 
(xxvii) In the area of export promotion activities cashew never received adequate care 
and assistance due to it from the Government of India. The details of grant in 
aid received by/sanctioned to the Council from MDA and under Plan for its 
activities during the VIII Plan period were are as follows: 
M.D.A. Rs. 76.61 lakhs 
PLAN FUNDS 
TOTAL 
EXPORT EARNINGS 
Rs. 76.00 lakhs 
Rs. 152.61 lakhs 
Rs 579298 lakhs 
The total export earning .from cashew during VIII period estimated at Rs. 5793 
crores where as the Government assistance for export promotion was only 1.4 
crores constituting 0.02% of the export earnings from cashew. 

21 
(:xxviii) The Cashew Export Promotion Council bad drawn up a few proposals for 
taking up export promotion activities envisaging an expenditure of Rs. 60 
crores and submitted the same to Government of India for clearance under 
Ninth Plan. The Government of India may be persuaded to sanction the 
schemes proposed by the Council and release adequate funds for export 
promotion activities. 
(xxix) The Central Sales Tax Act provides for grant of Sales Tax exemption for 
commodities bought in the course of export. State Governments are levying 
Sales Tax on raw cashewnuts on the ground that raw cashewnuts and cashew 
kernels are two separate commodities and hence. the provision un~er Central 
Sales Tax are not applicable to raw cashewnuts. Therefore, the Central Sales 
Tax Act may be amended in such a way that raw cashewnuts and cashew 
kernels are treated as a single commodity for granting exemption for Sales Tax 
as in the case of groundnuts. Alternatively, the State Government may be 
persuaded to grant exemption to raw cashewnuts from Sales Tax/Purchase Tax 
and other State levies to make cashew kernels competitive in the international 
markets. 
(xxx) The Government of India had introduced schemes for grant of REP licences/ 
Exim Scrips/Special Import Licences, etc. as incentives to exporters. The 
premia realised on the transfer or sale of these enable the exporters to offer 
competitive rates for their goods in the international markets and thereby 
enable them to maintain/improve their export performance. 
Sales Tax authorities in different States assess the exporters to Sales Tax 
on premia received by the exporters on the sale of REP licences/Exim Scrips/ 
Special Import Licences, treating them as sale of goods. This has resulted in 
the exporters losing part of the incentives which the Central Government bas 
thoughtfully provided to them as an export promotion measure. 
Therefore, the incentives and assistance provided by Government of India 
for exports may be formulated in such a way that the benefits are not taken 
away by State Governments.

out of the total of 993. Other main commercial banks which bad their establishment in 
Kerala were S.B.I. with 223 branches, Canara Bank 224 branches, I.O.B., Syndicate Bank

have taken significant steps for providing liberal financial assistance and taken creative 
measures to help the SSI sector.

Finance for monitoring the bank's lending under SSI sector. The Bank also bad a: full­
fledged Technical Consultancy Cell which was well equipped to take up the work relating to 
project apraisals, viability studies, rehabilitation studies, etc. The bank also bad (a) Green 
Channel Qearance Scheme; and (b) Specialised SSI branches in 85 identified districts to

C. Discussions with the Representatives of the State Government of Kerala on 4.1.97 
The representatives of the State Government informed the Committee that the economy 
of the State was basically agrarian, although there had been a decline in the share of 
agriculture in the State's income in the last few years. After the announcement of the New 
Industrial Policy of 1991, the State Gpvernment have followed a two pronged strategy for 
development of industry, viz. (i) provision of requisite infrastructural support for 
establishment of industries; and {ii) extension of promotional support in the form of 
monitory and fiscal incentives, training and marketing support, etc. 
2. The State was a pioneer in setting up the Kerala Industrial lnfrastructural 
Development Corporation (KINFRA) for developing and managing infrastructure for 
industrial units. The Export Promotion Industrial Park at Kochi, Apparel Park at 
Thiruvananthapuram and the Integrated Infrastructure Township at Palakkad were some of 
the ongoing projects of the Corporation. 
3. The KSIDC, the premier promotional agency for industry in the State and the KPC 
the prime lending agency for the SSI, were the two major institutions in the State sector 
which have spearheaded the growth of industry in the last two years. 
4. The Small Scale Sector, with around 1,54,818 registered units as on November 30, 
1996 continued to show a positive growth trend with an average rate of 15%, which is above 
the all India average. It is estimated that the total investment in this sector would be of tht 
order of Rs. 1,74,182 lakhs, employing approximately 8.18 lakhs persons. 
5. The State Government have been implementing various schemes viz.,­
(i) provision of subsidised technical know-how; 

23 
(ii) allotment of developed land and factory shed in industrial estates; 
(iii) provision of 50% of promoter's margin subject to a maximum of Rs. 1 lakh or 
20% of the project cost whichever is less; 
(iv) subsidy at the rate of 15% of Fixed Capital Investment upto a limit of Rs. 15 
lakhs; and 
(v) various schemes for tax exemption, concessional sales tax rates, price preference, 
special incentive for women etc. The State had also taken up the sick unit revival 
programme in a big way. 
6. The Committee was informed that the credit deposit ratio in the State is low as 
compared to other States i.e. 45%. It was mainly due to reluctance on the part of the 
financial institutions to give adequate and timely finance to the SSis. The State Government 
felt that the C.D. ratio should be raised with the co-operation of the commercial banks.

B. Discussions with the Representatives of Banks and Financial. Institutions on 7 .1.97 
The Committee was informed that the banks and financial institutions have been 
implementing the Nayak Committees' recommendations to ensure adequate and timely flow 
of credit. The bankers were also effectively implementing on an on-going basis the Finance 
Ministry's Seven Point Action Plan for the SSis. Referring to the major problems being 
faced by the b;:mks and financial institutions the Committee was informed that there was--
(a) lack of will and integrity on the part of entrepreneurs. Many times, units are 
established just to avail the benefits available from the Government, particularly, in 
case of small loans; 
(b) not disclosing the entire operations to the Bankers. Failure to submit the 
information/requirements like financial statements, stock statements etc. called for 
in time; 
(c) diversion of funds for acquisition of fixed assets and assets outside the business­
which hampers the day-to-day functions of the unit; and 
(d) resorting to overtrading by borrowing funds at very high cost from outside sources. 
2. Suggesting various measures for overall development of SSI sector the representatives 
of the Syndicate Bank informed the Committee that government should provide adequate 
data base for use of SSI units in order to help them in placing their investments etc. They 
also emphasized upon the need for frequent interaction by various agencies with the industry 
associations to tackle the problems. 
C. Discussions with the Representatives of State Government on 7 .1.97 
The representatives of the State Government informed the Committee that Karnataka 
was a pioneer in industrial development in the country. Development of SSis had been a 
very significant feature of Karnataka's economic development in the recent years. There 
were 1628 SSI units in the State in the year 1960 and the figure stood at 1,75,380 by the end 
of March, 1996. The largest concentration of SSI units in the State were in food and 
beverages followed by textile and wood products. Major segments of small scale industries in 
the State were electronics, beedies, agarbathi, readymade garments, bakery and roofing 
tiles. 
2. The State Government had established a Karnataka State Financial Corporation to 
provide finance to medium and small scale industries in the State. The State had also set up 
a Karnataka Industrial Investment & Development Corporation for providing finance to 
large scale industries in the State. The Government of India had approved the establishment 
of Growth Centres at Hassan, Dharwad and Raichur. Besides, Government of Karnataka 
have also proposed setting up of seven mini Growth Centres at Bijapur, Kolar, Chitradurga, 
Chikmagalur, Gadag, Nippani and Bellary. 
3. The State Government was adopting the following measures to promote industrial 
development in the State:- • 
(i) A new industrial policy for the period 1996-2001 has been announced by the 
Government of Kamataka which lays great stress on development of infrastructure 
facilities. 
(ii) Karnataka Udyog Mitra has been set up to streamline the activities of Single 
Window Agency in the State. 
(iii) An export promotion park is being set up at Whitefield near Bangalore to promote 
exports. 
(iv) A foreign investment promotion board has been set up. 

26 
(v) The State Government has announced new Textile Policy in August, 96. 
(vi) A programme called VISWA has been launched in 1991 with the objective of providing 
continuous employment to artisans. 
(vii) Subsidy scheme to tiny sector food processing industries has been announced. 
(viii) Karnataka Council for Technology Upgradation has been set up.

investment for small scale could then be revised upto one crore.

Scale Industries was simple, but, credit was not flowing in adequate quantity to the SSis. 
3. SIDBI has not been functioning properly. The SSI had the hope that SIDBI would 
cater to the needs to Tiny and Small Scale Industries but, it had turned to be a mini IDBI. 
The Small Industries Management Association suggested that the SIDBI should become a 
full fledged Bank funding the Small Sca]e Industry directly with at least two branches

( a) Most of the SSI units are started by technocrats or by individuals who have practical

view. Financial assistance of Rs. 250 crores to Small Scale Industrialists was provided 
during the year 1995-96. A similar quantum of assistance has been provided during the 
year 1996-97 also. As of date, 30% of the total credit have been provided to Small 
Scale Industries, extended by all the Commercial Banks in Tamil Nadu.

RECOMMENDATIONS 
1. The Committee notes that the limits on investment in the plant and machinery of 
Rs. 60 lakhs (Rs. 75 lakhs in the case of export oriented units) for classification of an 
industry as a Small Scale Industry were fixed quite some time back. There have been a 
demand from almost every section of the Industry with whom the Committee interacted 
during the course of the study visit for suitable increase in the investment limit. ':fhe 
Committee, accordingly, recommends that the existing limits on investment may be revised 
upward to Rs. 1.00 crore (Rs. 1.25 crore in the case of export oriented units) for the 
purpose of classifying any industry as a Small Scale Industry. 
2. The Committee notes that the limits of annual turn over of Rs. 30 lakhs, Rs. 50 
lakhs and Rs. 75 lakhs for concessions in excise duty in varying degrees were determined 
quite some time back. The Committee recommends that these limits may be revised to 
Rs. 1.00 crores. Rs. 2.00 crores and Rs. 3.00 crores, respectively, taking into consideration 
the increase in the cost of production that has taken place over the period of time. 
3. The Sub-Committee notes that the problems being faced by the SSI are numerous. In 
order that the State Governments are able to pay adequate attention to these problems, the 
Sub-Committee recommends that there should be a separate Directorate or Ministry for the 
Small Scale Industry in the States. 
4. The Committee expresses its dissatisfaction over the fact that no census has taken 
place after the last census of Small Scale Industry which was held for the year 1987-88 and 
that the Central Ministry has no data to indicate the trends of manufacture of reserved and 
non-reserved items after the year 1987-88. The Committee feels that the Government would 
not be able to take proper decisions with regard to the promotion of the Small Scale 
Industry in the absence of basic statistics pertaining to these industries. It is also desirable 
that such statistics should be maintained at the State level even as had been pointed out by 
some of the bankers during the. course of the study visit. The Sub-Committee accordingly 
recommends that the Ministry of Industry sh9uld take immediate steps for undertaking the 
476/RS F-3-B 

29 
census of the Small Scale Industries and the State Governments may also be persuaded in this 
regard. 
5. The Committee notes with dissatisfaction that there is no clear cut machinery at 
present for monitoring whether the large/medium sector industries were complying with the 
requirement of export obligation of a minimum of the 75% of its production in case they 
create fresh capacity in an item in the reserved area. The Committee feels that non­
compliance of this condition would seriously affect the protective cover provided to the 
Small Scale Industry. The Committee further feels that obtention of a bond from the parties 
by the DGFT for the purpose of fulfilling the export obligation is not sufficient enough to 
ensure compliance. The Sub-Committee accordingly recommends that the compliance of the 
provisions of export obligation should be monitored more meticulously in the interest of the 
Small Scale Sector. 
6. The Sub-Committee is not convinced with the position as stated by the Ministry of 
Industry that there is no distinction between reserved and non-reserved items on the issue of 
exemption from excise duties. In the opinion of the Committee, relaxation in excise duty is a 
very valuable concession but the same has been denied to the industries engaged in the 
manufacture of reserved items. The Sub-Committee feels that the existing practice negates 
the very concept of reservation of items for the Small Scale Sector. The Sub-Committee, 
accordingly, recommends that the Government should review this aspect and see if 
concessions in excise duty can be allowed on the basis of the criteria whether an industry in 
manufacturing an item in the reserved list. 
7. The Sub-Committee notes that the laws framed by Government for the protection of 
SSis are not being properly implemented. The Sub-Committee accordingly, recommends that 
the SSis should be protected from undue competition on account of the entry of big companies 
in the sphere of activities reserved for Small Scale Sector. The Sub-Committee further 
recommends that areas reserved for SSI must be vigorously monitored against encroachment 
by MNCs and large companies and severe action must be taken against the infringing 
company. It not only harms the cause of SSI but it violates Indian laws as well. \ 
8. The Single Window Scheme deviced by Government for the convenience of SSis 
was reported not to befunctioning properly. The Sub-Committee, accordingly, recommends 
that the bottlenecks in the implementation of the Single Window System may be examined 
through interaction between the representatives of the Central and State Governments and 
suitable remedial measures be taken up. Government may consider placing the Single Window 
System effective under a separate Director appointed for SSI by the Directorate which shall , 
look into all aspects ranging from registration and setting up of the unit, providing technical, 
financial and marketing assistance and coordination between the Central ·and the State 
Departments. 
9. The Sub-Committee notes with satisfaction that the Ministry of Industry had taken 
up with the Ministry of Finance in their pre-budget proposal for allowing some fiscal 
concessions to the SSis for promotion of research and development. The Sub-Committee 
recommends that the Ministry of Industry should pursue this matter with the Ministry of 
Finance once again for the purpose of promotion of Small Scale Industry. 
10. The Sub-Committee during the course of its study visit noted that the SSis lacked 
the resources for undertaking Research and Development activities for upgradation of the 
technology at their own level. There was also a demand for provision of making facilities 
from the Government side. The Sub-Committee accordingly recommends that the 
Government should take more initiative for making available latest technology to Small Scale 
... , 

30 
Sector so that their products are cost effective and receptive in the market. Government 
should also take Immediate steps for enhancing marketing facilities to the SSI units. 
11. The Committee notes that the Coir Industry is presently unorgc-·1ised and that 80% 
of the work force engaged in Coir Industry are women. The Committee recommends that the 
technology being adopted in the Coir Industry needs to be modernised for the conv~nience of 
the workt!rs engaged in the industry majority of which are women. 
12. Most of the representatives of the manufacturers and the exporters at the places 
visited by the Sub-Committee complained that they were not getting adequate financial 
support from the Commercial Banks and the Financial Institutions. Some of them also 
complained about the higher rate of interest being charged in the country starting from 16%. 
It was brought to the notice of the Committee that the rate of interest world over was 
around 6% and therefore, the Small Scale Units in the country, particularly, the ones 
engaged in export did not have equal level playing field as compared to their competitors in 
the open market. The Sub-Committee recommends that the Ministry of Industry may take up 
these issues with the Ministry of Finance. The Sub-Committee further recommends that 
Government should charge lower rate of interest on loans advanced to Small Scale Industries. 
In addition to reducing the interest rates for the SSI units, their debt equity should be raised 
to 4:1. 
13. There·· were complaints from many quarters regarding the complicated procedure 
prescribed for sanction of loans to the Small Scale Industries.. The Sub-Committee 
accordingly recommends that the Commercial Banks should come out with a check list for the 
information of the general public for the purpose of obtaining loan so that the unnecessary 
delay in the course of complying with the procedural requirements is avoided. 
14. The position regarding repayment of loans advanced by Commercial Banks was not 
reported to be very good during the course of the Sub-Committees visit. The representatives 
of the manufacturers at Cochin proposed for an insurance scheme for the Small Scale 
Industries to cover up the risk of industrial loans given by the Commercial Banks/Financial 
Institutions in order to do away with the requirement of collateral security. The Sub· 
Committee recommends that the Ministry may examine the feasibility of this proposal because 
this could increase the now to finances to the SSis which is restricted hitherto. Banks while 
discounting the Bills of Small Scale Industries, in case of default i.e non payment by large 
company should only proceed against the larger companies. The Sub-Committee further 
recommends the Ministry of Industry to exaunine the possibility of SIDBI entering into direct 
lending as suggested by the Small Scale Industries Management Association at Madras. The 
refinancing of SSis by NABARD may however, continue. 
15. The Committee notes with dissatisfaction that the number of sick units in the States 
visited by the Sub-Committee were very large. The representatives of financial institutions at 
Bangalore were posed with the questiO.,.I) of rehabilitation of the sick units in their State to 
which no satisfactory reply came forward. The Sub-Committee recommends that the Banks 
and Financial Institutions should stimulate their efforts and should adopt a more liberal policy 
for revival of such sick units. The Sub-Committee further recommends that the 
recommendations of Naik Committee in this regard should be implemented strictly. 
16. One of the main reasons for sickness of industry which was brought to the notice of 
the Sub-Committee during the course of the discussions with the representatives of banks 
and financial institutions at Bangalore was that the SSis did not get adequate working 
capital. Although commercial banks in all the three States have stated that !hey are 
implementing the recommendations of the Naik Committee bat it is feared that the SSis are 

31 
not getting working capital at the rate of 20% of the turnover as prescribed by the Naik 
Committee. The Sub-Committee recommends that the financial Institutions and banks should 
make available the required working capital to the SSis because insumcient financial 
assistance 'is of no use either to the unit or to the rmanclal institution. 
17. The Sub-Committee was informed during the course of its visit to the Handicraft 
Unit of Poompuhar, Mahabalipuram that the craftsmen were required to buy sandalwood of 
their own and it took them 15 days to one month to carve one piece. The cost of 
sandalwood being quite high, these craftsmen were subjected to serious hardship. Keeping 
this in view, the Committee recommends that the local nationalised banks and the financial 
institutions of the States should come forward for their rescue and they should be provided 
with loans for purchase of sandalwood for short durations upto the sale of their carvings. 
18. The Committee is unhappy to note that the Small Scale Industry almost everywhere 
faces a problem on account of the non-availability/duplicate of 'C' Forms leading to 
imposition of high rate of interest by way of penalty on the entrepreneurs without any fault 
of theirs. The SSis are subjected to enormous harassment on this account. The Committee 
recommends that corruption on account of the non-avilabllity of 'C' Forms should be done 
away with Immediately by making the forms available easlly or the State Governments should 
amend their relevent rules, accordingly. 
19. The Committee notes that almost all SSI units were being troubled off and on by 
the Inspectors from different Departments both of the Central and State Governments. The 
representatives of manufacturers at Cochin pleaded before the Committee that the SSis 
which were managing their affairs of their own should atleast be left free from the unlawful 
interference of inspectors of various Government Agencies. The Sub-Committee accordingly, 
recommends that Government should take Immediate steps for doing away with tb.e unlawful 
interference of the Inspectors in the activities of the Small Scale Industries. There should be 
an end to the 'Inspector Raj.' 
20. The Sub-Committee during the course of, the study visit noted that industry as a 
whole and the Small Scale Industry in particular are faced with power shortage. The· 
Committee was informed that there was a 30% power cut in Kerala for the industrial sector. 
The power situation was not satisfactory even in Kamataka and Tamil Nadu. In the view of 
the Sub-Committee, power is one .of the most important input in industrial production and it 
is not possible for the small scale units to go in for captive power generation. The larger 
units may, of course, manage to arrange for captive p.>wer generation but, for both category 
of manufacturers, products do not remain cost effective. The Sub-Committee accordingly 
recommends that the Ministry of Industry, realising of importance of power in the industrial 
sector, should immediately coordinate with the Central Ministry of Power so that the short 
term as well as the long term measures could be taken to meet the requirement of power of 
the Small Scale Sector. 
21. The Sub-Committee during the course of its study visit noted that the SSis have not 
been provided with adequate infrastructure facilities like roads, drainage, sanitation, 
surrounding cleanlines, etc. The Sub-Committee accordingly recoliunends that- ~e Central 
and the State Governments should take immediate steps tor the development of· such 
infrastructural facilities for the SSis. 
22. Many of the SSis visited by the Sub-Committee, complained .about non-availability 
of raw materials at reasonable prices and there was also a demand of subsidy from certain 
category of SSis. An example in this regard is Aggarbatti Industry at Kamataka which 
brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee the imminent threat of shortage of bamboo 
..... 

32 
sticks resulting in the closure of the industry. They also demanded either a quota or 
concession in excise duty on perfumes used for Aggarbatti Industries. The Sub-Committee 
accordingly recommends that Government should take immediate steps to ensure that raw 
material is available to the SSis at subsidised rates and quota should be allocated wherever 
required. The Sub-Committee further recommends that SSis engaged in the areas of 
educatioD,_.&n-aud--defence should. ~.aUowed -special facUitie~. 
23. The Sub-Committee during the course of its study visit noted that the workers 
engaged in the SSis were not provided with the basic amenities like drinking water, toilet 
facilities, accommodation, creche facilities, etc. The Sub-Committee recommends that 
Government should pay more attention towards the implementation of rules and regulations 
in this regard •. 
24. The Sub-Committee is constrained to note that the ESI contribution deducted from 
the salary of the workers in the SSis is not being utilised for their benefit. The health 
services provided under the ESI being far from satisfactory, the workers are compelled to go 
in for private treatment which they get easily and without any loss of salary. The Sub· 
Committee accordingly recommends that (a) the amolJnt to be spent by Government on the 
health care of the workers should be given directly to the workers in any suitable form like 
medic~ allowance etc. so that they are able to derive an actual benefit: and (b) steps may be 
taken to meet expenditure on education of the children of employees of SSI Units and 
allowapce like Children Education Allowance should be given to them. The Sub-Committee 
further recommends that industries with less than SO workers should be allowed to opt either 
for ESJC or Workmen Compensation with medical benefit through private insurance. 
25. Various types of problems faced by the Small Scale Industries were brought to the 
notice Qf the Sub-Committee during the course of the study visit. Many of these problems 
had a b~aring on the State Government. Some of the problems had a bearing on the Central 
Government also. To name a few, the Aggarbatti Manufacturers resented the notification of 
Aggarbatti Industry under section 85 of the Factories Act. Another example is that of the 
problem posed by the Leather Manufacturers Association at Bangalore complaining that the 
State Government was not giving preference to their product and were placing orders for 
supply of leather belts to U.P. Government. Yet another example in this regard is the 
grievance of manufacturers of beedi regarding categorisation of beedi industry as a non­
priority industry. It was brought to the notice of the Committee that in the Agra-Ferozabad 
region, G;,ts was supplied to the Small Scale Industries @ Rs. 5100-55()(Y-per cubic meter as 
compared to the rate of Rs. 1800-35()()1.. per cubic meter at which it is being supplied in 
other part~ of the country. The Sub-Committee is not satisfied with this position and it 
strongly recommends that the supply of gas to the SSis at Agra-Ferozabad areas may be made 
~t the same rate at which.it is being supplied to SSis in other parts of the country. A women 
entrepreneur at Bangalore engaged in the dealing of note books for the school children 
pointed out that duty on the note books should be done away with. These are serious 
problems in the view of the Sub-Committee which should be redressed urgently. 
26. The Sub-Committee, however, feels that multiple agencies are involved for the 
redressal <;>f these problems. In order to redress these grievances, the Sub-Committee 
recommen4s that there should be a constant interaction between the Industries Department of 
Central and State Governments. The representatives of the Finance Department of the 
Central Government and the State Governments should also be associated in these meetings 
because mo$t of the problems of Small Scale Industry have a financial bearing also. The Sub­
Committee further. recommends that there should be a periodic Secretary level meetings of the 
Industries ~partment of States and the Central Government in which the representatives of 

33 
the Finance Department of the Central and State Governments be also present. The Ministry 
of industry should also consider the possibility of having Minister level meetings between the 
Central and the State Governnl~nts at reasonable intervals. The Sub-Committee feels that this 
would go a long way in redressing the grievances of the Small Scale Industry. 
27. The Sub-Committee recommends that Goveriunent should interact with the concerned 
SSis every three months for implementation and follow lip-or -the ·recommenaaUons or· the 
Sub-Committee. 
28. The Sub-Committee feels that the Small Scale entrepreneurs are doing a wonderful 
job for the country and every effort should be made to encourage them. They are providing 
large number of jobs to the people of the country. The Sub-Committee, accordingly, 
recommends that Government should institute a President's Award for the best SSI unit on 
the criteria of quality and production. 
Summary of the Recommendations 
The recommendations of the Sub-Committee are summarised as follows: 
(1) the existing limits on investment in plant and machinery may be revised upward to 
Rs. 1.00 crore (Rs. 1.25 crores in the case of export oriented units) for the purpose 
of classifying any industry as a Small Scale Industry. 
(2) The Committee notes that the limits of annual turn over of Rs. 30 lakhs, Rs. 50 
lakhs and Rs. 75 lakhs for concessions in excise duty in varying degrees were 
determined quite some time back. the Committee recommends that these limits 
may be revised to Rs. 1.00 crore, Rs. 2.00 crores and Rs. 3.00 crores, respectively, 
taking into consideration the increase in the cost of production that has taken place 
over the period of time. 
(3) There should be a separate Directorate or Ministry for the Small Scale Industry in 
the States. 
( 4) the Ministry of Industry should take immediate steps for undertaking the census of 
the Small Scale Industries and the State Governments may also be persuaded in 
this regard. 
(5) The Compliance on the provisions of export obligation should be monitored more 
meticulously in the interest of the Small Scale Sector. 
( 6) The Sub-Committee is not convinced with the position as stated by the Ministry of 
Industry that there is no distinction between reserved and non-reserved items on 
the issue of exemption from excise duties. In the opinion of the Committee, 
relaxation in excise duty is a very valuable concession but the same bas been denied 
to the industries engaged in the manufacture of reserved items. The Sub­
Committee feels that the existing practice negates the very concept of reservation of 
items. for the Small Scale Sector. The Sub-Committee, accordingly, recommends 
that the Government should review this aspect and see if concessions in excise duty 
can be allowed on the basis of the criteria whether an industry is manufacturing an 
item in the reserved list. 
(7) The Sub-Committee further notes that the laws framed by Government for the 
protection of SSis are not being properly implemented. The Sub-Committee, 
accordingly, recommends that the SSis should be protected from undue 
comp,tition on account of the entry of big companies in the sphere of activities 
reserved for small scale sector. The Sub-Committee further recommends that areas 
reserved for SSI must be vigorously monitored against encroachment by MNCs and 
large companies and severe action must be taken against the infringing company. It 
not only harms the cause of SSI but it violates Indian laws as well. 

34 
(8) The botOenecks in the implementation of the Single Window System may be examined 
throop Interaction between the representatives of the Central and State Governments 
and suitable remedial measures be taken up. Government may consider placing the 
Single Window System effective under a separate Director appointed for SSI by the \ 
Directorate which shaD look Into all aspects ranging from registration and setting up 
of the unit, providing teclmieal, financial and marketing assistance and coordination 
between the Central and the State Departments. 
(9) The ·sub-Committee notes with satisfaction that the Ministry of Industry had taken up 
with the Ministry of Finance in their pre-budget proposal for allowing some fiscal 
~ncessions to the SSis for promotion of reSearch and development. The Sub- · 
COmmittee recommends U.at the Ministry of Industry should pursue this matter with 
the Ministry of Finance once again for the purpose of promotion of Small Scale 
lndustnr. 
(10) Government should take more initiative for making available latest technology to 
small scale sector so that their products are cost effective and receptive in the market. 
Government should also take immediate steps for providing marketing taenities to 
the SSI units. 
(11) The Committee recommends that the technology being adopted in the coir industry 
needs to be modernised for the convenience of the workers engaged in the industry, 
80%. of which are women. 
(12) The Ministry of Industry may take up the ma~ter regarding problems being faced by 
SSis in getting adequate financial support from commercial banks/financial 
Institution with the Ministry of Finance. 
The Government should charge lower rate of interest on loans advanced to small 
scale Industries in addition to reducing the Interest rates for the SSI units, their debt 
equity should be raised to 4:1. 
(13) There were complaints from many quarters regarding the complicated procedure 
prescribed for sanction of loans to the Small Scale Industries. The Sub-Committee, 
accordingly, recommends that the Commercial Banks should come out with a check 
list for the Information of the general public for the purpose of obtaining loan so that 
the unnecessary delay In the course of complying with the procedural requirements is 
avoided. 
(14) The Ministry may examine the feasibnity of the prdposal of an Insurance scheme for 
the SSis to cover up the risk of Industrial loans given by commercial banks/rmancial 
institutions in order to do away with the requirement of collateral security because 
this could increase the Dow of rmances to the SSis which is restricted hitherto Banks 
while discounting the BOis of Small Scale Industries in case of default i.e. non 
payment by large company should only proceed against the large companies. The 
Sub-Committee further recommends the Ministry of Industry to examine the 
possibnity of SIDBI entering into direct lending as suggested by the Small Scale 
Industries Management Association at Madras. The refinancing of SSis by NABARD 
may however, continue. 
(15) The Banks and Financial Institutions should stimulate their efforts and should adopt 
a more liberal policy for revival of such sick units. The Sub-Committee further 
recommends that the recommendations of Naik Committee in this regard should be 
implemented strictly. · 
(16) The financial institutions and banks should make available ~~-;quired working 
capital to ~e SSis because insumcient financial assistance is of'i'IP Us, either to the

Answers 
Criteria for calling an industry a small 
scale industry is investment in plant & 
machinery which is, at present, upto 
Rs. 60 lakhs. In the case of ancillary 
and export oriented SSI units, it is upto 
Rs. 75 lakhs. This criteria is uniform for 
all the items. 
In April 1967, only 47 items were 
reserved for small scale sector which 
was increased upto 504 items in April 
1978 after their conversion into National 
Industrial Oassification (NIC) code. 
The number increased to 807 items in 
1978 itself. Then it increased to 873 
items in· October 1984. There was some 
decline in the number thereafter and it 
came down to 835 in March 1989 and 
increased to 836 in July 1989 and for 
the last seven years it is stagnant at 836. 
Hence, it is not correct to say that SSis 
are drifting from reserved items to non­
reserved items. 
The last Census of Small Scale 
Industries was held for the year 1987-
88. This office has no data to indicate 
the trend of the manufacture of 
reserved items and non-reserved items 
separately after 1987-88. 
No clearcut machinery, at present, 
exists for monitoring this aspect. It is 
because while the licence is granted by 
the Department of Industrial Policy & 
Promotion, exports are regulated by 
Ministry of Commerce and the item 
might fall under the administrative 
control of some other Ministry like 
Petroleum, Chemicals, Food Processing, 
Electronics, Textiles, etc. Action is 
taken only when some complaints are

There are no separate concessions to

and non-reserved items. There is no 
distinction between two.

De-licensing of industrial sector has 
opened up the sector (whether large, 
medium or small) for setting up units 
for manufacture of delicensed items. 
Now, they are not required to obtain 
any permission or licence excepting 
some items reserved for Government or 
SSI sector. As a result, any large unit 
can manufacture any item and thus can 
pose a threat/competition to the units 
producing same items in the small 
sector. The protection zone has been 
narrowed by reduction of tariffs, SSI 
units are eligible for full excise

Taxation Policy 
1. What are the concessions in (i) Excise 
duty (ii) Import duty (iii) Sales Tax to 
SSis engaged in manufacture of (a) 
reserved items (b) non-reserved items. 
Whether there is any distinction in the

Answers 
exemption, i.e., Rs. 30 lakhs and 
concessional exemption upto Rs. 75 
lakhs. In addition, there were nil rate of 
duty on many items upto 1994-95 which 
were withdrawn for large number of 
items. Higher the excise duty on any 
item large is the zone of protection. 
Now if the duty has been reduced from 
say 80% earlier to 30% now this zone 
of protection has been reduced. 
Further, large scale units avail 
MODV AT and the effective rate of 
excise duty paid by the large scale 
sector is less. SSI sector cannot avail 
MODVAT because of excise 
exemption, procurement of inputs from 
retail market and inability to produce 
necessary documents. 
The 2nd All India Census of Registered 
Small Scale Units was held in the year 
1991 for the base year 1987-88. 
Concessions in excise duty are full 
exemption upto annual turnover of Rs. 
30 lakhs, 10% less than the normal duty 
subject to the minimum of 5 percentage 
points between Rs. 30 lakhs to Rs. 50 
lakhs and 5 percentage less than normal 
duty subject to the minimum of 5% 
upto a turnover of Rs. 75 lakhs. 
There is no concession to the SSI units 
available on import duty. 
Sales tax concessions are provided by 
the State Governments. However, there 
is practically no reduction in duty. The 
State Governments mostly provide sales 
tax loan or deferment of sales tax 
payment etc. The quantum and nature 
of these concessions varies from State to 
State. 
None of the above mentioned three 
fiscal concessions make any distinction

Answers 
between reserved and non-reserved 
items. 
For the excise exemption scheme the 
criteria is not the identification of unit 
in terms of investment in plant & 
machinery but annual turnover. Any 
unit whose annual turnover is less than 
Rs. 300 lakhs whether registered or 
unregistered are entitled for excise 
exemption. 
The Deptt. of SSI&ARI has been 
requesting Ministry of Finance for 
enhancement of SSI exemption ceiling 
for the last four years on the ground of 
cost escalation after 1986-87 and 
inflation but the same has not been 
accepted by Ministry of Finance 
because:-
(i) Only 2% of the 27 lakh SSI units 
are registered with Central Excise 
authorities . 
. (ii) 90% of the SSI units have turnover 
less than Rs. 30 lakhs and all such 
units are exempted from excise 
duty. 
(iii) Loss of excise revenue of large 
magnitude cannot be afforded 
when there is a big budget deficit. 
There is no distinction between reserved 
and non-reserved items on the issue of 
exemption from excise duty. 
Exempting an item from excise duty 
comes under the administrative purview 
of Ministry of Finance. A copy of this 
question ~as been sent to Deptt. of 
Revenue, ··~Ministry of Finance to 
ascertain the reasons for non-grant of 
excise exemption to Gas Stove Lighters.

The main items for which demand for 
exci_se exemption is pending frOlll. the 
SSI units are Wrist watches, 
Polyurethene, Sandal wood oil and 
Computer components. We have been 
including these items in our pre-budget 
proposal being sent to Ministry of 
Finance every year for the last four 
years. We shall be again including these 
items for excise exemption in our pre­
budget proposal for 1997-98. 
No special concessions are available to

with Ministry of Finance in our pre­
budget proposal. However, there has 
been no positive response. We shall be 
taking up this matter again.

industrial units in rural and backward 
areas for the SSI units were applicable 
only upto March 1990. The Government 
did not decide to extend it beyond that 
date since similar exemptions are . now 
available under Section 80 lA. ·This 
Section provides for five year tax 
holiday for the units in the specified 
backward areas. 
Yes, it means that the fiscal benefit 
available to SSI units are shrinking that 
is why this department has been taking 
this issue with Ministry of Finance 
through pre-budget proposals.

Sickness in small scale Industries 
1. Whether the number of sick industrial 
units is on an increase during the past 5 
years? If so, the reasons of failure to 
contain this trend. 
Answers 
This department has taken up this 
matter with Ministry of Finance to allow 
flat rate of Income Tax as in the case of 
shopkeepers for the tiny units, i.e., for 
those units whose investment in plant-&-~ 
machinery is less than Rs. 5 lakhs. The 
flat rate, however, will not be justifiable 
in respect of large SSI units since their 
turnover and consequent profit may be 
very high and it may be difficult to fix a 
flat tax. 
The matter comes under the 
administrative purview of Ministry of 
Finance. A copy of the question has 
been forwarded to them.

compared to borrowal accounts has 
been hovering between 7% to 8.5% 
during the last five years with marginal 
variation. The number of sick industrial

the competitive setting. A number of

possible exposure to intermediate and

thereby promote marketing prospects. It

opportunity to promote sales in these 
exhibitions but it can safely be stated

widespread awareness of SSI 
manufacturers which gives sweeping 
advantage to the sector as a whole.

financial position, remains tied up and 
they are not able to provide finance to 
new SSI units. Banks have been 
requested to complete the' appraisal 
quickly and take over the working 
capital component at the earliest. 
The matter comes under the 
administrative purview of Ministry of 
Labour. Statutory laws have been 
enacted to protect the interest of 
labourerS'\vorkers. The Employees' 
State Insurance Act, 1948 (modified 
from time to time) provides for benefits 
to employees in case of sickness, 
maternity and employment injury to 
workers employed in or in connection 
with work in factories. 
There is a consistent demand for 
changes in various labour laws 
applicable to small scale industries, e.g. 
Provident Fund Act, ESI Act, Factories 
Act, etc. Application of these laws have 
two procedural implications: compulsion 
for preparation and maintenance of 
recordstegisters, etc. for fulfilment of 
the obligations under these legislations 
and visit of the Inspectors to the units 
to ensure and enforce compliance. The 
application of above laws affect tiny 
units the most. The tiny entrepreneurs 
are already burdened with the day to 
day responsibilities of purchase of raw 
materials, running of the machines, 
marketing of products, etc. Over and 
above, the entrepreneurs are required 
to maintain records in compliance of the 
labour related legislations. To that 
extent, the time available with them for 
production and marketing activities 
become less. The visits of the inspectors 
in the factory premises may cause 
further harassment of the 
entrepreneurs. The State Industries 
Ministers' Conference was organised in 
June 1993 to consider issues relating to 
simplification of rules and procedures

Ministers had made some 
recommendations regarding relaxation 
or changes in some labour laws.
