STUDY REPORT(S) 
OF 
SUB-COMMITTEE-II 

REPORT OF THE ~..COMMITTEE·D ON THE FUNcriONING 
OF NATIONAL RENEWAL FUND (NRF) AND BOARD FOR 
INDUSTRIAL AND FINANCIAL RECONSTRUCTION (BIFR) 
I, the Convenor of Sub-Committee-IT (of the Department-related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Industry) having been authorised by the Sub-Committee, present its 
Report on the functioning of NRF and BIFR, 
The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Comlnittee on Industry in its meeting 
held on the 29th October, 1996, constituted five Sub-Committees on different subjects. The 
Sub-Committee-IT was assigned . the job of studying the functioning of National Renewal 
Fund (NRF) vis-a-vis its objectives set at the time of i~ inception in 1992, and the Board for 
Industrial and Financial Reconstruction (BIFR) with reference to some of the sick industrial 
units. The Sub-Committee consisted of the following Members : 
1. Shri Sanat Mehta-Convenor 
MEMBERS 
2. Shri Govindrao Adik 
3. Shrimati Jayaprada Nahata 
4. Choudhary Chunni Lal 
S. Shri M.K. Patil 
6. Shri Naval Kishore Rai 
7. Shri Sunil Khan 
2. The Sub-Committee in its first meeting held on the lOth December, 1996, decided to visit 
Ahmedabad, Mumbai, Nepanagar-(Indore) and Calcutta from 6th to lOth January, 1997 to 
hold discussions with the management and trade unions representatives of some of the sick 
units. ' 
3. Accordingly, the Sub-Committee visited Ahmedabad on the 6th, Mumbai on the 7th, 
Indore on the 8th and Calcutta on the 9th and lOth January, 1997, and held discussions with 
the representatives of different public sector enterprises and their trade unions. The names 
of the representatives with whom the Sub-Committee interacted during its visit are placed at 
An~ure. · 
4. The Sub-Committee also heard the Secretary (Industrial Development), Secretary (Heavy 
Industry), Secretary (Textiles) and Additional Secretary (Banking) on the subject in its 
meetinc held on the 28th January, 1997. 
5. The Sub-Committee considered and adopted the draft Report in its meeting held on the 
19~ February, 1997. 
Ahmedabad 
Textile LliliOur Association (TLA): 
6. The 1LA lamented that Ahmedabad, once the Manchester of India, has now turned into 
a graveyard of closed textile mills. Thousands of workers of the closed mills have still not 
been paid their terminal benefits forcing them to lead a miserable life.. They demanded 
immediate payment of legal dues to such workers to enable them to rehabilitate themselves. 
63 

64 
The Association also felt that the stipend for the trainees, training period and other financial 
limits put under the NRF needed suitable revision. 
State Government Omclals : 
7. It was impressed upon that the benefit of NRF should cover displaced ·workers prior to 
1991 also as it presently covers post-1991 workers. There should be a provision to train the 
children of the workers as most of the workers are quite old and relatively less productive. 
Revision of stipend and training period under the NRF was also emphasised. It was felt that 
the procedure under the BIFR was cumbersome and time-consuming and needed suitable 
amendment. Besides, the following points relating to NRF and BIFR were emphasised: 
(i) Strengthening the expertise in BIFR; 
(ii) Abolition of AAIFR due to its redundancy; 
(iii) Decentralisation of BIFR; 
(iv) Converting BIFR into Liquidation Court to avoid going to High Court; 
(v) Making cut-off dates in SICA mandatory; 
(vi) Expediting decision making by BIFR; 
(vii) Expanding the scope of NRF to cover the State level undertakings and the private 
sector units; and 
(viii) Creation of State Renewal Fund in all States by contributions from employees, 
workers, Central and State Gov~mments and financial institutions for providing 
resources for restructuring for the State level public and private enterprises and 
payment of wages and other legal dues to the employees of the closed units. 
8. The representatives of the Gandhi Labour Institute (GLI) informed that after 24th July, 
1991, 48000 workers were displaced. Among these, nearly 33,000 workers are only from 
Ahmedabad. The present scheme of NRF is applicable to this group only. 49,000 workers 
displaced between 1977 and 1990 are unfortunately not eligible for training and 
redeployment under the NRF for which there seems to be no rationale. Recently, 14,000 
workers have been rendered unemployed because of closure of GSTC Mills. Consequently, 
1.11 lakh workers have been rendered jobless in the last decade and a half. Majority of the 
workers displaced from the private sector mills have not been paid their legal dues. The GLI 
has retrained more than 5000 workers in various vocational trades during the last 18 month 
and most of them are engaged in self-employment. 
9. The representatives of GLI also highlighted the following points for a policy decision by 
the Government of India for effective retraining and redeployment scheme: 
(a) The workers who are 50-plus in age often request for nominating their dependents 
for training which should be permitted under the scheme. 
(b) There are 10% of the workers who are suffering from various types of diseases and 
1% of them are dead. There is a necessity to take a dependent from this category 
for training with the . available benefits to ensure their effective deployment to 
support the family. 
I , 
(c) Fmancial assistance to set-up self-employment by the retrained workers in view of 
poor response from the ·banks. 

65 
(d) The ideal training period should be twenty days per month for four months and for 
four hours a day. 
(e) A provision for tool kit immediately after retraining is expected to motivate workers 
to continue the work for which they have taken training. 
(f) 49,000 workers displaced between 1977 and 1990 should be covered the NRF 
scheme. This is the demand from the workers displaced before 1991. 
(g) While private closed mills have not paid legitimate dues of workers rendered 
unemployed, GSTC has paid about Rs. 250 crores to the 14000 employees now 
rendered jobless. Government of India should share this burden on the State 
Exchequer. 
Trade Union representaUves : 
10. The representatives of NTC Mills Technicians and Officers' Staff Union, Gujarat 
Trade Union Munch and Centre of India Trade Unions were of the view that eight units of 
the NTC could be revived if sufficient fund to modernise these units is made available. The 
Operating Agency (IDBI) has also recommended revival of NTC Gujarat and ATIRA 
(Ahmedabad Textile Research Association) appointed by the Central Government has also 
reported that nine out of ten mills could be made viable. They also informed that the 
Rehabilitation Scheme for the employees opting for Voluntary Retirement Scheme (VRS) 
has not been implemented so far. Even the workers have not received their payment for 
November and December, 1996. The representatives were also of the view that the present 
procedure of BIFR takes a long time and needed to be cut short. They also felt that while 
the Management was favoured by the BIFR, the workers were often ignored. 
Mumbai 
NaUonal Bicycle CorporaUon of India Ltd. (N,CIL): 
11. The NBCIL established by Birla Group in 1939 was taken over by Government of 
India in 1974 and was nationalised in 1980, thus making it as a wholly-owned Government 
Company. The Company has two factories-one at Mumbai and the other at Ghaziabad. 
The total manpower in the company is presently 679 with an annual wages bill of Rs. 3.20 
Crores. The Compamy has a total liability of Rs. 133.95 Crores. It has a net loss of 
Rs. 12.70 Crores in 1993-94, Rs. 13.90 Crores in 1994-95 and Rs. 15.69 Crores in 1995-96. 
12. As the Company was chronically sick, it was referred to BIFR in February, 1992. 
The BIFR after hearing the concerned parties finally came to the conclusion in 1993 that the 
NBCIL be wound up and forwarded the relevant papers of its hearings to the High Court of 
Mumbai for further nece'Ssary action. 
13. In February, 1996, the National Bicycle Kamgar Sangh, Ghaziabad, through its 
counsel submitted that workers unions of Ghaziabad and Mumbai units have agreed to come 
together and with the help of a Consultant prepared a draft proposal for revival of the 
company. It was requested that the said proposal should be examined by the Operating 
Agency (OA) and the Government. The AAIFR authorities thereupon directed the OA to 
examine the rehabilitation proposal, hold meetings of the concerned parties and submit a 
Report to AAIFR. The OA reported that the draft proposal submitted by the Unions did 
not inspire much Confidence and submitted that a final view can be taken only after taking 
into account the techno-economic viability study and market potential of the proposed 

66 
motorised tri-cycle. The AAIFR then directed the Central Government to get a techno­
economic study conducted. The report of the study is yet to be submitted. Meanwhile, the 
~ also directed the uni~ns to inform it in writing their final decision regarding 
formation of workers cooperative to take over the management of NBCIL, if rehabilitation 
scheme is evolved. The next date of hearing is fixed for 7th February, 1997. 
14. The Sub-Committee was also informed that some employees of the Company have 
also filed a writ petition in the High Court of Delhi against the BIFR's order of 1993, 
besides hearing of winding petition by the High Court of Mumbai. As the case is pending in 
different Courts and the AAIFR, the Government is not in a position to plan any further 
action unless a revival package is evolved. 
15. Nearly 683 employees have opted for VRS from 1990-91 to 1993-94, with a total 
payment of about Rs. 7.28 Crores. The scheme which was in operation till September, 1993 
was withdrawn by Government of Ind~due to BIFR's. recommendation for winding up of 
the Company during its hearing on 5th OctoQ_er, 1993. Even today 466 VRS applications are 
pending with the Company. H the scheme is e~d to all the employees of the Company 
the majority of them are likely to opt for it. ', 
NatlODIII Bicycle Karmacbarl Sangb : 
16. The National Bicycle Karmachari Sangh in its submission emphasised the following 
pointS:-
(i) The duty of every welfare state is the welfare of its people through providing jobs 
and generation of employment. Even our Constitution lays ample emphasis on this. 
Therefore, Government should make such laws which may help sick units to become 
viable and thereby generate employment. 
(ii) BIFR should be equipped with more legal powers and have the status of a full 
fledged statutory body so that its order could be binding for Promoters and other 
parties. 
(iii) BIFR should have its own team of technical/ financial experts to examine and make 
revival schemes for sick companies rather· than depending upon outside agencies. 
(iv) BIFR should consider all the reasons of sickness, such as outdated plants and 
machinery, corruption in the Management, mismanagement, etc. 
(v) BIFR should be authorised to sanction I recommend loans from NRF for revival of 
sick units. 
(vi) Government of India should protect the interests of workers by means of reviving all 
sick industrial units and guaranteeing their employment as unemployment \l}ay cause 
social unrest in the country. 
(vii) The scheme of retraining of retrenched employees should be stopped which is being 
funded from NRF because there is no point in training a qualified and trained 
worker. Instead, efforts should be to revive the sick company with the help of this 
fund. 
(viii) BIFR should be empowered to direct promoters of the Company to arrange funds 
for revival from its other units which are economically viable. 
\ (ix) BIFR's Chairman should be a Judge of the Supreme Court or any High Court. 
(x) The use of petroleum-propelled vehicles should be discouraged to reduce import of 
petroleum and the use of bicycle should be encouraged. Therefore, NBCIL and 
CCIL, Calcutta should be revived to restrict monopoly of other bicycle 
manufacturers. 
17. The Director, Centre for Workers' Management, Delhi in his submission before the 

67 
Sub-Comittee stated that the NRF has in effect been reduced to an unemployment 
generation fund. The major share of NRF funds have been used to pay for Voluntary 
Retirement Scheme in the public sector without a clear focus on the precise needs of these 
enterprises. This has not oruy resulted in the rendering of workers unemployed but also 
dearth of skilled workers in many enterprises. Very little attention has been paid to the 
schemes for re-training, redeployment and counselling and placement services under the 
National Renewal Grant Fund (NRGF) and the coverage under them is extremely small. 
The primary focus of these schemes is to develop entrepreneurship. Studies, however, 
indicate that across the world less than 1% of workers trained for entrepreneurship were 
actually able to set-up small enterprises while even fewer succeded. 
18. The Sub-Committee was also informed that the NRF has failed to make a 
meaningful contribution to sick industries. This is particularly the case where the sick 
Company was found to be viable by the BIFR and the workers were willing to take-over the 
sick Company through workers' cooperative. The workers take-over however, does not 
fructify since the workers are not able to satisfy the banks and financial institutions of their 
ability to raise finances. Necessary funds for the take-over and the rehabilitation of sick 
Companies through worker's cooperative have not been forthcoming from the NRF. 
19. With regard to the functioning of BIFR, the Sub-Committee was informed that one 
fundamental problem is the role of banks and financial institutions in the revival of sick 
enterprises, their failure to comply with the BIFR directions and the inability of BIFR to 
discipline these institutions. In a majority of cases, where workers' t~ke-overs failed to 
fructify the enterprises, was because of the fact that banks and financial institutions and the 
State Government concerned failed to provide the requisite and permissiple reliefs and 
concessions. Even where workers or their unions have managed to convince the banks and 
financial institutions of their capability to successfully run an enterprise as a worker's 
Cooperative, the State Government or one of its agencies refuses to cooperate. There is 
absolutely no mechanism for ensuring the responsibility of banks and financial institutions 
through the existing mechanism of Sick Industrial Companies (special provisions) Act, 1985 
(SICA). The BIFR is entirely dependent on these institutions in their role as OAs for 
technical advice and support. The decisions of the BIFR are often delayed due to 
lackadaisical approach of OAs. Presently, there are no penalties on delays on the part of 
OAs. 
Rashtriya MUI Mazdoor Sangh : 
20. The Rashtriya Mill Mazdoor Sangh in its presentation submitted that the SICA 
nowhere acknowledges that the Workers' Union is the sole bargaining agent. The workers 
being one of the most important factors of production and the emphasis on workers' 
participation in management in the Constitution, suitable changes in the SICA is required. 
The Sangh felt that the decentralization of the BIFR bench at New Delhi and at places like 
Calcutta, Chennai, Mumbai and Bangalore assigning territorial jurisdiction will help in the 
expeditious disposal of cases. The workers being an integral part of an industrial unit can 
supply vital information regarding mismanagement, wastage, non-use of machinery, etc. and 
should be associated at every stage through their recognized unions to avoid future 
complications. The Sangh also submitted that workers' Legal dues should be paid prior to 
transfer of property or take-over. Presently, their legal dues remain unpaid for a number of 
years, it was also submitted that the BIFR should take serious cognizance of non­
implementation of sanctioned scheme on the part of the company and issue appropriate 
orders to OA. It is usually seen that the sanctioned schemes are not implemented by the 
Company perhaps to make the Company sick again. The Sangh lamented that while huge 

68 
amount has been spent on VRs-no amount is being spent on modernisation or 
restructuring of the sick units. It also demanded adequate representation of workers· on 
BIFR benches. 
Indore 
N•ps Limit•d, N•panagar: 
21. Nepa Limited, a pioneer unit in the newsprint sector, has been in existence for the 
last four decades. Presently, it has an installed capacity of 88,000 tonnes. It has been 
supplying newsprint to the major newspaper establishments spread all over the country. The 
Mill with a manpower of nearly 4500 workers is presently facing the following problems:-
(i) changing scenario due to liberalisation and globalisation; 
(ii) decanalization and duty free direct import of newsprint; 
(iii) changes in the newsprint allocation and distribution policy; 
(iv) the competitive edge of new, modem and economic size newsprint units like 
'Hindustan Newsprint Ltd., Mysore Paper Mills and 1NPL; 
(v) higher cost of production due to very old plant and equipments; 
(vi) indiscipline, lack of team spirit, law and order problem and interference from state 
agencies, etc.; 
(vii) conventional forest raw material from longer leads adds to high cost of production; 
(viii) low pay scales do not attract talented manpower; . 
(ix) heavy financial liability to the tune of Rs. 63.50 crores as on 31.12.96. 
22. The Nepa Mill is closed since August, 1996 on account of serious financial 
constraints due to adverse market conditions for indigenous ·newsprint arising out of 
liberalised import of newsprint under OGL. Because of non-payment of energy bill, Madhya 
Pradesh Electricity Board (MPEB) disconnected power supply and is not inclined to. restore 
power unless commitment to clear the dues in 15 to 20 instalments alongwith current 
monthly bills of Rs. 2.5 crores (approx) is given. In the absence of grid power, the 
production is suspended since August, 1996. 
· 23. The Management of Nepa Limited apprised the Sub-Committee that their approach 
to face the situation is to immediately diversify into cultural varieties of writing and printing 
papers besides newsprint, to become self-sufficient in power and steam requirements, to 
revamp the existing Pulp Mill and to install a Sugar mill of 5000 TCD capacity for obtaining 
150 TPD bagasse pulp, where sugar will be a by-product. The Management suggested that if 
following steps are taken immediately, the loss making unit can be converted into a profit 
making unit after gestation period of only two years: 
(a) Conversion of Government loan of Rs. 32.50 crores into equity; 
(b) Waiver/defennent of TMC and surcharge of Rs. 22.31 crores by MPEB; 
(c) Investment of a capital expenditure of Rs. 37 crores for 15 MW TO set and Boiler; 

69 
(d) Investment of Rs. 38 crores for the revamping of the existing Pulp Mill; 
(e) Installation of a Sugar Mill and Bagasse Pulping unit of Rs. 150 crores. 
Joint Front of NeJHI MiUs Trade Unions: 
24. The Joint Front of Nepa Mills Trade Unions in their submission before the Sub­
Committee highlighted the following issues/points:-
(i) The problems of the workers are not adequately brought to the notice of the Board 
and the Ministry of Industry due to absence of a Director on·the Board representing 
labour. Therefore, a senior level person drawn from the Trade Unions in Madhya 
Pradesh should be nominated on the Board immediately; 
(ii) The wages of Nepa employees have not been revised since 1st January, 1992. The 
wage revision since 1st January, 1997 has also become due. The workers and their 
families are financially broken as the prices are rising continuously. Relief should, 
therefore, be provided to the workers through immediate·payment of their dues from 
1st January, 1992. 
(iii) Anti-dumping duty may be imposed on imported newsprint. 
(iv) Excise duty on craft paper may be reduced for a certain period of time; 
(v) Budegtary support for implementation of Mill development/upgradation scheme of 
Rs. 280 crores. 
(vi) Imposition of custom duty of atleast 40% on import of newsprint and restoration of 
important ratio of 4:1. 
(vii) An opportunity may be given to the workers of Nepa Limited to restart the 
production. An amount of Rs. 15 crores may be given for purchase of essential 
inputs; 
(viii) Financial restructuring by conversion of outstanding Government loans into equity 
and waiving of the accrued interest on loans, including penal interest upto 31.3.96. 
(ix) Waival of accumulated loss of Rs.39 crores upto 31.3.96. 
Calcutta 
Bum Stcndard Company Limited : 
25. The Bum Standard Company Limited (BSCL), a Gove ... 1ment company was formed 
out of the two renowned firms - Bum & Company Ltd. and Indian Standard Wagon 
Company Limited following their nationalisation in April 1975. Since 11.6.1987, Bharat 
Bhari Udyog Nigam Ltd. (BBUNL) has been formed by the Government act as a Holding 
Company for the subsidiary companies in Eastern India including Bum Standard Company 
Ltd. 
26. BSCL is the largest rolling stock manufacturer in the country. It has diversified into 
other non-traditional areas like Tram cars, mining equipment like Roof Supports, Scrapper 
chain conveyors, Mine Car Tripplers, etc. The business activities of the company could 
broadly be classified into (i) Railway Engineering items, (ii) Offshore Division, 
(iii) Refractory Division and (iv) Project Division. 

70 
27. During 1993-94, 1994-95 and 1995-96, the company suffered huge cash loss of Rs. 85 
crores. There was no generation of fund due to poor operational performance. The company 
was compelled to defer payment of statutory liabilities which has accumulated to Rs. 19 crores 
of which Provident Fund and arrear of salary attribute to about Rs. 12 crores. 
28. The total manpower position of all the units as on 30.11.96 is 9813. 2738 employees 
have so far availed themselves of the benefit of VRS. About 250-300 employees are 
expected to be separated through VRS against budget allocation of Rs. 6 crores for the year 
1996-97. 
29. The Industrial Relations during the year 1995-96, by and large, remained cordial. 
However, because of delayed payment of salary and wages, there were sproadic incidents of 
demonstration, strikes and gheraos. 
30. The company has been declared sick by the BIFR in its meeting held on 20.1.1995. 
a revival package is still awaited. 
31. The main reasons for the sickness of the company, according to the Management, 
have been the following:-
( a) Large manpower. 
(b) Imbalances and inadequacies in the warning and high overheads with respect to 
actual performance. 
(c) Total dependence or Railway Authorities for the orders of wagons and its 
components. 
(d) Lack of appropriate framework and facilities for diversification. 
(e) Constraints in streamlining the production due to lack of investment. 
{f) Subsistance of procedural bottle-necks. 
32. The company requires the following assistance from Government:-
(i) Four Refractory units, namely, Raniganj, Gulfurbari, Jabalpur and Niwar are 
chronically sick and economically unviable since inception. These units have 
incurred a cash loss of Rs. 116.78 crores till 31.3.96. The entire non-plan 
assistance received from the Government till 31.3.96 amounting to Rs. 6782 
crores had to be diverted to these sick refractory units. Till the BIFR package is 
worked out and fmally accepted by Government, Government may assist BSCL 
to the extent of the yearly cash loss suffered by it atleast, from 1995-96 onwards. 
" I 
(ii) The Offshore Division of the company has no work since November, 1993. 
Recently, , a repair work of Rs. 7 crores has been received which is under 
executiQI1· Even the order is executed, there will be hardly any margin. 
(iii) A minimum plan assistance of Rs. 8 crores is required for modernisation and 
capital investment to increase production and productivity level in both the 
Engineering Units and Salem works. 
(iv) Government assistance is required for settling the dispute with Me Dennott 
International Inc. amicably and providing necessary funds for payment of dues. 
(v) Government being the promoter is requested to take a positive view on the 
revival package, which is under fmalisation. 

71 
(vi) The pay scales of the officers of the PSUs have been revised by the Government 
w.e.l 1.1.1992. This has not been implement.. in BSCL. This has resulted in 
demoralisation amongst the Officers. In addition, this is causing exodus of 
talents in the company. Government may, therefore, give clearance for the 
implementation of revised pay scales to officers. The company is confident of 
meeting the additional cost involved without any budgetary support in view of 
the improved business scenario of wagon industry. 
(vii) Government assistance is required for reverting to the earlier system of 
allocating wagon orders against rolling stock programme with BICP defined 
prices. The Public Sector should continue to get 60% of total wagon order 
allocated. The system of open tendering introduced in 1995-96 should be 
discontinued.

Tyre Corporation of India Limited: 
34. The Tyre Corporation of India Limited (TCIL) was formed by nationalisation of two 
sick private sector companies Incheck Tyres Limited and National Rubber Manufacturers 
Limited in 1984. TCIL is the only public sector company in Automotive Tyre Industry. 
35. Due to change in the Sick Industrial Companies (special provisions) Act, 1985, 
TCIL had to be referred t9 BlFR in June, 1992 and IDBI was appointed as the Operating 
Agency (OA) to exaniiiie the. techno-economic viability of the company and to prepare a 
scheme for revival and rehaJ>ilitation. The OA was advised by the BIFR to make separate 
scheme for revival of (i) Tyre' l~vision, Kankinara and (ii) IRP division, Tangra considering 
separate profit centre and unit-Wise viability. The final package for revival of the two units 
has since been submitted by the OA in February, 1996. The BIFR approved the scheme for 
implementation. However; Government of India has preferred an appeal against BIFR order 
with AAIFR and stay order has been granted in the matter. 
36. In vie\\ of non-implementation of the BIFR approved package, the company is in 
dire need of working capital for increasing/continuing its production. Consequently, the 
company has started production of other brand Automotive Tyres on conversion basis for 
other private Tyre .Companies to utilise the available idle plant capacity and to reduce the 
cash loss of the unit. The production of own brand tyre is almost negligible. The Production 
at IRP Division (Tangra) remained suspended since August, 1993 due to low pr.·ductivity 
and negative contribution.

Tl'lllll Unions of TCIL: 
40. Various Trade Unions of the TCIL felt that modernisation and expansion 
programmes of TCn., were not taken up in time. Modernisation scheme at an estimated 
capital outlay of Rs. 66.71 crores was drawn up only for Tyre Division at Kankinara in 
1987-88 but no scheme was taken up for IRP Division Tangra, Calcutta. Production in this 
Division has been kept suspended since August, 1993 and the long established reputed 
market of the products of this Division has been allowed to erode. The representatives 
informed the Sub-Committee that Government of India is committed to revive the TCIL and 
referred to an announcement in the Parliament by the Hon 'ble Minister of Industry for 
release of Rs. 4 crores for resumption of production at IRD Division, Tangra. The amount 
is, however, yet to be released. They submitted that the employees of TCIL had undergone 
much sufferings during the last four years, sacrified a lot and agreed to all the conditions 
stipulated by BIFR, including closure of Kalyani Unit. They demanded immediate 
implementation of the BIFR sanctioned scheme in toto as further delay will incur more cash 
losses and will adversely affect the viability of the Scheme. 
Jt~sop & CompGny Limit1d: 
41. The Jessop &. Company Umited (JCL) is a public sector undertaking engaged in 
design, development, manufacture and commissioning of:-
(i) Railway Rolling Stock, EMU Coaches and wagons for Indian Railways. 
(ii) Heavy duty cranes for steel plants and ports. 
(iii) Road construction Equipment i.e. Road Rollers, Vibratory Rollers, Motors 
Grader etc. 
(iv) Mining machineries like Longwall Roof Supports system and accessories for mining 
~ industry. 
(v) Paper machinery. 
42. The Sub-Committee was informed that after take-over by Government of India in 
1973, the company continued its operation as an on-going concern but practically there was 
no .fresh infusion of fund. Due to increase in business activity in the area of Mining and 
Paper Machinery the company was, however, in a position to earn profit for consecutive 
period of three years i.e. from 1987-88 to 1989-90. Due to dearth of orders coupled with 
working capital problem, the company started incurring losses form 1990-91 onwards. Due to 
successive cash loss incurred by the company, the net worth became negative as on 31.3.94 
and the company was· referred to BIFR under SICA in February, 1995. The State 

73 
Bank of India who were appointed as O.A. by the BIFR Bench for formulating the revival 
package, has since submitted its sch!'me to the Bench. As per direction of BIFR, further 
study is being carried out in regard to productivity norm and manpower assessment by a 
reputed Consultant. The following reasons for sickness have been advanced by the 
Management of JCL:-
(a) Jessop was the major supplier of Mining equipment to the coal sector and the 
turnover went up toRs. 67 crores in 1989-90. Due to lack of adequate fund the Coal 
sector was reluctant to place further orders and turnover went down to Rs. 2.S crores 
during 1993-94. Besides, the order for manufacture and supply of MG Coaches was 
closed in 1993-94 due to conversion from Metre Gauge to Broad Gauge by the 
Railways affecting the company's turnover to the tone of Rs. 15 crores. 
(b) Shortage of Working Capital. 
(c) High financial charges because of penal rate of interest in State Bank of India and 
inclusion of interest on Government plan and non-plan loans. 
(d) The current average age of the workmen is about 49 years, which is· very high for a 
heavy engineering company. 
(e) Old and obsolete plant and machinery-adequate upgradationlmodemisation could 
not be undertaken due to paucity of fund. 
43. The combination of the aforesaid reasons resulted in decline in the performance of 
the company over successive years and by the end of 1993-94, the company had an 
accumulated loss of Rs. 156.76 crores. The net worth of the company became negative and it 
was referred to BIFR in February, 1995. 
44. In the last hearing of BIFR Bench held on 25th September, 1996, BIFR Bench, 
Government of India, OA and representatives of employees unanimously agreed that Jessop 
should be revived as per the guidelines suggested by O.A. After implementation of BIFR 
package, which would definitely ease out the working capital problem, the company is 
hopeful that it will be able to tum around and generate profit from the first year of 
operation.

Mining & Allied Machinery Corporation Ud.: 
46. The Mining & Allied Machinery Corporation Limited (MAMC), Durgapur in West 
Bengal, a Government of India Enterprise, was set up in 1965 in technical and financial 
collaboration with the then USSR to manufacture Mining Equipment for underground coal 

74 
mining and other mineral mining. The plant at Durgapur was conceived as an independent 
unit with all infrastructural facilities like Foundries, Forge Shop, Structural Shop, Machine 
Shop, Laboratories, etc. 
47. As part of financial restructuring in 1981-82, the Government gave some relief to 
the company by way of waival of interest on Government loans, conversion of interest into 
non-plan loan and moratorium on repayment of loan upto March, 1985. In 1987. the 
company was allowed benefit of a comprehensive restructuring by writing off plan and non­
plan loan with waival of outstanding interest on Government loans. As a result of the 
restructuring, the company was able to minimise its cash losses to Rs. 2 lakhs during 1986-87 
but high wage bill and slower pace of development of underground mining and limited 
diversification had an adverse effect and the company could not maintain the profitability on 
sustainable basis. 
48. The large capacity of equipment manufacture and an elaborate social infrastructure 
for its more than 6000 employees right at the beginning of the company's operation without 
taking stock of the market for its range of products, made the viability of operation suspect 
right from the inception of the company. The under-utilisation of mining equipment 
manufacturing capacity forced the company to undertake diversification of its product range. 
But diversification efforts could not hear the desired result as not adequate funds were made 
available to reinforce its manufacturing infrastructure. Even today, the company is faced 
with a number of problems. Cash credit account with the SBI remains inoperative for the 
last many years. No Bank guarantee facility is available. The complete erosion in working 
capital of the company has made provision of plant inputs for production, a difficult 
proposition. 
49. In 1989, VRS was introduced in the company to lessen the burden of manpower. 
2129 employees were benefited under this Scheme. With the help of VRS and attrition, the 
manpower of the company has been reduced from 7700 to the present strength of 4320. The 
manpower strength of around 2500 is considered adequate for viability of the company. · 
50. In 1992, the company was referred to the BIFR after erosion of its net worth and 
the BIFR in its order in January, 1995 issued a show cause notice for winding up of the 
company. A stay order on the proceedings of the BIFR has been issued by the Calcutta 
High· Court and the matter rests there.

company to save the workers from hunger and unemployment. They suggested that MAMC 
could be revived if it is merged with Coal India Limited (CIL} as the company was formed 
for ·manufacturing coal equipments required by CIL. They demanded payment of all dues to 
the workers as they are undergoing severe financial difficulties. They also felt that policy of 
the company underwent frequent changes with every change of the top brass of the 
company. This has affected the functioning of the Company adversely.

RECOMMENDATIONS 
58. BIFR and AAIFR under Sick Industries Companies Act (SICA) 1985 and National 
Renewal Fund have been established to deal with the growing industrial sickness in the large 
and medium industries in the public and private organised sectors and for restructuring of 
the industries in India. At present the industrial sickness in India is of massive proportion 
which is eating into the vitals of Indian economy and has made India a high cost and low 
productivity economy making it non-competitive in the world market. 
59. BIFR is supposed to prevent the industrial sickness by restructuring a unit, cure the 
industrial sickness by rehabilitating and/or restructuring the unit and facilitate exit of the 
terminally sick units. The objective of establishing NRF is to provide a social safety net for 
476/RS F-6-A 

76 
the affected workers both in organised public and private sectors and the unorganised sectors. 
due to macro economic adjustment and the Structural Adjustment Programme (Government 
of India, 1991). The objectives of NRF are: 
(i) To provide assistance to cover the cost of retraining and redeployment of 
employees arising as a result of modernisation, technology upgradation, and 
industrial restructuring. 
(ii) To provide funds, where necessary, for compensation of employees affected by 
restructuring or closure of industrial units both in the public and private sectors. 
(iii) To provide funds for Employment Generation Schemes both in the organised and 
unorganised sectors in order to provide a social safety net for labour, (GOI, 1992-
93). Hence the whole endeavour of NRF is to reduce the adverse impact of the 
New Economic Policy, NRF is supposed to ensure transition from regulated 
economy to market economy with minimum adverse impacts on the people. 
60. However, most of the representations made before the Sub-Committee felt that the 
two instruments of BIFR and NRF have not been able to fulfil the objectives for which they 
were established. Therefore, there is urgent need for strengthening these two instruments by 
restructuring them so that they can deal with growing industrial sickness in an effective 
manner. 
61. At the outset, the Sub-Committee felt that BIFR has not been able to serve the 
purpose for which it was created. Meanwhile, after the New Economic Policy of 
Government of India, SICA was amended in 1991 to bring Public Sector Undertakings 
under its purview. The Act was further amended in 1994. The constitution of NRF and 
amendment of SICA to bring Public Sector Undertakings under its purview created a hope 
that the loss making and sick PSUs will now be restructured and will make profit, and these 
goals will be achieved smoothly while providing safety net to the affected workers. 
62. The expenditure of five years indicates that nothing much happened on both these 
fronts. On the contrary, the Sub-Committee during its discussion with various forums got an 
impression that because of reference to BIFR and delayed decision, Banks discontinued the 
Working Capital which these PSUs were getting earlier and crisis became friore severe. Even 
funds from NRF which by September, 1996 covered 94, 332 employees under VRS could not 
bring them out of sickness or losses. At some places complaint was received that enough 
grant from NRF was not given even though employees were willing to accept VRS. The Sub· 
Committee feels that it is worth examination that instead of spreading the coverage of NRF to 
all PSUs, whether it would have been worthwhile to concentrate on few, so that at least these 
few units would have come out of sickness. Gujarat State Textile Corporation has done this 
with the help of Government of Gujarat and had saved the State from continuous annual 
losses of GSTC. 
63. The Sub-Committee carried an impression that in the case of Tyre Corporation of 
India things would not have been that dimcult if proper attention and immediate action were 
taken. 
64. During examination of Secretary (Heavy Industry), he stated that all 49 operating 
enterprises of the department are in direct competition with private sector. Of these, 13 are 
profit making the remaining 34 are making losses, 26 of these loss making public enterprises 
have been referred to BIFR. 
65. He resumed up by stating that a comprehensive package of measures of financial 
476/ RS F-6-B 

77 
restructuring, induction of J.V. partner with technology and marketing strengths, 
rationalisation of manpower through VRS, providing funds as per package by creating a 
'revolving fund to ensure quick and timely infusion of funds in accordance with approved plans 
by BIFR and CCEA will go a long way. 
66. The Sub-Committee strongly feels that much time has passed since reference of 26 public 
enterprises to BIFR and so the above mentioned measures should be immediately taken without 
much loss of time and units should be revived. 
67. The Sub-Committee also got an impression that out of the Units which the Sub· 
Committee visited except a few, all are recoverable according to the Secretary (Heavy Industry). 
The Sub-Committee feels that this task should be immediately completed so that premise given in 
Common Minimum Programme with regard to 'Industrial Sickness' can be fulfilled. 
68. The Second general observation the Sub-Committee wants to make is with regard to 
utilising surplus land to revive the sick units mainly in the case of Textile Mills. 
69. During appearance before the Sub-Committee, Secretary (Textiles) gave some details. 
He mentioned that the revised turnaround package was approved by the Cabinet in 1995 May. 
The total amount which was to be invested in modernisation was Rs. 2005 crores. It was 
expected that 79 mills would be modernised and 18 mills would be merged with viable ones. This 
turnaround package was based on the funds which were to be obtained by sale of surplus land in 
the NTC Mills. The amount that could have been realised was Rs. 2350 crores. 
70. The fact is that we have not been able to sell the lands. The main reason was that most 
of these sale proceeds was to come from city of Mumbai. Almost Rs. 1900 crores was to come 
from sale of surplus land of Mumbai Mills. The Maharashtra Government which was requested 
to give permission for sale of these lands did not give it and, therefore, turnaround package 
could not be implemented because of paucity of funds. 
71. In this connection, it will be interesting to note that the problem of surplus land of 
Textile Mills in Mumbai has dogged successive State Governments at least for two decades. 
Apart from the above mentioned scheme narrated by Secretary (Textiles), even recently 
Maharashtra Government has appointed a study Group to avoid piecemeal and isolated 
development, and Government has asked the Study Group to prepare an integrated 
development plan for the mill lands. This has also not yielded any result. 
72. The Sub-Committee feels that this problem is not attended in proper perspective. Plight 
of 80,000 textile workers in Mumbai and almost equal number in Ahmedabad cannot be ignored 
by any Government. It is a sad commentary on us that the scheme evolved by the Government 
of India is put off, while regeneration of Ahmedabad Scheme sent py Government of Gujarat 
and recommended by Empowered Committee of NRF to Cabinet has remained 
unimplemented. It is estimated that Textile Mills in Mumbai are located on 600 acres of prime 
land in Central Bombay. Same is the situation in Ahmedabad. 
73. Because of such position, now it seems that financial agencies have started approaching 
Courts to dispose of land and the properties of closed textile mills or mills under liquidation. 
The Sub-Committee is afraid that if this is allowed, the valuable properties, machinery and 
prime land will be disposed of at throwaway prices. Recent decision on ARYODA YA ENG. & 
MFG. CO. LTD. (MILL), Ahmedabad by Gujarat High Court is indicative. 
74. There are several representations with regard to the functioning of BIFR under SICA 
1985. 

78 
Slow and costly process of BIFR 
75. Most of the representations have highlighted that process in BIFR is time consuming 
and costly particularly for workers and trade unions. Therefore, there is need to speed up 
process in the BIFR and make it cost effective. The suggestion for this is to decentralize the 
BIFR by creating regional benches in the States where the incidence of industrial sickness is 
very high. (Suggested by RMMS Mumbai, Mr. K.N. Raval, Senior Advocate, Ahmedabad 
and Dr. S.S. Mehta, GLI, Ahmedabad). A decentralised State level BIFR will be able to 
cover , even small scale units which are at present not considered by the BIFR. Government 
should not hesitate to provide more than one bench at Delhi, if so required to expedite the 
cases and process ·thereon. 
76. The number of benches are inadequate to deal with the volume of work. As result of 
this the companies which ar.e defined as potentially sick, namely those whose networth is 
eroded by more than SO percent and not wholly, are by and large not faling the forms and 
there is inadequate monitoring process to ensure preventive work. Unless the number of 
benches are increased, this monitoring can not be done. With the decentralised State level 
BIFRs, this ina~equacy wm also be taken care of. 
77. Benches are mostly manned by retired Government servants. Goswami Committee 
bas pointed out that the members of BIFR are generalists. Most of the members of BIFR 
are retired or seconded officers from the civil services. It is necessary to associate 
professional management consultants or even functioning or retired Public Sector 
Undertakings officials, who h~ve field experience of running an industry to man the benches. 
(Mr. K.N. Raval Advocate, Dr. S.S. Mehta, Gandhi Labour Institute). In fact, Section 4(3) 
of SICA, 1985 states that BIFR members should have special knowledge an professional 
experience of not less than fifteen years in science, technology, economics, banking industry, 
law, labour matters, industrial finance, industrial management, industrial reconstruction, 
administration, investment, accountancy of marketing. 
78. The Sub-Committee also feels that as we have acceptef;l: policy of liberalisation 
with human face there should be also suitable members from' 'labour movement on these 
benches. Not only this, but at present, concerned Labour Union in neither made party nor 
called upon to appear. The Sub-Committee feels that concerned Labour Union should also be 
given opportunity to appear before BIFR. 
79. The Sub-Committee also feels that wherever in any case workers of the unit offer to 
turnaround the unit by forming Workers CooperatiiVe, BIFJ{ should give priority to such 
workers cooperative and give necessary support once such scheme is approved. 
80.· BIFR bas, by and large, appointed senior retired Government officers as its 
nominee directors on the Board of sick companies. This requires to be looked into for 
ensuring that the concerned persons take a more protective role and do not restrict their 
participation in the process of revival merely to attend the Board meetings. They should be 
called upon to continuously visit the plant and give monthly report of their visits so that the 
BIFR is kept informed of the developments in the company on a day-do-day basis. A constant 
monitoring scheme should be provided to have the best possible results. 
81. The Act provides for an operating agency to be appointed by the BIFR from the 
several financial institutions like IDBI, IFCt etc. Some of these institutions are reluctant to 
act as such because of time and other constraints. Sometimes the institutions are not able to 
take a fresh view and continue to give the assessment of the viability of prospects on the 
basis of their past perception and in a given case, even past prejudices. It is, th·erefore, 

79 
suggested that the BIFR should be equipped with an independent agency manned by 
competent persons to assess the viability of various schemes which may 'be suggested. The 
practice of appointing the concerned financial institutions as the operating agency can never 
give a fresh picture or approach which is sometimes absolutely critical for reviving the 
company. This will require amendment of Section 3(1) (i). 
82. BIFR should have power to sanction the scheme without the consent of the concerned 
bank or rmancial institution if BIFR is satisfied that the scheme is workable even though 
institutions are not willing to consent. This will require amendment of Section 18. 
83. The power to give interim directions is restricted to Section 22(a) of the Act which 
permits BIFR to prevent disposition of assets. This is inadequate. BIFR must be given far 
more interim powers pending the inquiry not only to prevent disposition of assets, but also, in 
a given case appoint a provisional receiver in case where the company's operations are shut 
down or a provisional manager or agent to take over the management and run the 
management of the existing unit in any opinion is formed that the unit is being operative in a 
dishonest or fraudulent manner. 
84. The method of functioning by which the BIFR hearings take place like a court 
hearing is not fully appropriate. Such hearing should take place at the final stage but in the 
interaganum there should be greater interaction between the operating agency of the BIFR, as 
suggested earlier, and the company so that the various implications can be gone into in detail. 
85. One of the greatest attractions of taking over a sick unit is setting of losses U'S. 72(a) 
of the Income Tax Act. Earlier, BIFR had power to sanction the schel.e with orders U'S. 
72(a). Now even if the BIFR sanctions the scheme approval under section 72(a) is still 
required. This is time consuming and leads to duplication of efforts and there is no reason 
why the BIFR should not have the power to grant relief U'S.72(a). 
86. One of the reasons of dewy of about two to three years is that in over 50 percent of 
the cases owners have challenged before the AAIFR the closure as recommended by BIFR. 
During the pendency of the appeal to AAIFR, all legal proceedings are suspended U'S. 22 of 
SICA. Therefore, it is recommended that since AAIFR is redundant and leads to unnecessary 
delay and therefore AAIFR should be abolished. Instead, the expertise in the BIFR should be 
increased so that judgement by BIFR is based on better information. 
SICA to Override ULCA for a Viable Area Employment Regeneration Project 
87. SICA 1985 overrides Section 25 M, Section 25 N and Section 25 0 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 for the purpose of restructuring of a unit uls. 18(4) of SICA 1985. In a 
similar way SICA 1985 should override Urban Land Ceiling Act for the purpose of a Viable 
Area Employment Regeneration Scheme for the prompt re-entry for new viable economic 
activities. The best example is that of proposed Ahmedabad Local Economy Rejuvination 
Trust (ALERT) to be created out of the sale of redeveloped land of 15 mills under 
liquidation which would have created over 50,000 jobs. 
SICA to Override . FERA 
88. Under Section 32 of SICA it overrides all Acts except Foreign Exchange Regulation 
ACT (FERA), 1973 and Urban Land (Ceiling & Regulation) Act (ULCRA), 1976. It is 
suggested that SICA should override FERA so that non-resident Indians are encouraged to 
take over the sick companies. 
89. BIFR is empowered to recommend liquidation. According to the present procedure 

80 
these cases have to be referred to the High Court. In the cases which are before BIFR under 
SICA 1985, the Companies Act may be amended to authorize the BIFR to pass the orders for 
winding up as if it were in the liquidation court. (Section 20-1 of SICA). This step will save 
about ~12 months. 
90. There is a legal ambiguity in Section 20 of SICA that requires clarification and 
amendment. Section 20(4) states that (i) notwithstanding contained in Section 20(2) and 20(3), 
BIFR can sell the assets of a company that is recommended to be wound up and (ii) the sale 
proceeds must be forwarded to the concerned High Court for distribution according to Section 
529 A of the Companies Act. The first part implies that Section (I) is winding up. However, if 
this is so, then once the opinion of winding up is forwarded to the High Court, the case goes 
outside SICA and enters the realm of the Companies Act. That being so the courts can insist 
that sales u/s. 20(4) of (SICA) be structured according to the time delaying detail, strictly 
sequential procedures laid down by the Companies Act, the Companies (Court) rules and the 
civil procedure court. Therefore, this ambiguity in Section 20 should be removed so that BIFR 
can sell the assets of a company after deciding to wind it up u1; 20 (1). 
Policy Package for Restructuring Industry and Sector 
91. BIFR has been so far dealing on case to case basis, even though it is very apparent 
that the sickness is in the industry/sector like cotton and jute textile industry as a whole for 
preventing further sickness in the sector, sectorial recommendations are necessary. Therefore, 
BIFR must also apply its mind for restructuring of the sector or a policy package for the 
industry/sector which would be a preventive measure which is one of the objective of the BIFR 
and SICA. 
92. This issue is highlighted before the Sub-Committee on the basis of cases with regard 
to NEPA, NTC and Kamani Tubes. It was brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee that 
NEPA was a thriving industrial unit with positive networth. It has been supplying newsprints 
to the major newspaper establishments spread all over the country who were making huge 
profits. NEPA Mill is closed since August, 1996 on account of serious financial constraints due 
to adverse market conditions for indigenous newsprints arising out of liberalised import of 
newsprint under OGL. Because of the non-payment of energy bill, Madhya Pradesh 
Electricity Board disconnected power supply. 
93. The management suggested that if some policy measures are taken immediately, the 
loss making unit can be converted into a profit making unit in only two years. These are the 
preventive steps which should be taken by BIFR. 
94. The case of NTC is another unique one. If all the units of NTC are aggregated they 
become unviable. However, several individual units under NTC are viable, but since they can 
not be examined individually by the BIFR and therefore they are also turning sick. It is also a 
matter of policy which should be taken up by BIFR. 
95. The third case is of the Kamani Tubes and other workers cooperatives recommended 
by BIFR. Even after the BIFR has recommended that these units must be taken over by the 
workers cooperatives the banks and financial institutions continue to treat them as sick units. 
Therefore, inspite of the sacrifices by the workers, the tum around has not taken place 
because the banks and financial institutions have not played their assigned role to which they 
have agreed in the BIFR. Therefore, BIFR should make it mandatory for the banks and the 
financial institutions that once they have recommended a case for rehabilitation they should 
come forward and help in the turnaround of these units. This is an important issue for policy 
making. 

81 
96. At present, 185 references of the Government companies have been made to BIFR, 
of which 140 were registered. Registration with BIFR means that its functioning becomes 
loose and losses increased and turnaround becomes more and more difficult as time goes off. 
The efforts are slackened by the authorities. The flow of funds particularly working capital 
flow is difficult to get. The purpose of referring public sector units to BIFR, was that they 
should recommend its restructuring to make them viable. This has not happened. Therefore, 
BIFR as a matter of policy should take up and recommend restructuring of the public sector 
units without delay. 
Performance of NRF 
97. The National Renewal fund of Rs. 200 crores was established by Government of 
India in 1991-92 to provide a social safety net for the affected workers, both in public, 
private and unorganised sectors due to macro-economic adjustment and the structural 
adjustment programme. Between 1992-93 and 1996-97, Rs. 3100 crores have been allocated 
to National Renewal Fund. Of this, Rs. 1769.66 crores have been transferred to Public 
Account. Rs. 1505.17 crores have been released for voluntary retirement scheme of 94,332 
workers in the Central Government Public undertakings upto 30th September, 1996. 
98. All the representation made before the Sub-Committee have found the working of 
the National Renewal Fund very unsatisfactory and a large deviation from the original 
objectives. NRF has failed to provide the social safety net for the workers, both in the 
organised and unorganised sectors. NRF has in no way reduced the adverse impact of the 
macro economic adjustment and the structural adjustment programme on the workers. 
99. The biggest failure of the NRF is to provide for the Area Employment Regeneration 
Scheme which was supposed to be the key to success of the objective of NRF. The original 
budget allocation of NRF Rs. 150 crores for Ahmedabad project of area regeneration was 
not transferred because of the bureaucratic rigidity. The Empowered Committee of the NRF 
and even other concerned officials found this scheme viable and yet the funds were not 
released and therefore the benefits of the NRF have not come forth. The failure to 
implement area regeneration project under NRF in a sad commentary on the functioning of 
NRF. The implementation of the area regeneration project and its success would have 
ensured the faith and confidence of the workers in the NRF and the Government. Even now 
if NRF has to make significant impact in the labour market then it is only through proper 
implementation area regeneration schemes in Ahmedabad, Kanpur, Indore, Calcutta, etc. 
Therefore, the Sub-Committee feels and recommends very strongly that NRF must be utilised 
to provide social safety net to workers and it must adhere to its original objectives as spelt out 
in 1991-92. Leaving apart the generation of new jobs, the rehabilitation of workers should be 
done in such a manner that they do not remain without work. 
100. Non-implementation of the area regeneration projects have another implicit 
danger. In places like Ahmedabad, Mumbai, Calcutta, Kanpur and Indore where large 
amount of valuable land resource are lying unutilised if they are not recycled in a planned 
manner, it would result into haphazard growth of the area leading to environmental 
problems. There is also· possibility that this valuable land might be grabbed by the builder 
lobby at throw away prices without any significant benefit to the society. This is the lesson 
which we have in some places in Ahmedabad and in Mumbai. By not taking up area 
regeneration scheme a great opportunity has been lost of developing a suitable policy 
package to recycle the valuable land resource. 
101. Voluntary Retirement Scheme has been able to get rid of 94,332 workers upto 30th 

82 
September, 1996 but it is not necessary that it might have led to becoming those units viable 
and therefore obviously VRS to 94,332 workers does not fulfil any objective or any benefit 
to the society. 
102. In a major sense VRS is a very unproductive scheme which has neither helped the 
country nor the concerned units or even the workers who have taken VRS or the workers 
who have remained behind. 
103. Even the retraining programme undertaken under NRF has remained of a very 
small size looking to the requirement of the affected workers. Even where the programme 
has taken its roots (Ahmedabad, Indore, etc.), the institutions running such programme find 
that the structure is too rigid. 
104. At Ahmedabad, Gandhi Labour Institute which runs the training programme, told 
the Sub-Committee to: 
(a) relax the age limit which is of 35 years 
(b) lower the educational qualification 
(c) lengthen the duration of course from 50 days to 180 days 
(d) increase the stipend 
105. The Sub-Sub-Committee feels that the Ministry should give little more flexibility 
looking to the local environment and largely accept the suggestions. 
106. In initial period of NRF it was also mentioned that the problem of legal dues of the 
affected workers will get priority. But nothing has so far happened in this matter. Workers 
of 46 Textile Mills of Ahmedabad have not received their legal dues of Rs. 222 crores. And 
when these Units are taken into liquidation and assets are disposed of, it is not likely that 
workers will get even sizable portion of their dues. This problem also needs some solution. 
107. Thus most of the trade unions and workers have found the functioning of NRF to 
be very unsatisfactory and non-productive. The following suggestions have come forward 
with regard to the concept of social safety net: 
(i) At present, it seems that NRF has come to a standstill. It seems there is no 
provision for it in 1996-97 and onwards. This implies that the adverse impact of 
macro economic adjustment and structural adjustment programmes on workers has 
come to an end. This is not true. The adverse impact would continue in the new 
economic regime. Therefore, the social safety net would be required. Therefore, 
NRF should be augmented by creating State Renewal Funds. Punjab and 
Rajasthan have already created State Renewal Fund but unfortunately only for 
State level public enterprises. Gujarat has also proposed State Renewal Fund which 
is likely to be funded by the Asian Development Bank. The major objectives of the 
NRF and SRF will be to provide human face to liberalisation. The State Renewal 
Fund but unfortunately only for State level public enterprises. Gujarat has also 
proposed State Renewal Fund can be created through a regular contribution from 
healthy units by the de facto partner of the unit, i.e. the entrepreneur, the 
employees, the financial institutions, Central and State Governments. It can also 
be supported by NRF. Such a fund on a regular basis can provide social safety net 
on a continuous basis. 
(ii) The Centre for Worker's Management, Mumbai has drawn the attention of the 
Sub-Committee that the present scheme for retraining, redeployment and 
counselling is extremely small. It has suggested that the schemes for retraining need 
to be fine-tuned with the specific skills and capabilities of workers concerned as· 

83 
well as in relation with where realistic and feasible opportunities of redeployment 
exist. On the question of development of small business for generating employment, 
this scheme also needs to be extended to the creation of new enterprises through 
workers cooperatives. 
(iii) The Working Committee of INTUC expresses its reservations over the present 
functioning of NRF. It is of the view that NRF should be converted into a statutory 
fund through a new law assuring adequate representation to the working class so 
that funds may flow directly to the NRF instead of the Consolidated Fund of India. 
108. Taking the views expressed before the sub-Committee, the Sub-Committee felt that 
the functioning of NRF has totally neglected the area of providing funds for Employment 
Generation schemes, both in the organised and unorganised sector in order to provide a social 
safety net for labour. VRS existed even before NRF was created. The basic approach should 
be to provide immediate alternative employment to an affected worker who became 
unemployed for no fault of his. At one stage NRF made an effort to evolve some scheme of 
unemployment insurance. One does not know why that effort was abandoned. 
109. The Sub-Committee urges upon the Government to consider the views expressed 
and suggestions made before the Sub-Committee seriously and give NRF the proper status and 
see that aims enumerated at the time of formation of NRF are achieved during the Ninth 
Plan. 
110. Overall representations before the Sub-Committee has been that functioning of the 
BIFR and NRF is not very satisfactory. However, they are very important for the 
imple~ntation of the new economic policy with a human face which, of course, calls for 
restructuring them on the lines suggested above. 
SANAT MEHTA 
Convenor, 
Sub-Committee II on NRF and BIFR.

OF

476/RS F-7-B 
. -.·. 

COMPOSITION OF THE SUB-COMMITTEE 
1. Shri M. Ramananthan-Convenor 
2. Syed Sibtey Razi 
3. Shri T.M. Venkatachalam 
4. Shri Jayant Kumar Malhoutra 
5. Shri Chitturi Ravindra 
6. Shri B.L. Shankar 
7. Shri V. Alagirisamy 
8. Shri Chandra Sekhar 
SUB-COMMITTEE-III ON TRENDS IN INDUSTRIAL OUTPUT 
Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry at its meeting held 
on the 29th October, 1996 constituted five Sub-Committees to examine five different 
subjects pertaining to the Ministries of Industry, Steel and Mines. Sub-Committee-Ill has 
been assigned the task of examining the prevailing trends in industrial output. 
2. In the process of examination, the Sub:Committee heard the views of the Secretary of 
the Ministries of Industry and Power. It also studied the notes and papers received from 
the Planning Commission, the CII, the ASSOCHAM and the FICCI. The Sub-Committee, 
however, could not hear the views of the representatives of the Ministries of 
Communications and Transport as well as the Planning Commission due to paucity of time. 
3. The Sub-Committee began its study with the oral evidence of the Secretary, 
Department of Industrial Policy Promotion, Ministry of Industry and had an interactive 
discussion on the wide ranging interconnected issues reflecting upon the industrial growth 
rate in the current financial year. 
4. The Secretary, at the very outset, admitted that industrial production during the 
period April to August of the current financial year declined to 8.6% as compared to 
12.6% in the corresponding period of the last year. Several sub-sectors of the industry 
namely, electricity, fertilizer, petroleum, textile, steel; etc. were showing signs of 
slowdown. Since, all these sub-sectors belong to the core sector of the economy, any 
negative trend in their growth rate was bound to have a telling impact of the overall 
growth rate. Electricity and mining have, largely, been responsible for bringing down the 
total growth of industrial sector. In case of electricity, it recorded a meagre 2. 5 per cent of 
growth rate in the first quarter of the financial year 1996-97 whereas it was 12.5 per cent 
in the corresponding period of the financial year 1995-96. The performance of Mining 
sector was even worse than the electricity as it could manage to have a nominal growth 
rate of only 1.8 per cent in the same period this year as against the growth rate of 15.4 
per cent last year. Manufacturing sector continued to be in a fluid state. The decline in 
performance of infrastructure industries and major industries can be seen from the 
93 

94 
undermentioned Tables received from the Ministry of Industry alongwith their written 
comments.
