5. The Sub-Committee was further informed that Ministry of Industry tried to identify 
the reasons for this downturn and after an analysis it came to the conclusion that there were 
three major reasons responsible for the present state of growth rate as given below: 
(i) High cost of capital and non-availability of Funds: The cost of capital available 
to the industry is very high. The industry is getting money at an interest rate as 
high 
• 

95 
as 20%. This led to the non-availability of funds thus making the situation in 
money market very tight. 
(ii) Lack of Liquidity in the market: Another factor responsible for downward trend 
has been the liquidity crunch. The squeeze in money supply growth effected by 
RBI resulted in a liquidity crisis. There was acute shortage of money in the 
market because of short supply from RBI. 
(iii) Poor Infrastructure: After liberalisation, the economy registered an increase in 
growth rate. This put extraordinary strain on infrastructure and brought out its 
weaknesses and state of unpreparedness to the fore. Every constituent of the 
infrastructure proved incapable and was found wanting. The performance of 
sectors like electricity generation, petroleum, steel, fertilizers, etc. can be 
quoted as best examples for highlighting the poor state of infrastructure. 
6. Apart from these factors, the analysis of the Ministry identified a couple of other 
reasons such as lack of coordination among Government ministries and departments, 
absence of a coherent picture vis-a-vis liberalisation policy, marginal fall in the savings, delay 
in decision-making as a result of which less foreign direct investment (FDI) proposals were 
received, slump in stock market, increase in PSUs liabilities, etc. 
7. Government were, however, taking all remedial measures to revive the industrial 
output. Accordingly, RBI issued instructions to public sector banks to cut their prime 
lending rates (PLRs). Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) was also reduced by 2% to ease the 
money market. All ministries and departments were taking steps to improve the situation. 
This apart, the Secretary expressed confidence that the growth rate should be in double digit 
though it was too early to say whether it will reach 12 per cent. Since, a number of players 
were involved in the whole process, the growth rate might fall short of the target. 
8. Elaborating further on what needs to be done, the Sub-Committee was informed on 
the issue of foreign investment that an economy, liberalising after years of closed door 
policy, needed free flow of money. But the tight money policy adopted by RBI resulted in 
shortage of capital in the market. This crisis was further compounded by less availability of 
foreign capital. At a time when its neighbouring country, China was attracting more foreign 
investment, India could manage only two billion dollars as foregin investment in 1995-96 as 
against thirty-five billion dollars investment in China. Therefore, India required billions of 
dollars as foreign investment to prop up its economic reforms. He opined that improvement 
in existing facilities and efforts to get more investment for creating new capacity should form 
a part of the strategy to be evolved to get over the crisis. As far as, lack of coordination and 
delay in decision-making was concerned, the Secretary admitted that these two factors were 
also proving to be major hurdles in increase in growth rate. The suggestion for having a 
nodal agency preferably the Department of Industrial Development tor monitoring and 
maintaining the tempo of growth rate was well received by the Secretary. Such a nodal 
agency should also be entrusted with the task of coordinating among all ministries and 
departments in order to ensure speedy disposal of investment proposals and to reach the 
targeted mark of growth rate. 
9. Commenting upon the poor infrastructure, the Sub-Committee was informed that the 
infrastructure was wholly and solely dependent on the public sector or for that matter the 
Government. There has been no private participation or investment on infrastructure as it 
did not yield any profit or financial return. What actually is required to be done is to 
encourage and attract both private as well as foreign investment in this sector. 
10. Similarly, the Secretary emphasized the need to increase savings. As per the data 

96 
available with the Ministry, the savings stood at 24-25% whereas to achieve a growth target 
of 7-8%, there had to be savings to the tune of 28% of GDP. Savings need to register an 
increase of 2%. Government expenditure should be reduced in order to increase the savings. 
At present, larger revenue expenditure has resulted in increased borrowings by Government. 
This continues to be a major impediment in the progress of the economy. Fiscal and revenue 
deficits stood at 5.9 per cent and 3.1 per cent of GDP respectively in 1995-96. Though fiscal 
deficit declined from 8.3 per cent of GDP in 1990-91 to 5.9 per cent in 1995-96, the size of 
the deficit continues to be high. What is more worrying is the high revenue deficit which has 
fallen only by 0.4 percentage point in the last five years, from 3.5 per cent of GDP in 
1990-91 to 3.1 per cent in 1995-96. As a result of high revenue deficit, Government has to 
borrow from the market to meet current expenditures. 
11. The Secretary, in all, was for inprovement of the existing infrastructure, more inflow 
of foreign direct investment, cut in the interest to ease the liquidity crisis, proper 
coordination among various ministries and departments of the Government, reduction in 
Government expenditure and increase in savings, evolution of a coherent industrial policy 
especially vis-a-vis private and foreign investment and so on and so forth. 
12. The Sub-Committee next heard the Secretary, Ministry of Power. Power being a 
major constituent of the infrastructure, has been the most decisive factor in bringing down 
the growth rate of industrial output. First six to seven months of the current financial year 
witnessed drastic fall in the electricity generation. This fall, in turn, had resulted in a 
slowdown in industrial output. The Secretary, Ministry of Power, in his submission informed 
the Sub-Committee that the country had 86,000 MW of installed capacity, out of which, 
about 35,000 MW was hydro and the remaining was completely thermal. The average 
shortage at the national level was 10%. The growth rate in demand is almost about eight per 
cent per annum, whereas the energy generated is about seven per cent per annum. So, there 
is a gap of one per cent year after year. This one per cent gap in demand and supply is the 
reason for power shortage all over the country. The availability of power is dependent on 
rains and availability of coal. This year, the hydro generation has dropped by almost 8% 
because of scanty rains in the catchment areas of almost all the regions of the country. 
Similarly, erratic supply of coal to the thermal power stations led to steep decline in 
electricity generation. The requirement of coal stood at 195 million tonnes last year. It fell 
short by 10 millions tonnes in 1995-96. Non-availability of coal has uniformly been affected 
the performance of power stations. In the current year, the power stations require around 
215 million tonnes of coal but the Ministry of Coal have already made it clear that they 
would not be able to supply more than 195 million tonnes of coal. So, there is a gap of 20 
million tonnes. Same is the case with gas-based units. Due to non-availability of gas, 
production never goes beyond 50---60%. As a result, the gap between demand and supply is 
widening. The demand for power is increasing every year by 8%. But the target is not being 
achieved. The reason why the target is not being achieved is the lack of investment. Private 
participation as well as foreign investments have somehow not been able to materialise. In 
this way, the power shortage continues to persist. 
13. The Sub-Committee was further informed that the Ministry of Power had prepared a 
National Action Plan to improve the condition of the power sector. Modernisation and 
increase in the efficiency of existing power plants, priority to hydro projects, autonomy to 
the State Electricity Boards and improvement in their management, rationalisation of tariffs, 
participation of private sector in distribution and setting up of central as well as State 
Electricity Regulatory Commissions are some of the highlights of this plan. A copy of the 
action plan is placed as Annexure-H. 
14. The Sub-Committee also had the opportunity to examine the background papers and 
notes received from the Planning Commission and various Chambers of Commerce and 
Industry. Close scrutiny of these papers proved helpful for the members to further grasp the 
gravity of the problem. All these papers, in a nut shell, pointed out that the downturn in 
industrial growth has been mainly due to (i) infrastructure bottlenecks, (ii) liquidity crunch, 

97 
(iii) high interest rates; (iv) slump in demand, (v) nsmg revenue expenditure and 
(vi) slowdown in the production activity of major sub-sectors of the industry. 
CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
1. The most important factor which had led to slow growth in the industrial production 
is the lack of infrastructure in the country. No parti'YUlar attention has been given during the 
last so many years to develop this sector and to meet the increasing demand of the 
industries. Our ports are congested which takes days and weeks for the ships to get a berth 
at the ports. Similarly, there is a shortage of wagons. The roads cannot cope with the 
increasing demand of the the transport sector and other basic infrastructures are also 
lacking. In fact, the Finance Minister while replying to budget debate last year in the Lok 
Sabha was candid enough to admit that Indian infrastructure was trailing at its seams. He 
had added that as regards electricity, the shortage was around 20%. As regards roads, the 
Finance Minister had this to say: 
" ..... we have only about 74,000 kilometres of-what is called National Highway. But in 
many places the facetious statement is that, it is only a National Highway. It is not a 
National Highway. It ought to be world class roads. 
A gentleman from the Ford Motor Company who have set up a plant now in 
Maharashtra and who are setting up another plant in Tamil Nadu, told me that it takes five 
to nine days for a truck to go from Mumbai ·to a place in Tamil Nadu. If we have an 
efficient and cost effective system and if it takes nine days for goods to move from Mumbai 
to Madras, how is it possible? It should take not more than two days if we have world class 
roads there. That is why we have identified the infrastructure challenge as the second 
challenge after the fiscal challenge."* 
It is a matter of grave concern for the country that in all these years of planned 
economy, the infrastructure had totally been neglected. It speaks volumes of the lack of 
foresightedness and casts a serious reflection on the functioning of the Planning Commission 
which overseas and coordinates the planned development in the country. 
The Sub-Committee, therefore, suggests that action to augment the infrastructure in the 
country should be developed on a war footing and cooperation of all concerned should be 
secured. The policy for private investments should not only be crystal clear but also 
transparent. 
2. Industry has been virtually cash-strapped over the last few months. Two most 
discernible reasons for this are the adoption of tight money policy by RBI and the less flow 
of foreign capital in terms of direct investment. As far as, money policy of RBI is 
concerned, the situation was eased out to some extent by the reduction of CRR by 2%. This 
step made available around Rs. 8,000 crore to the market. With regard to the foreign direct 
investment, the indications have not been found to be as much encouraging as they were 
when the economic reforms were first introduced in India. It has been observed that 
whatever foreign capital has been invested or is being invested, is limited only to consumer 
durables or luxury items, whereas the basic infrastructural areas have been left untouched. 
Understandably, the attraction of foreign investors seems to be to make megabucks with 
scant regard to the creation of infrastructural facilities. The intention of this Sub-Committee, 
is not at all, to concetrate on infrastructure alone, the investment should be evenly dispersed 
so that overall growth could be achieved. It should never be lopsided in any manner. 
Therefore, this Sub-Committee is of the view that steps should be taken to attract foreign 
investment not just for one sector of the economy but for all the fields of economic activities, 
Priority, defmitely, be accorded to hitherto untouched or haltbeartedly touched sectors of the 
economy. 
*Finance Minister while replying to Budge~ deba~ In tbe Lok Sabha on 6.9.1996. 

98 
3. Another problem that dogged the industry is the high rate of interest on the funds 
provided by banks and other financial institutions. Though efforts were made on the part of 
RBI to control the situation, the timing of its intervention was little too late. Almost all the 
public sector banks have lowered their prime lending rates (PLRs) following the guidelines 
issued by RBI. Similarly, the reduction in Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) provided succour to 
the market which was bearing the brunt of liquidity crunch. In this regard, the Sub­
Committee has come to the conclusion that non-availability of easy money to the industry has 
assumed the proportions of a perennial problem. Here the role of RBI becomes significant. 
It being an apex body should take initiative to see that lending norms are further Iiberalised 
so that dying spirits of the market could be revived. Moreover, it should maintain mo:-e 
watchful control over the way the money market moves. 
4. Perhaps, the most crucial factor that has adversely affected the growth rate of 
industrial production, is the actual shortage of power, the survival of modem hi-tech 
industries depend mainly on the availability of power. If the availability of electricity is 
disturbed, the industrial activity gets equally affected within the possibility of its coming to a 
grinding halt within the stroke of a second. Apparently, little over sixty per cent of 
electricity is generated by thermal plants which have to depend on the supply of coal. 
A little delay, in the supply of coal, leads to the closure of generation units. On the other 
hand, in spite of having abundance of water resources, the contribution of hydel electricity 
plants is a mere thirty five per cent. In other words, this is a classic example of improper 
and poor management of natural resources. Although the Ministry of Power has chalked out 
a national action plan in order to improve the power situation, what remains to be seen is as 
to how successful this programme would be. In the opinion of this Sub-Committee, the entire 
power sector needs complete overhauling and restructuring. The present spate of unabated 
power crisis is swaping the entire country. It should be checked and this problem needs to be 
addressed with the help of both short-term as well as long-term measures. Foreign investment 
in the power sector should be encouraged with the emphasis being on hyde) electricity. As a 
short-term measure the focus should be on the modernisation of the existing plants so that 
their efficiency could be enhanced. The transmission and distribution losses of electricity, the 
present rate of which is too high all over the country, also needs to be checked with a strong 
hand. The various State Electricity Boards also need to be revamped with a view to instil 
efficiency in their operation. 
5. Revenue expenditure of the Government has been steadily rising. This compels the 
Government to resort to borrowings. Borrowings in. tum result in diversion of funds away 
from the corporate sector and ultimately leading to the shortage of funds in the market. This 
shortage pushes the interest rates up and creates liquidity crunch. All this is caused by the 
mounting fiscal deficit owing to which the Government has to tum to the market to meet its 
current expenditure. Therefore the need of the hour is to curb the wastefull expenditure and 
to bring the level of fiScal deficit down to manageable limits. The Sub-Committee strongly 
feels that Government should take immediate steps to curb the revenue expenditure with a 
view to increase the savings. The Sub-Committee recommends that all the mega and major 
Government projects should be completed within the given time frame. 
6. Another issue closely linked with the Government expenditure is the domestic 
savings. At present, the domestic savings stand at 24-25% of the GDP. To achieve a growth 
target of 7-8% the percentage of savings should go up. It should in the opinion of the Sub­
Committee, at least regis!..::r an increase of 2%. Requisite incentives need to be offered to 
increase the domestic savings. In this way, the issues of government expenditure, fiscal deficit 
and savings are interconnected and should be dealt with a common frame of mind. 

99 
7. There has been much talk about the lack of coordination among various ministries 
and departments of the government. Lack of coordination hampers the upward movement of 
growth rate as the projects and proposals get stuck at different stages for one reason or the 
other. Although, the policy of single window clearance was given too much media hype, yet 
many a project are delayed because of bureaucratic redtapism and indifference. The need is 
to create an atmosphere of change where each and every agency of the government is well­
acquainted with its work and putting in its best effort to achieve the target. The Department 
of Industrial Development, may be the choice for this job. Such a nodal agency will not only 
do the coordination work but will also be instrumental in putting forward a coherent picture 
of the Indi;tn ..... economic reforms to the world business community. 
Further, the Sub-Committee notes with discontent the statement of the Secretary. IPP 
made before the Sub-Committee that there is no proper coordination in the process of decision 
making in the various departments and industries. This, in the opinion of the Sub-Committee 
leads to delay in the process of decision making. The Sub-Committee fails to understand the 
reasons for lack of coordination in the process of decision making among the various 
Ministries I Departments when there exists & Planning Commission as well as the Committee 
of Secretaries where the differences, if any, among the MinistriesDepartments can be sorted 
out conveniently. The Sub-Committee recommends that efforts should be made to increase 
coordination in the process of decision making so that the projects are completed within the 
prescribed time frame and cost over-runs are also avoided. 
8. The Sub-Committee is constrained to note that the Planning Commission have not 
yet been able to make known details of the 9th Plan which is to be implemented in a little 
over a months time. This, in the opinion of the Sub-Committee prevents the public from 
going in for an advance planning which is very essential for maintaining a steady growth rate. 
The Planning Commission had also not come out with a mid-term review. At present we are 
only 3 years ahead of entering into the year 2001 and the Sub-Committee is apprehensive that 
if the present state of planning continues, India would enter into the next millennium on a 
bullock cart. 
9. The Sub-Committee is of the view that in order to reduce the Government expenditure 
a time has now come that Government should come out with unambiguous policies with 
regard to central public sector undertakings. The country can no longer afford to carry on 
with dilly-dally policies with regard to this important sector of the economy. The Government 
must take hard decision wherever necessary. Time has now come when ideological issues 
should take a back seat and investment decisions should be made only on the basis of pure 
economic considerations. A decision in respect of such of those public sector undertakings 
which have over the year been incurring losses year after year and their equity has become 
zero, must be taken without any futher delay. In respect of such of the undertakings it is not 
necessary for the Government to give non-plan support merely to protect the employment. 
Alternative schemes ought to be thought of such industrial units in a time bound programme. 
The Sub-Committee notes with deep regret in respect of large number of such loss making 
units most of which are now before the BIFR, the Government has not come out with any 
clear cut policy. These units are a drag on the country's economy.

ANNEXURE-II 
Common Minimum 
National Action Plan 
for Power 
Ministry of Power 
Government of India 
December, 1996 

' \ 

COMMON MINIMUM NATIONAL ACfiON PLAN FOR POWER 
The Chief Ministers met on 16th October and 3rd December, 1996 to discuss and 
deliberate upon the issues pertaining to the power sector. 
Recognized that the . gap between demand and supply of power is widening. 
Acknowledged that the financial position of State Electricity Boards is fast 
deteriorating and the future development of the power sector cannot be: sustained without 
viable State Electricity Boards and improvement of operational performance of State 
Electricity Boards. " 
Agreed that reforms and restructuring of State Electricity Boards are urgent and must 
be carried out in definite time frame. 
Identified creation of Regulatory Commissions as a step in this direction. 
Noted that the requirements of the future expansion and improvement of power sector 
cannot be fully achieved through public resources alone and it is essential to encourage 
private sector participation in generation, transmission and distribution. 
Observed that the changing scenario in the power sector calls for further delegation of 
powers and simplification of procedures. 
A national consensus evolved for improving the performance of the power sector in a 
time bound manner and the following was adopted. 
I. National Energy Policy 
* The Government would soon finalise a National Energy Policy. 
II. State Electricity Regulatory Commission 
* Each State/Union Territory shall set up an independent State Electricity 
Reg~latory Commission (SERC). 
• To set up SERCs, Central Government will amend Indian Electricity Act, 1910 and 
Electricity (Supply) Act, 1948. 
* To start with such SERCs will undertake only tariff fixation. 
* Licencing, planning and other related functions could also be delegated to SERCs as 
and when· each State Government notifies it. 
107 
476/RS F---8-A 

108 
* Appeals against orders of SERCs will be to respective High Courts unless any State 
Government specificully prefers such appeals being made to the Central Electricity 
Regulatory Commission. 
Ill. Central Electricity Regulatory Commission 
• Union Government will set up a eentral Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC). 
• CERC will set the bulk tariffs for all Central generating and transmission utilities. 
• Licencing, planning and other related functions could also be delegated to CERC as 
and when the Central Government notifies it. 
• All issues concerning inter-State now and exchange of power shall also be decided by 
the CERC. 
• To enable setting up of CERC, Central Government will amend Indian Electricity 
Act, 1910 and Electricity (Supply) Act, 1948. 
IV. Rationalisation of Retail Tariffs 
• Determination of retail tariffs, including wheeling charges etc., will be decided by 
SERCs which will ensure a minimum overall 3% rate of return to each utility with 
immediate effect. 
\ 
• Cross-subsidization between categories of consumers may be allowed by SERCs. No 
sector shall, however, pay less than 50% of the average cost of supply (cost of 
generation plus transmission and distribution). Tariffs for agricultural sector will not 
be less than fifty paise per Kwh to be brought to 50% of the average cost in not more 
than three years. 
• Recommendations of SERCs are mandatory. If any deviations from tariffs 
recommended by it are made by a State/UT Government, it will have to provide for 
the financial implications of such deviations explicitly in the State budget. 
• Fuel Adjustment Charges (FCA) would be automatically incorporated in the tariff. 
• There shall be a package- of incentives and disincentives to encourage and facilitate 
the implementation of tariff rationalisation by the States. 
476/RS F-8-B 

109 
V. Private Sector Participation in Distribution 
* State Governments agree to a gradual programme of private sector participation in 
distribution of electricity. The process of private participation shall be initially in one or 
two viable geographical areas covering both urb<Jn and rural areas in a State and the 
State may extend this to other parts of the State gradually. 
VI. Role of Central Agencies 
* The Central Government would make a comprehensive review of the role of Central 
Electricity Authority (CEA). Techno-economic approval of competitively bid power 
projects will be simplified and CEA shall not be concerned with capital cost, tariff and 
other commercial aspects of the project. Powers regarding approval of projects shall 
stand delegated to the States in respect of thermal power stations upto 250 MW. 
However, in respect of thermal projects beyond-250 MW capacity and other schemes, 
CEA's appraisal will continue in respect of planning and other related matters, such as to 
promote best optimal development of the river and its tributaries for power generation 
including proper hydro-thermal coordination; the suitability of location and size of 
thermal power stations; fuel linkages; adequacy of power evacuation facilities covering 
inter-state and intra-state and the transmission schemes form and integral part of the 
National Power Grid for maximization of benefits for the country as a whole. Subject to 
such technical cle,afances which shall be accorded within two months State Governments 
will have powers tb accord approval for power projects. 
* The role of FIPB will be minimised by putting as many projects on· the automatic 
clearance route as feasible. 
* Government of lndia will issue transparent guidelines and delegate more powers for 
environmental clearance to State agencies. State agencies should equip themselves with 
requisite physical and technical expertise. 
* Government of India will delegate more powers to the States for issue of forest 
clearances. Statutory amendments will be made in the relevant Central statutes. 
* Ministry of Environment and Forests have proposed the following delegation to the 
States for environment clearance: 
(i) All co-generation plants and captive power plants up to 250 MW. 
(ii) Coal based plants upto 500 MW using fluidized bed technology subject to sensitive 
areas restrictions. 
(iii) Power staHons upto 250 MW on conventional technology. 
(iv) Gas/Naphtha based station upto 500 MW. 
vn. Autonomy to the State Electricity Boards 
* States will allow maximum possible autonomy to the State Electricity Boards. The State 
Electricity Boards will be restructured and corporatised and run on commercial basis. 
VIII. Improvements in the Management Practices of State Electricity Boards 
* State Electricity Boards will professionalize their technical inventory manpower and 
project management practices. 

110 
IX. Improvement of Physical Parameters 
* Government of India will carry out necessary amendiiMMI in the relevant Acts/Rule to 
allow private participation in transmission. 
* State Governments will provide higher allocation for early oompletion of public sector 
projects. 
* Renovation and modernization of existing power plots allall be done in a time bound 
manner. PFC and other fmancial institutions will give hiahk priority for funding of R & 
M schemes. Clearance to R & M projects will fully be del-sated to the States and no 
clearance will be required from CEA. 
* PLF of those thermal power stations having less tho 40% PLF at present would be 
increased by 3% annually, by 2% in case of those plants vmh PLF between 40 and 60% 
and by 1% for those plants with PLF over 60%. ·The overall PLF in the State sector in 
the country must come up to a minimum of 65% and tile national average to 70% by 
2002 A.D. 
* Compulsory metering at substations and on all major feeders would be introduced. 
Compulsory metering of all ~ew electricity connections as also of connections to 
agriculture sector exceeding 10 HP will be undertaken and completed in two years. All 
electric supplies would be metered by 2002 A.D. · 
* Compulsory annual energy audit of large consumers i.e., 100 KV A and above would be 
undertaken. 
* Time of the day metering would be introduced for big power consumers for better load 
management. 
X. Co-generation/Captive power plants 
* State Governments will encourage Co-generation/Captive power plants. To facilitate 
evacuation of power from these plants to the grids, States shall formulate clear and 
transparent policies for purchase of power and wheeliD& charges which provide fair 
returns to the Co-generation/Captive power plant owners. Captive power plants could 
also sell power to a group of industries as well as other categories of consumers in the 
said industrial zone or area. Wheeling of power from captive power plants to consumers 
located at a distance or through displacement basis shall be encouraged and the States 
will issue clear and transparent long term policies in this regard. 
XI. Advance Action and ffigh Priority for Hydro Projeds ---. 
* A national policy on hydro power development will be evolved by the Central 
Government which, inter-alia, would include development of mega hydro projects, both 
in the public sector and the private sector, at locations with substantial hydro potential, 
along with concomitant transmission facilities for evacuation of power to other Regions/ 
States. States shall prepare a shelf of fully cleared hydro projects for implementation on 
high priority. Special efforts will be made to promote hydro-electric projects in Himachal 
Pradesh and Jammu & Kashmir. 
~II. Due Emphasis for Investment in North-Eastern Region 
* Since there are geographical constraints in the North-Eastern Region, the Government 
shall constantly review the public and private investments being made in that region so 
that these States get equitable shares in the investments in the power sector. 

111 
xm. Alloeation of liquid ,.... 
• Government shall finalise Ute linkages of allocation of liquid fuels for power plants in 
consultation with State Governments soon. 
XIV. Mega power projetM M pit lleads 
• Development of mega p<rir.- projects at mine pitheads, both in the public sector and the 
private sector, with ~ facilities for evacuation of power to other Regions/ 
States would be encouraa-cf. 
SeUJoa up of washeries 
• Coal India Limited and its subsidiaries shall put up washeries at pitheads, wherever 
necessary. In case CIL c:aanot set up the washery, private sector would be permitted to 
set up such washerie1 at pitheads. In either case, supply, washing and transportation of 
coal shall be on the balil of legally enforceable commercial contracts.

STUDY REPORT(S) 
OF 
SUB-COMMITTEE-IV

The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry in its meeting 
held on the 29th October, 1996 constituted five Sub-Committees on different subjects. The 
Chairman of the Com~ittec authorised the Convenor of each Sub-Committee to chalk out 
the programme of work including study visits of each Sub-Committee. 
I, Ram Deo Bhandari was nominated the Convenor of the Sub-Committee-IV on 
'Mining Sector'. The Sub-Committee was assigned the job of studying the problems in the 
way of mining organisations, the problems of mine workers, development of mining activities 
etc. in various States of the country. For this purpose the Sub-Collllllittee undertook two 
study visits to Calcutta, Bhubaneshwar, Tatanagar, Vdaipur, Mangalore and Bangalore 
during the months of December, 1996 and January, 1997. The study-reports in respect of the 
visits/discussions with various organisations have been given in detail on the following pages.

Convenor,

Discussion with Management of Hindustan Copper Limited, Calcutta 
Sub-Committee IV of Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry 
on Mining Sector met at 3.00 P.M. on 26.12.96 in the Conference Hall of Hotel Hindustan

PRESENT

Shri Om Parkash, Deputy Secretary 
Representatives of Hindustan Copper Ltd. Calcutta 
1. Shri K.N. Ghosh, C.M.D. 
2. Shri I. Sen, Director (P) 
3. Shri M. Basu, E.D. (M.P.&S) 
4. Shri S.N. Chakraborty Adv. (F) 
5. Shri T.P. Girish, G.M. (P&MS) 
6. Shri S.N. Singh, G.M. (Peri & Ad) 
7. Shri M.N. Samaddar, D.G.M. (F) 
Presentative of Ministry of Mines, Central Government 
Shri M.E. Madhu Sudan, Industrial Advisor 
The Convenor introd.uced the Members and officers of the Sub-Committee and explained 
in brief the purpose of the visit of the Sub-Committee. 
Shri K.N. Ghosh, C.M.D. of H.C.L. introduced his team of officers and welcomed the 
Committee and informed that presently H.C.L. is the sole producer of primary copper in the 
country with designed capacity to produce 47,500 tonnes of refined primary copper from 
indigenous sources. The main operating units are Khetri Copper Complex at · Khetri, 
Rajasthan; Indian Copper Complex at Ghatsila, Bihar, Malanjkhand Copper Project, 
Madhya Pradesh and Taloja Copper Project, Maharashtra. 
He also informed the Sub-Committee that India is not fortunate in respect of Copper' 
deposits. Inspite of intensive geophysical and geological survey and prospecting and 
exploration activities, no major new deposits could be discovered. The grade ore in India is 
very poor i.e. around 1% as against 2.5 to 3.5% copper content in other parts of the world. 
The geometry of ore body in India is very much unfavourable for large scale mechanisation. 
The by-product content in Indian ore is very low i.e. 4-5% as against 20-40% in other 
116 

117 
parts of the world. The combined effect of all these has made cost of rnmmg and 
beneficiation in terms of copper metal very costly but the smelting and refining cost of 
HCL's plants are comparable to the international cost of production. Copper being a 
strategic metal required for use in Defence, Telecommunication, Space Research, Railways 
and power sector the Government had evolved earlier a number of protective measures like 
levy of varied higher customs duty; canalising the,import of copper and controlling the prices 
of copper through administered pricing system which was much higher than landed cost of 
imported copper. 
However with the liberalisation of economy, canalisation of copper and the system of 
administered prices were done away with from March, 1992 and the import duty on copper 
was reduced to 45% ad valorem on C.I.F. With reduction in customs duty coupled with 
unprecedented fall in LME price of copper. HCL has suffered a huge loss of 69.55 crore 
during 1993-94. But during 1994-95 the scenario changed with increasing customs duty from 
45% to 50% as well as increasing the LME prices and thus the company made a. profit of 
72.26 crore during 1994-95 and a record profit of Rs. 75.84 crores in 1995-96 as the company 
has achieved all time high production of 37,280 tonnes of wire rod at Taloja. 
But at present the customs duty on import of copper has been brought down from 35% 
ad valorem for cathode and wire bar and 40% for wire to a flat rate of 30% for all the 
items. Moreover, the petroleum products have hiked their prices. All these resulted in a loss 
of Rs. 73.22 crore during the first half of the year 1996-97. 
On the efforts of H.C.L. the Sub-Committee was informed that a number of 
uneconomic mines like Lapso Kyanite mine, Dariba mine and operations like Brass Rolling 
mill operation etc. has been closed. H.C.L. is also taking action to close down Mosaboni 
mine in phases where the cost of production is very uneconomical due to the depth 
(1230 m). 
On a specific question by a Member the Sub-Committee was informed that reasons for 
less production than the targets was power shortage and the shut out period of the smelter, 
which for 2 to 21/ 2 months in a year does not work. However the company is considering 
some alternative arrangements. In reply to another question it was informed that now no 
licence is required for import of copper and any body can import it. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that due to losses the staff was reduced by 
4012 through Voluntary Retirement Scheme and that if no financial assistance is given the 
company will be heading towards closure. The Rakha mines can be further developed if 
funds are made available. 
There was no ecological problem in and around the Company's units. The Company 
was fully aware about the social and welfare activities of its employees. 
Once the expansion of highly profitable Project of Khetri is commissioned the Company 
will be in much better position to face the fluctuations in LME. For the intervening period 
of 2-3 years the company has requested the Government for some temporary relief and 
support on immediate basis to combat the situation arising out of crash LME price so that 
the company could take care of wages of the employees and its growth is not halted. The 
capital investment for green field copper smelt~r and refinery complex of 100,000 TPA 
capacity is around Rs. 1370 crore whereas the same for expansion of capacity of KCC 
smelter and refinery to 100,000 TPA is expected to be only Rs. 528.65 crore. 
The Sub-Committee visited the Ghatsila Copper Complex and was taken inside the 
copper mine. The Sub-Committee received a number of representations from the workers. 
Wherein they demanded that those working for a number of years as contract labour should 
be regularised and their social and welfare needs be attended to. They also demanded that 
their service conditions be improved. It was pleaded that the lease for Sidheshwar-Chapri 

118 
may be renewed in favour of H.C.L./I.C.C. in order to facilitate expansion and at the same 
time the proposed closure of Mosabani and Badia Mines of HCI./ICCIMSB group of mines 
be stopped to save about a lakh of employees and their families. The closure of these mines 
will have a wide ranging effect. 
Indian Copper Complex, Ghatsila has Mosabani Copper mine with 6923 hectare valid 
for 20 years from 16.6.1984. Presently only 187 hectare has been leased but permitted to 
mine from only 37 hectares. The State Govt. of Bihar should grant the permission for lease. 
There is a vast scope of expansion of this mine. It produced 0.02% Uranium worth 
Rs. 12 crores. The refinery for silver/gold requires strict vigil. 
Rakha mine has the area of 785.09 hectare, valid for 20 years w.e.f. 29.8.71. But the 
State Govt. intimated that renewal can be considered only for 23 hectares. Kendadih has an 
area of 1179.60 hectare valid for 20 years w.e.f. 3.6.73. The State Govt. intimated that 
renewal can be considered only for 35.50 hectare but later on the State Govt. agreed for 
669.97 hectare. If the State Govt. does not renew the lease there is a chance of lakhs of 
employees being rendered jobless. The authorities propose to increase the ore from 
900 T.P.D. to 2500 T.P.D. so that people may get employment. The capacity of smelter 
plant is proposed to be increased from 16500 T.P.A. to 31000 T.P.A. 
Shri B.P. Gupta a representative of Bihar State Govt. informed the Sub-Committee that 
H.C.L. extracts 75% of copper from Mosabani mine. They have not put up any extensive 
proposal to ensure the State Govt. that they need whole of the area. The State Govt. would 
never intend that the employees may be rendered jobless or the plant may be closed. The 
question of renewal of the lease is still under the consideration of State Mining Deptt. He 
told the Sub-Committee that in reality the required areas can be renewed but they 
themselves are surrendering some of the mining leases. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that-
(i) Sufficient . financial assistance may be provided to HCL expeditiously as per 
package Ill. 
(ii) Customs duty on imported copper should be increased again to protect the 
indigenous copper mining companies. 
(iii) If the organisation under the Government control fails to run the company 
profitably, foreign companies should not be allowed to take over the production of 
copper etc. and indigenous companies should be given a chance as far as possible 
to upkeep the interests of the organisation. The indigenous copper mining 
companies should be encouraged and joint ventures according to FIPB policies 
should be allowed where equity participation is mainly in the fields of 
infrastructure and equipment. 
(iv) The Mosabani and Badia mines may not be allowed to close down. 
(v) Western mines should be explored on a large scale. 
(vi) The Welfare and service conditions of the employees may be improved and all 
employees may be regularised. The provisions for the welfare activities of 
employees and their families should be sufficiently increased. 
(vii) The hygienic conditions as well as other medical facilities at H.C.L's hospital at 
Mosabani should be thoroughly probed and improved expeditiously. 
(viii) The teachers serving in Central Schools/K.Vs in the Copper Complex etc. may be 
paid by Government and not by H.C.L. 
(ix) The State Government of Bihar may be requested to renew the leases of 
Siddheshvari and Chapri mines expeditiously and the mining process at these may 
be expedited. 
(x) The areas where _gold and silver is refined should have sufficient reliable security 
to prevent pilferage of these metals. 

119 
(xi) The assets/properties of all Executives, connected with copper mining and 
smelting may be got enquired/verified secretly. 
(xii) A separate cell should be created in the Ministry of Mines or in the organisation 
concerned to deal with all matters like payment of wages, welfare measures etc. of 
the contract labourers.

Representative of the Ministry of Mines 
Shri M.E. Madhusudan-Industrial Advisor 
Shri G.S. Garcha, Director raw materials of SAIL welcomed the Sub-Committee and 
informed that the Raw Materials Division was formed as a unit of SAIL in 1990 by bringing 
together under one umbrella the captive mines of Bokaro, Rourkela, Durgapur and TISCO. 
The objective of forming Raw Material Division. was to have an integrated development of 
the mines and to coordinate the requirements of raw materials to SAIL steel plants. 
He further informed the Sub-Committee that SAIL operates 9 iron ore mines, 5 lime 

120 
stones, two dolomite mines and 3 collieries. SAIL is self sufficient in iron ore and meets 
about 50% of the flux requirements from captive sources. The Sub-Committee was informed 
that the total iron ore reserve from Kiriburu, Meghahetburu, Gua, Bolani, Bursua, Chiria, 
Dalli-Rajhara mines is 2,937 MT and capacity is 23.7 MTPY; total lime stone reserves from 
Kuteshwar, Bhawanathpur, I.L.Q. Satna, Purnapani & Nandini mines is 413 M.T. with total 
capacity of 5.52 MTPY; Dolomite reserve from Tulsidamar & Hirri mines is 39 MT whereas 
the total capacity is 1.02 MTPY. The iron ore mines at Kiriburu, Meghahatburu, Bolani 
Bursua, Dalli-Rajhara and Gua are fully mechanised. Facilities to wash the ore are also 
provided at Kiriburu, Megbahatburu, Bolani, Bursua & Dalli. 
He informed the Sub-Co11;1mittee that the iron ore requirement will go up to 29.6 MT 
from the current level of 22.4 MT, flux requirement from 7.5 MT to 8.9 MT and coking coal 
from 17.1 MT to 19.2 MT. 
Keeping in mind the long gestation period, RMO has initiated actions during the last 
two years to enchance the capacity of the existing mines and also for opening up of new 
mines. A new iron ore mine at Rowghet in Madhya Pradesh with capacity of 5 MTPA has 
been developed. The new iron ore mine at Chiria with a capacity of 6.5 MTP A is to be 
developed. Its feasibility report has been prepared and further action will be initiated once 
the issue of modernisation of liSCO is finalised. Similarly the capacity of Bursua mine from 
1.8 MTPA to 2.7 MTPA is being augmented. 
In the field of Flux, Kuteswar mine is being modernised to 3.0 MTPA Satna mine is 
being modernised to enhance its capacity from 0.24 MTP A to 0.84 MTPA, a new Baraduar 
dolomite mine with capacity of 1.5 MTPA is being re~opened. Hirri dolomite mine's capacity 
is being expanded from 0.6 MTPA to 0.9 MTPA. New areas for lime stone and dolomite are 
also being searched. 
Shri Garcha further informed that most of the mines of RMD are located economically 
backward tribal regions. But the RMD has taken considerable interest in the peripheral 
development of the villages surrounding the mines. The budget allocations for 1995-96 was 
Rs. 19.20 lakh and 1996-97 it has been raised to Rs. 25 lakhs for improving all the 
necessities for the peripheral villagers. In addition the RMD is providing facilities like 
drinking water, construction of roads and culverts, educational facilities and medical facilities 
to villagers of peripheral villages. 
In reply to question of an honourable member Shri Garcha replied that they have 
sufficient quantities of ores in mines, however they are busy in finding new mines for that 
purpose. There is no cause of worry. 
In reply to a question by another Member, Shri Garcha replied that they are 
maintaining the ecology satisfactorily and they planted 2,33,600 plants. 
The Sub-Committee was happy to note that the Raw Materials Division of SAIL, has 
been doing very well. 
The Sub-Committe~ recommends that the budgetary allocation for welfare activities 
should be raised from the present Rs. 25 lakhs.

REPRESENTATIVE OF THE MINISTRY OF MINES 
Shri M.E. Madhusudan - Industrial Advisor 
Dr. M. Ramakrishnan, D.D.G. (Operations) welcomed the Sub-Committee and 
explained the working achievements, targets and objectives of G.S.I. through slides. He also 
informed the Sub-Committee that the main activities of G.S.I. are mineral exploration (coal, 
lignite ores and minerals), systematic geological mapping; specialised thematic studies, 
Airborne geological surveys, Marine surveys, geotechnical investigation, dis-semination of 
information etc. He also informed that the country is more or less self reliant in respect of 
35 minerals which provide adequate support to the basic industries like steel, ferro alloys 
aluminium, cement, refractories, glass & ceramics, agriculture foundries, abrasives, paints 
and borad spectrum of inorganic chemicals. There is, however, very significant gap in 
respect of 17 minerals and metals for which the country is partly or largely dependent on 
imports. 
G.S.I. has been associated with practically every major discovery in the country and has 
a brilliant track record of exploration and assessment of many important mineral deposits. 
G.S.I.'s current shift of thrust from exploration of ba~ metals and ferrous group of minerals 
to gold and diamond have started yielding results. 
j 

122 
Annually around 170 mineral investigations are being taken up in recent years which 
include about 35 for coal and lignite, 45 for gold, 40 for basemetals, 10 for strategic and rare 
minerals, 7 for diamond, 18 for dimension stones and 15 for fertilizer minerals. G.S.I. has a 
chain of laboratories. 
One honourable Member while praising the activities of G.S.I. drew the attention 
towards Supreme Court's recent verdict. The Sub-Committee was ir.formed that this will have 
some adverse effect on their work as most of the exploratory work is to be done in forests. On 
another question the G.S.I. informed that they require financial assistance for replacement of 
old and worn out machinery and equipment replacement of old ship as well as for 
expansionary works during the first year of the 9th Plan. 
One honourable member mentioned about the speciality of shore water whiCh does not 
percolate in the earth near the shore. 
The G.S.I officers of Bhubaneshwer complained that they have to go to dense forests in 
performance of their work where their life is not secured. They should be allowed to stay in 
nearby guest houses. They should be given priority in allotment. The forest department should 
be asked to cooperate with them . They pleaded that for Rs. 1351.- for personal servants a 
justifiable amount may be fixed and sufficient transport facilities may be provided. There are 
no promotional chances to scientists of G.S.I. They demanded that their department should 
be attached with Ministry of Science & Technology. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that:-
(i) The water on the sea shore should be tested to find out what are the qualities that 
the water does not percolate in the soil. 
(ii) G.S.I. should assess the quantum of diamonds in Orissa and Madhya Pradesh areas; 
and report to Govt. 
(iii) In Bihar Champaran, Giridih, Koderma etc. may be explored to find out gold, 
diamonds etc. 
(iv) The grievances of scientists of G.S.I. as to risk to their personal life, raising the limit 
of personal servants etc. may be considered. 
(v) A cess on minerals may be imposed. 
(vi) Sufficient promotional avenues may be opened for scientists in G.S.I.

The General Manager, Orissa Mining Corporation informed that the mineral 
476/RS F-9-B 
.. 

125 
resources of Orissa are exp1qred by a number of agencies, primarily by the Geological 
Survey of India and the Directorate of Mining & Geology of the State. Other organisations 
viz. O.M.C., SAIL, NALCO, MCL, TISCO and others having mining interests in Orissa 
also do exploration in areas of their interest. All these agencies exchange exploration data, 
examine the result of their exploration and the programme of the coming year, so that there 
is no overlapping of their efforts and a proper coordination is established for achieving the 
best results. 
As a result of such coordinated effort the exploration of tentative reserves in million 
tonnes of coal (46,527) Chromite (183.00), Bauxite (1,670), Iron Ore (3,120), Limestone 
(1,212), Dolomite (1,171) Manganese (49.00) and Graphite (1.5) have been established. 
23 drilling rigs have been put into operation to explore new areas containing Coal, 
Bauxite, Chromite, Iron Ore, Manganese etc. 
There are 581 mining leases in the State covering of tot~l area of 1125 sq. kms., out of 
which mining operation is actively conducted in 370 leases over 919 sq. kms. The important 
minerals produced in those mines are Coal, Chromite, Iron Ore, Manganese, Limestone, 
Dolomite, Bauxite and Graphite. The production of different minerals in 1994-95 was 43.83 
million tonnes and in 1995-96 it v·as 51.12 million tonnes. 
In 1994-95, 2.26 M.T. of minerals worth Rs. 178.43 crores was exported and in 1995-96, 
1.1 M.T. of minerals worth Rs. 209.49 crores was exported. 
The State has sent a detailed proposal for enhancement of royalty on non coal min~rals 
since 1995 and if approved by the Central Government it will go a long way in 
supplementing the internal resources of the State. NALCO is the only organisation owned 
by Govt. of India, who operates bauxite mining which is located at Panchpatmali of Koraput 
district. They produced 2.15 MT in 1994-95 and 2.46 MT in 1995-96. ·The entire transport is 
done by Railways. The aluminium ingots are also transported to Paradeep for export. 
NALCO has set up a captive power plant of 500 MW which utilises the thermal grade coal 
abundantly occuring the Talcher coal field. 
The General Manager, O.M.C. informed the Sub-Committee that there are three 
companies involved in mining i.e. INDAL, LNT and HINDALCO. The total iron ore 
deposit is 3000 M. T. On the Joint Venture with Melanco an expenditure of Rs. 2500 crore 
including a separate railway line from Pachpaani to Paradeep port was incurred. The 
Melantalli mine was leased out to N.M.D.C. but this is not feasible as there is no railway 
line and therefore in could not be explored and a huge amount was wasted on it. The mine 
is lying idle. From Nandmardan mine O.M .. C. proposed that 50% of the mine produce 
should be given to O.M.C. The Sub-Committee was also informed that for the last two years 
steel plants in the country have increased production in their captive mines in order to meet 
the requirement of iron ore and hence totally stopped procurement from other producers. It 
has affected the iron ore produced by Banspani sector iron ore reserves. Alternatively the 
iron ore produced by Banspani sector has to be exported. For that purpose it has to reach 
Paradeep port by a very long route resulting in heavy cost. If a railway line from Banspani 
to Jakhapura is expedited the cost would be half and thus the welfare obligations of mine 
workers as well as their surroundings could be locked well. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that the railway line in Orissa from . Banspani to 
Jakhapura may be expedited. 
The O.M.C. authorities also explained a number of problems in chromite mining. They 
informed that in the State there are 11 chromite mines, two in Dhenkanal, three in keonjhar 
,. 

-------------- -- ----- -----------
126 
and six in Jahazpur. Chromite ore is not sold to any steel plant. Steel plants confine to their 
captive plants. O.M.C. supplies to private parties and the rest is exported. There is a 
recession in the prices of chrome. A Japanese expert team found platinum also in chrome 
ore. The Sub-Committee was informed that the prices of ferro-chromium fluctuate sharply 
between 38 to 90 cents per pound in the International market as against the production cost 
of 42 to 45 cents. During such recession period many of the ferro-chromium plants in the 
country convert their furnaces for producing ferro-manganese till the prices of ferro­
chromium pick up. At this point of time the sale of ferro-chromium in internal market fall.s 
sharply and the situation is not compensated by increase of export because Government of 
India fixes the annual quota of export at 3,00,000 tonne~ per annum and the entire export is 
canalised through M.M.T.C. O.M.C. operates chromite leases by employing a work force of 
about 6000 persons. During the recession period the liquidity position falls sharply. The total 
offtake being limited, addition of new operators will not only induct more number of 
competitors but also aim at prohibitive mining cost for maintenance of 6,000 work force by 
O.M.C. If friable chrome ore is d~alised like iron ore and quota is lifted it may be 
possible for O.M.C. and other operators to explore new areas of international market for 
export of their chromite product and make good the shortfall in internal market. 
The Sub-Committee · recommends that the export of friable chrome ore may be 
decanalised and the quota be lifted immediately. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that in Orissa there is a vast scope for exploration/ 
exploitation of mineral resources. The Ministry of Forests has not been keeping in view the 
proposal of O.M.C. O.M.C. wants the responsibility of scientific mining. The leasing out of 
the coal mines etc. is yet to be examined by O.M.C. · 
The Sub-Committee recommends that the proposal of the State Government of Orissa for 
enhancement of reyalty on non-coal minerals may be cleared expeditiously.

The Convenor introduced the Members and officers of the Sub-Committee and 
explained · the purpose of the visit. 
Shri S.N. Johri, C.M.D. of NALCO, informed the Sub-Committee· that NALCO is 
considered to be a turning point in the 50 years old history of Indian aluminium industry. In 
a major leap forward, NALCO has not only addressed to the country's needs for self 
sufficiency in Aluminium, but also given the country the technology edge in producing this 
strategic metal on the best of world standards. 
While giving a brief account of the working of NALCO the C.M.D. informed the Sub­
Committee that 1995-96 saw the primary metal production reaching an all time high of 5.30 
lakh tonnes, a growth of 9.9% over 1994-95, The domestic consumption in 1995-96 was 
estimated at 5 lakh tonnes, a growth of 11.4% over 1994-95. 
The primary producers with their prices linked to LME have posted comfortable profits. 
In the globalisation process of Indian market the Government have, somehow offered better 
concessions to aluminium imports at a custom tarrif of 12% compared to excise duty of 15% 
on domestic production. He further informed that the company has 11.48% SCs, 4.35% 
S.Ts. & 6.37%, OBCs in 'A' grade posts whereas in 'B' grade the representation is 11.06% 
(S.Cs.), 9.26% (S.Ts.) & 9.()4% (OBCs) in grade 'C' the representation is 19.06% (S.C.); 
23.60% (S.Ts.) and 6.24% (OBCs) and in grade 'D' this representation is 11.46% (SCs.), 
31.65% (S.Ts.) and 27.98% (OBCs). One honourable Member proposed that the Scheduled 
Caste candidates for executive posts should be given some separate special training by 
spending some money on them. The Sub-C~mmittee recommends 'that to fill the backlog of 
vacancies reserved for various categories, special eft'orts may be made to train the persons till 
they attain the required skill and be appointed. 
A few representations received from the employees Unions indicated that they are not 
allotted accommodation. Some people who we_re affected by development of lands have not 
been employed. Medical facilities are not sufficient. The labour class is not treated equally in 
the matter of gate pass. There is a lack of canteen facilities for the children of employees 
and no educational facilities have been· provided. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that all land affected persons be provided suitable jobs, 
proper medical facilities may be provided to all employees and housing problem may be 
solved. The labourers may be provided some facilities in the matter of gate pass. Proper 
canteen facilities should be provided and proper arrangements may be made for imparting 
education to the children of the employees. 

128 
The Sub-Committee visited the hospital run by NALCO and it found the conditions there, 
very horrible. A number of latest machines and equipments were wanting there only. The Sub­
Committee recommends that the conclitions in hospital may be improved. All necessary modern 
machines and equipments may be procured. 
The Sub-Committee was informed by some representatives of workers that in the matter of 
allotment of contracts by tenders some racket is going on as only a few relations of an executive 
are continuously getting the contracts which is causing some discontent among the employe~s. 
The Sub-Committee strongly recommends that in the matter of allotting contracts by tenders 
there should be complete transparency and no undue advantage should be given to anybody. The 
vigilance department should keep an eye on such matters. 
It was pleaded before the Sub-Committee that the rate of customs duty should not be 
decreased further to save the industry.

The difference is also not much. It was informed that more than 50% of the villages of Orissa 
are not electrified.

are paying serious attention to this.

Discussion witb Director, Uranium Corporation of India Ltd. Jaduguda 
On the 30th December, 1996 the Sub-Committee while returning from Ghatsila mines 
visited the Uranium Corporation of India Ltd. an held discussion with its Director who 
informed the Sub-Committee that Uranium is found in nature in the form of Uranium Jllinerals 
as oxides, hydro-oxides, carbonates, phosphates, sulphates, vandates and molybdate of 
Uranium. There are more than 100 minerals containing Uranium. He also informed that in 
acidic rocks like granite, the average content of Uranium varies from 4 ppm to 8 ppm. However, 
in basic vocks as basalts, the content is of the order of 1 to 20 ppm. Shri Bhasin also informed 
that Uranium is also present in soil and water of the earth. He also informed that in India 1.9% 
of the total energy is produced from 9 units out of which 5 are under construction. The 
authorised and paid up capital figures at present stand at Rs. 360 crores and Rs. 371 crores 
respectively. In the process of production of Uranium, Jaduguda mine and mill, Bhatin mine, 
Narwapahar mine Uranium recovery plants at Surda, Raka mine and Mosaboni mine are 
playing a signif~a~nt role. the work of routine monitoring of radiological and conventional 
hazards in the mine and maintaining survillance on the environment of the uranium mining and 
milling industry is being carried out by BARC etc. All social and welfare aspects of the 
employees were found satisfactory by the Sub-Committee. Shri Bhasin also informed that the 
present installed capacity of the nuclear power stations in India is 1230 M.W. and with the 
commissioning of additional PHWRs under construction its installed capacity will increase to 
2170 MW. 
The imbaluaa in sharlag of beneftts of recovering valuable metals i.e. Uranium, Copper, 

129 
Nickel from copper concentrate tailings supplied by Ws Hindustan Copper Ltd. (PSU under 
the control of Ministry of Mines) to Ws Uranium Corporation (I) Ltd. (PSU under the 
control of Deptt. of Atomic Energy) was brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee. As 
these two units were located nearby (15 kms away), the Committee took the opportunity on 
30.12.96 of seeing the facilities established & discussed these issues. 
Ws Hindustan Copper Ltd. (ICC) Ghatsila, while processing copper, supplies free of 
cost, tailings of Copper concentrates containing small quantities of Uranium to Ws Uranium 
Corporation of India Unit, Jaduguda Mines, ·a Public Sector Undertaking under the 
administrative control of Deptt. of Atomic Energy to recover Uranium. Ws Uranium 
Corporation of India Ltd., having facilities extracts Uranium in small quantities from these 
tailings. Besides, Uranium, UCIL, recovers in small quantities Copper, Nickel from the 
tailings supplies by HCL. The tailings after processing by UCIL are supplied back to HCL for 
proper disposal. In the process of by-products recovery, UCIL bears the cost of transporting 
copper Concentrate tailings and realises the values of. Uranium, Copper and Nickel. M/s 
HCL supplies free of cost copper concentrate tailings, bears the cost of transporting tailings 
from UCIL, Jaduguda & does not share the benefits of by-product recovery of Uranium, 
Copper & Nickel. In the process tie up, even the cost of mining, grinding & crushing of 
copper having small quntities of Uranium are not remitted to HCL by UCIL. 
It is obligatory on the part of HCL to dispose off tailings free of Uranium and 
contribution made by UCIL in recovering small quantities of Uranium requires appreciation. 
However, there are imbalances in the sharing of benefits of recovering valuable metals from 
copper concentrate tailings in the tie-up between HCL & UCIL which are situated 15 to 16 
kms. apart. 
Since the cost of mining, crushing & grinding of copper ore by HCL is appreciated and 
as copper tailings/concentrates are supplied free of cost, the sub-committee recommends that 
the benefits of recovering valuable by-products i.e. Uranium, Copper & Nickel should be 
shared equally between HCL & UCIL and suitable mechanism may be evolved. 
The Sub-Committee also recommends that (i) the safety of workers in handling radio­
active Uranium should further be scrutinised and B.A.R.C's latest report should be sought 
and (ii) the environmental pollution especially water pollution by the wastes of Uranium 
Corporation of India Limited should be further scrutinised.

The Sub-Committee was taken round the various units of the smelter plant and was 
made to understand the technical working of various sections. The Sub-Committee also 
visited the staff canteen and hospital complex. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that the lead smelter has the capacity of annually 
producing 8000 tonnes of lead and 12,000 kg. of silver from lead concentrate called Galena 
which is received from its different captive mines of Zawar, Rajpura, Dariba and Agucha, 
all in Rajasthan. The Concentrate is pot sintered. The sinter thus produced is treated with 
hard coke in a low shaft blast furnace from where slag is tapped out and the hard lead 
obtained ,is taken to refinery where in a series of processes it is refined to an extent of 
99.9% purity. Silver is produced as the by product. In all there are 612 workers who work in 
three shifts. Broadly the smelter comprises of charge preparation, sintering blast fumance, 
lead refinery and silver refinery sections. 
On a pointed query the Sub-Committee was informed that the necessary raw material 
i.e. lead concentrate will have to be imported in future depending upon the availability of 
this raw material in the Country. Though this smelter cannot be expanded further but there 
is no question of its being closed. 
During the year 1995-96, 4006 MT of lead and 8695 Kg. of silver was produced by this 
plant. The plant is being maintained satisfactorily and the management is aware of their 
social obligations towards the employees and their families. The medical expenditure per 
employee in 1994-95 was Rs.2432 and in 1995-96 it was Rs.2833 and again the expenditure 
on education of per child of the etpployees during 1994-95 was Rs. 794 and in 1995-96 it was 
Rs.1186. During the year 1995-96 a sum of Rs.7,78,773 was spent on colony for the 
employees & their families. Again a sum of Rs.7,90,843 was spent on transportation of 
school children of employees during .1995-96. On medical facilities a sum of Rs.18,89,386 
was spent during 1995-96 and on cultural, sports and other activities a sum of Rs.2,39,794 
was spent in 1995-96. 
In the hospital maintained by the unit free checking facilities are available to villages all 
around. Drinking water and electricity facilities are also provi4ed to villagers nearby. A 
school is also being run efficiently. // ;'~:' · 
The Sub-Committee was, on the whole, satisfied with visit::Of the plant and with the 
maintenance of various units. The Sub-Committee took keen interest in understanding the 
working in silver refinery. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that the entire process of producinglrefming silver in 

131 
the Tundoo plant should be strictly monitored and if possible it should be computerised by 
way of modernisation.

The Convenor introduced the Members and Officers of the Sub-Committee and 
explained in brief the purpose of the Sub-Committee. 
The Director of Mines and Geology !llso introduced his team of officers and informed 
the Sub-Committee as under :-
(a) Ever since the formation of the State of Rajasthan and the inception of the 
Department of Mines and Geology, the department has relentlessly engaged itself in the task 
of exploration and search for minerals on the one hand and development and opening of 
new mines, strengthening of mineral administration activ_ities and enhancement of state 
revenue on the other hand. It has also engaged itself in liaision with the central agencies in 
exploration of different minerals and petroleum in the state. Presently, the State is the sole 
producer of Jaspar and Wollastonite and leading producer of cement and steel grade 
limestone, soap stone, ball clay, calcities felspar, natural gypsum, kaolin, rock-phosphate, 
ochre and building stones besides tungsten, lead-zinc concentrates and copper metal. The 
development of the backward areas like Barmer, Bikaner, Jaisalmer etc. depends on mining. 
Zawan is the oldest mining areas. Lead-zinc and other minerals are in sufficient quantity in 
the State. Agucha mine is a big store for lead and zinc .. Steel grade limestone is available in 
Jaisalmer. 
Out of the 76 projects during the year 1996-97 working has been undertaken at 60 
projects up to December, 1996. The important minerals under these projects are marable, 
granite, cement, and steel grade limestone, lignite, base metals, clays, sand stone etc. 
The value of Minerals produced has gone up from Rs. 3.5 crores in 1950 to over 
Rs. 645 crores in 1993. Whereas the employment in Mines has also increased from 0.32 
lakhs in 1950 to about 3.50 lakhs in 1996. 
In Udaipur circle, constituted by Udaipur, Raj samand, Dungarpur and Banswara 
districts there are 366 mining leases for various major minerals and 32 prospecting .licences 
are in operation and 2969 mining leases are in operation for minor minerals. 
The Department started with a revenue of Rs. 48 lakhs in 1950 which increased to 
Rs. 21,452.20 lakhs in 1995-96 and to Rs. 11,165.751akhs up to December during the year 
1996-97. 
He further informed that the work in 473 leases in reserved forests and 837 leases in 
protected forests and 280 quarry licences areas has been stopped in pursuance of the recent 
judgement of the Supreme Court. The total area affected under the leases is around 664.57 
Sq. Kms. out of the total area of 2935.44 Sq. Kms. The closures of these mining areas will 
affect about 75,000 workers directly engaged in t.he mining sector and the State will be 
deprived of a revenue of Rs. 48.00 crores. All the investment in these affected areas would 
be a loss to the nation and would go waste.

The C.M.D. welcomed the Sub-Committee and introduced his team of officers while 
giving briefly the activities of Hindustan Zinc Limited he informed the Sub-Committee as 
under:-
. The HZL is, one of India's leading base metal producers, incorporated in Janaury, 1966 
as a public Sector Company after the take over of the erstwhile Metal Corporation of India 
Limited to develop mining and smelting capacities to substantially meet the domestic 
demand of zinc and lead rnetals. Its specturm of activities range from exploration, mining 
and ore processing to smelting and refining of lead, zinc, silver and cadmium metals. It is 
also an important producer of sulphuric acid, rock phosphate and tungsten concentrate. The 
Company is looking forward to opportunities in gold ~d other minerals. 
In 1990 Government of India upgraded the status of H.Z.L. to schedule 'A' Company 
rated as 'very good'. The Company is one of the Private Sector Enterprises identified by the 
G.O.I for disinvestment of its holdings. So far- the disinvestment 'has been 24.07% of the 
paid up capital and the number of shareholders stands at 11,400 as on 
30.11.1996. 
At present H.Z.L. with its headquarters at Udaipur operates five lead-zinc mines with a 
total lead-zinc ore production capacity of 3.04 million t.p.a. and four smelters with combined 
installed capacity of 149,000 t.p.a. zinc, and 65,000 t.p.a. lead, besides, a rock phosphate 
and tungsten mine. _

The Company earned a net profit before tax during 1995-96 at Rs. 65.71 crore. Whereas 
the sales turnover for 1995-96 had been Rs. 823.35 crores. Over the years the Company has 
implemented a number of technology updating schemes in mining, beneficiation and 
smelting leading to increased competitiveness of its operations. 
HZL is conscious of its responsibility for protection of the environment and ecology. 
The Company is in the forefront in takins well-planned and effective steps to check pollution 
and create healthy surroundings for its workforce and populace· residing in and around its 
units. To promote further growth the Company is contemplating proposals for expansion 
and/or the .opening of new mining and smelting units. 
The Company is going ahead with the expansion of Rampura-Agucha lead-zinc mine 
and beneficiation plant from the existing level of 9 lakh TPA to 13.5 lakh TPA at an 
estimated cost of Rs: 45 crore. The project is scheduled to be completed by the end of 1997. 
Zinc market in the country is expected to expand most at the rate of 6 to 7% during the 
9th Five Year Plan period (1997-2002) offering good opportunity to HZL to expand in its 
core competency area and continue to be a market leader in the domestic zinc market. To 
pursue this objective, HZL is contemplating to expand the existing operations besides setting 
up of a new green field zinc smelter having an annual capacity of 60,000 tonnes at an 
estimated capital outlay of'Rs. 600 crores. However, depending upon the techno-economic 
viability, the capacity could range between 60,000 and 100,000 tonnes per annum. 
As a diversification strategy gold bas been identified as yet another metal by HZL. It 
has undertaken exploration work at Jagpura gold prospect in Banswara District of 
Rajasthan. Under its diversification programme it has signed MOus·for joint ventures with 
different foreign collaborators. Recently, the one with BHPM, Australia has been cleared by 
FIB for exploration/development of base and precious minerals in the country. 
The Company during it11 span of over 30 years of service to the nation has emerged as a 
stronger force to reckon with in the non-ferrous metals sector of the country. Since the 
industrial scenario has become intensively competitive, both domestic and international, a 
conscious, effort is on to channelize the activities of the Company accordingly. While replying 
a question by an bon'ble Member the C.M.D. informed that mining in Sargipali area is 
sufficient for five to seven years. Consultancy has been identified for future plans in the area 
and the market survey is under consideration for the aluminium pipe industry. The Sub­
Committee was informed that due to suspension of the production of concentrate the 
earning of foreign exchange was less. But now we have increased. the production of 
concentrate there will be more profits. With the closure of two Zawar mines due to the 
recent judgement of the Supreme Court, 450 workers were affected and have been 
redeployed, production has come down from 750 t.p.d. to 150 t.p.d. i.e. a loss of 600 tonnes 
per day. They proposed that the mining leases granted before the forest conservation Act, 
1980 should be exempted from the recent Supreme Court judgement. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that Rampura-Agucha mines can be operated by 
closing other mines. But out of the total about 60% workers are unskilled and they cannot 
be deployed elsewhere. 30% lead and SO% Zinc can. be produced profitably by the surplus 
labour force. Due to non supply of power there is a loss of 3 to 4 crores in one month 
country's Zinc requirement is increasing by 7%. At present only 50% of the demand is met 
and therefore we have to import the metal but we don't want the monopoly of the foreign 
suppliers. •

higher wage to contract labourers. 
Shri K.H. Muniyappa intimated that recently the Central Labour Minister announced a 
minimum daily wage of Rs. 4<Y- to contract labourers which the Union should strive for the 
contract labourers in the Company. He a1so suggested that a contract labourer with a service 
of 10 years or mor~ should be regularised. The Union President informed that the contract 
labourer is different from the regular labourers. They are directly under the contractor who 
employs them and the contractor pays them. Their job is not of a permanent nature and 
their services are generally terminated after the completion of some work. Thus they go on 
changing their status. The Sub-Committee asked the Union to take up the cause of the 
contract labourers specially the cause of the Scheduled Caste and Scheduled Tribe labourers. 
Shri Gopinath intimated that they have three unions and they will make every effort to 
better the lot of the contract labourers.

Meeting with Officers' Assodation 
The officers' representatives informed the Sub-Committee that the performance of the 
Company is satisfactorily good. The officers' union · is not a recognised union. The
