Meeting with Management 
The Chairman and Managing Director welcomed the Sub-Committee and informed 
that:- · 
(i) · Kudremukh Iron Ore Company Limited, a wholly owned Government of India 
Enterprise, was established in 1976 ~o develop mine and plant facilities to produce 
7.5 million tonnes of concentrate a year. The min..!and plant facilities were 
commisSioned in 1980 and the first shipment of concentrate was made in October, 
1981. A pelletisation ·plant with a capacity of 3 million tonnes per year was 
commissioned in 1987 for production of high quality blast furnace and direct 
reduction grade pellets for export. 
(ii) The deposits occur as banded magnetite quartzite with an average thickness of iron 
formation extending upto 100 metres. The top layer of iron formation is weathered 
where magnetite is partiully oxid~sed to haematite. The underlying primary BMQ is 
similar to taconite ore. The present scope of mining includes only the weathered ore 
which has an average iron content "of 38 per cent. This is beneficiated to produce 
concentrate of + 67 per cent iron. Kudremukb deposits are known to be one of the 
largest deposits in the world. In one deposit alone, where the mining is currently 
carried out, reserves of 700 milllon tonnes of weathered ore and 450 million tonnes 
of primary BMQ have been proved. Besides, there are other deposits in the vicinity, 
namely, Nellibeedu, Gangrikal, Dababudan, etc., with probable reserve of 3000 
million tonnes. 
(iii) Kudremukh the largest iron ore mine in India, is designed to produce 22.6 million 
tonnes of crude ore per year with a daily production capacity of ROM 100,000 
tonnes. The mine is plunncd for a three-shift operation. Blast holes of 12.25" dia are 
drilled in 14 m benches. The blasting is carried out using slurry explosives. Blasted 
material is loaded by 10.7 cu.m. electrical shovel into large dumpers of 108 metric 
tonne capacity. 
(iv) The mined ore is transported from benches to two Gyratory Crushers capable of 
crushing the ore to minus 7" size. ·n1e crushed ore is screened into coarse and fine 
fractions before feeding to Autogenous Mills. The ore ground to minus 20 mesh size 
is subjected to primary magnetic separation and the concentrate, after regrinding, is 
subjected to secondary magnetic separation to recover the magnetic concentrate. 
Tertiary magnetic separation system has also been incorporated to improve the 
quality of magnetic concentrate. The primary magnetic tailings are subjected to spiral 
separation to recover non-magnetic concentrate which is also gr:ound in ball mills. 
Floatation technique for improvement of concentrate quality has been incorporated. 
Process modifications are being curried out regularly with an objective of improving 
the product specification. The entire operation using the most sophisticated 
equipment from crushing to shiploading is fully automated and computer-controlled. 
Computerised mine planning techniques are used to maintain strict quality control. 

139 
(v) The process of pelletisation involves mining of iron ore concentrate with limestone 
· fines and bentonite as additives. Dry grinding mill for grinding limestone and . 
bentonite to pelletisation process is provided. A small proportion of coke/coal is also 
used as additive for improving the physical and metallurgical properties of pellets. 
The mixed material is fed to pelletising discs of 7.5 m dia for production of green 
pellets which are screened on roller screens to remove the undersize and oversize 
materials. 
(vi) Iron ore concentrate stored in two stockpiles having a total capacity of 400,000 
tonnes is reclaimed by reclaimers and loaded into ships through a shiploader with a 
rated capacity of 6000 tonnes an hour. A loading rate of 30,000 to 35,000 tonnes a 
day is guaranteed for Panamax type vessel. The pellets are reclaimed by a bucket 
wheel reclaimer of 4000 tonnes per hour capacity for shiploading. A captive iron ore 
berth has been provided to handle ships of 65,000 DWT with a loaded draft of 12.5 
meters. With the provision of an exclusive berth, waiting time for ships is eliminated. 
Detailed feasibility studies are being carried out to develop the Mangalore Port to 
handle ships of about 120,000 DWT size. 
(vii) The Company has undertaken quality improvement programme to meet the 
international market requirement and also to augment its concentrator plant 
production capacity to 8.0 m_illion tonnes per year and that of the pellet plant, 
capacity to 3.5 million ton.ries per year. The main process route for quality 
improvement is the incorporation of the Floatation system. The Company proposes 
to have its own Captive Power Generation Plant of about 50 MW capacity at 
Mangalore to reduce its dependence on the State Electricity Grid for power supply 
and also to maintain the committed supply of concentrate and pellets to the 
customers. 
(viii) Kudremukh Iron Ore Company Limited has taken adequate measures to maintain 
the ecological balance. A 65 metre high dam and two mini dams have been built at 
Kudremukh to store plant tailings and mine wash off to avoid pollution of perennial 
rivers. The flora and fauna of the area have been preserved. Over 7.5 million .trees 
have been planted as part of the large scale afforestation programme. Afforestation 
has also been undertaken a top the hills after mining. 
(ix) Kudremukh produces high grade iron ore concentrate ideal for use as sinter feed and 
for pelletisation. The concentrate has been used in Japan, Czechoslovakia, 
Yugoslavia, France, Australia, Romania and China as blend for sintering and in Iran 
and Bahrain as a blend in pellet feed. The remarkable feature of Kudremukh Ore is · 
its low alumina, sulphur, phosphorous, Vanadium and other deletrious elements. 
Magnetite content of ore has an added advantage in terms of lesser fuel requirement 
in sintering and pelletisation. 
Kudremukh pellets have been used in blast furnances of steel mills in Hungary, 
Yugoslavia, Turkey, Australia, Taiwan, U.S.A., China, West Germany, Japan, Poland and 
Czechoslovakia. The pellets have also been used in Malaysia, Iraq, Indonesia, France and 
Qatar for DRI production. Kudremukh pellets are used in the two large direct reduction 
plants set up in the west coast of India. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that the . Company's power requirement at 
·Kudremukh was 80 MV A and 25 MVA at Mangalore and in terms of energy the power . 
requirement of the Company is about 600 million units. In November, 1995, a power cut of 
50% and demand cut of 30% was imposed on the Company. After Company's efforts it was

given an allocation of 9.0 million units per month from December, :1995 from the 
unallocated quota from central power generating stations. The Company got 9.0 million 
units from central quota out of Neyveli Lignite Corporation's generation,. from 15th 
November, 1996, KEB has withdrawn 9.0 million units from the central allocation and has 
also imposed 50% power cut and 25% demand cut severely hampering production 
operations in terms of quantity to be produced and also quality of material due to power 
interruptions, low voltage and il\adequate frequency cycle resulting in loss of production of 
about 800,000 tonnes of concentrate and 50,000 tonnes of pellets per annum representing a 
loss of turnover of Rs. 80 crores and reduction in profit of Rs. 30 crores. The following is

permission is not allowed stating that KIOCL cannot get this clearance unless the 
plant is set up by itself. BOOM route is preferred since there will be no involvement 
from KIOCL and completion date of the power plant will be faster, which is about

(d) Approval for 150,000 tonnes of naphtha is to be cleared for KIOCL whether the

The customs department has recently made a new interpretation that the production 
trucks are not entitled to duty exemption either for equipment or for spares. Unless the duty 
exemption is given, the impact of duty on the cost of production will be highly detrimental 
to KIOCL since it has to compete in the international market with other global mining 
giants. 
Ministry of Finance may be requested for issuing either an adhoc notification or a 
permanent relief through a special dispensation for not levying any customs/excise duty on 
supplies made to KIOCL by way of equipments, stores and consumables. 
Government of Karnataka are demanding fuel escalation charges and threatening 
disconnection of power every now and then. The company feels that fuel escalation charges 
are not applicable as per the Government order. The claims of an amount of almost Rs. 115 
crores by KEB will completely wipe out the financial viability of the Company's exports. 
The claim of fuel escalation charges and also the threat to disoonnect power .may be 
withdrawn. No solution appears to be in sight. 
The company has applied for prospecting licence in the adjoining Nellibeedu and 
Gangrikal areas for exploration and extending mining lease area. Prospecting licence for 
Nellibeedu has been granted and mining lease is yet to be obtained. 
In respect of Gangrikal, Government is yet to issue prospecting licence whereby the 
company can further mine about 550 to 600 million tonnes which will enhance the mining 
life by another 12 to 15 years. 
KIOCL has invested about Rs. 600 crores at 1976 prices and earns foreign exchange of 
Rs. 500 crores per annum which amounts to be more than Rs. 2000 crores at todays prices.

The Sub-Committee was informed by the representatives of workers that the employees 
of B.G.M.L. are leading a miserable life due to non receipt of their wages in time. They are 
not being paid any bonus. Wage revision has become due w.e.f. 1.1.93 but nothing is being 
done by the Management. There is no leave encashment in the Company. They also 
complained that there are only 20 doctors for about 60,000 persons. Due to practically no 
security all types of illegal activities and pilferage of gold are going on freely in the 
Company. 
One Hon'ble Member warned the workers to be watchful and careful about their rights. 
They should fight for their interests and try to maintain good relations with the 
management. Any illegal activity should be reported by the workers. 
The officers representatives also desired that Government should render financial 
assistance to the Company and the reference to BIFR should be revoked. They desired that 
the (VRS) Voluntary Retirement Scheme should be made applicable to officers also so that 
they may seek retirement paying way for the promotion of junior staff. 
The Chairman and Managing Director of B.G.M.L. informed the Sub-Committee that 
consequent upon the Company incurring losses continuously and its net worth becoming 
negative from 1988-89 onwards the Company was referred to BIFR and was declared a sick 
unit. A rehabilitation scheme is presently under the consideration of Government. He also 
informed that the tum-round strategy of the Company consists of:-
(i) Underground mining at shallow levels to improve the production of gold from 
650 kg. to 1100 kg. per annum. 

144 
(ii) rationalising manpower from 6200 to 3871 during the next three years. 
(iii) The cost reduction programme. 
(iv) the additional production of gold by processing the tailings through the Carbon-in­
pulp (C.I.P.) plant to be set up by B.G.M.L. 
(v) improving the performance of diversification activities by merging Engineering 
Workshops and project and contracts divisions to exploit market potentials. 
(vi) pursuing identification of new gold deposits for exploitation. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that with 31st March, 1996 asithe cut off date 
a rehabilitation proposal worth Rs. 186.02 crore was sent to Government. In addition to this 
Government of India was also requested to consider sacrifice amounting to Rs. 340.25 crores 
by conversion of plan loan in equity (Rs. 64 cr.); waiver of non-plan loan (Rs. 62.15 crore); 
waiver of interest on plan and non-plan loctns (Rs. 95.20 crores) and waiver of penal interest 
(Rs. 84.31 crore) and concession on interest for future (Rs. ,33.91 crore). 
The total accumulated loss incurred by the Company on 31.3.1996 stood at Rs. 249.31 
crore which would get reduced to Rs. 125.56 by 31.3.1997 once Government of India 
approves the waiver and write offs proposed by the operating agency. The Company would 
continue to incur losses during the first 3 years of the rehabilitation package and would start 
earning profits from the 4th year and onwards i.e. when C.I.P. plant will be operating at its 
full capacity and producing gold. 
The BIFR have issued a show cause notice for winding up of the Company fixing the 
next hearing on 5.2.1997. The BIFR had stipulated to comply with (a) Company's entering 
into a long term agreement with labour unions encompassing rationalization of manpower, 
settlement of labour dues, productivity norms etc. (b) finalising by operating Agency the 
rehabilitation scheme and conduct a joint meeting of all the parties concerned and report the 
results (this has been complied with); and (c) Government of India's communication of their 
specific views regarding restructuring of capital and liabilities and induction of fresh funds to 
the extent required as suggested in the OA's report. 
The Company has appealed against the show cause notice and has already submitted the 
annual plan 1997-98 and Ninth Five year Plan 1997-2002 taking into account the factors 
envisaged in rehabilitation package submitted by the operating agency. 
The total budgetary support envisaged from Government of India for putting up a 3 
million tonne per annum capacity CIP plant for extraction of gold by treating the old mine 
tailings is Rs. 70 crores which will have to be incurred during 9th Five Year Plan period. 
The expenditure from internal generation will only be during phases II and III of C.I.P. 
project which will be required beyond 9th Five Year Plan period. The expected pr6duction 
of gold from C.I.P. plant from 1997-98 to 2006-2007 will be 8823.30 kgs. Putting up the 
C.I.P. plant is the most important component of the turnaround strategy the Company is 
proposing for its revival and the total amount of Rs. 70 crores is to be provided for during 
the Ninth Plan period. As per OA's report during 1997-98 the gold production is anticipated 
to be 807 kgs. and the turnover during that period would be of the order of Rs. 1050 lakhs. 
Normal replacements and renewals of plant and machinery both in production and 
supporting services have to be met during the initial two years i.e. 1997;-98 and 1998-99 for 
which an outlay of Rs. 2.50 <..Tores each is to be provided for. This assistance would ensure 
projected production. No allocation is envisaged at present beyond 1998-99. 
The Chairman and Managing Director admitted that there is no 100% security but there 

145 
is enough security arrangement. He also informed that his request for deployment of central

that a number of cases of theft are pending but none of them pertains to theft of gold. He

mines but were later arrested. He informed that the revision of wages of the employees 
could not be done as there are _no sufficient funds for the purpose. Similarly some welfare

However the company has submitted a rehabilitation package to Government and it is 
pinning high hopes on the C.I.P. plant which is expected to do wonders when installed and

Shri Satish Chander, Director 
The Convenor introduced the Members and officers of the Sub-Committee and 
explained the purpose of its visit. 

147 
Shri Sangode also introduced his team of officers and informed the Sub-Committee that 
the Indian Bureau of Mines came into existence on 1.3.1948 under tbe then Ministry of 
Works, Mines and Power for formulation of National Mineral Policy for central coordination 
and planned development of country's mineral resources. Presently the functions of I.B.M. 
are being carried out by various divisions, one of which is Mines Control and Conservation ol 
Minerals Division which functions through Northern Zone (Ajmer), Central Zone (Nagpur) 
and Southern Zone (Bangalore). 
Under the jurisdiction of south zone there <8fe four regional offices at Bangalore, Goa, 
Hyderabad and Chennai besides one sub-regional Office at Nellore. The main responsibility of 
the zonal office is to enforce systematic development of mineral deposits and conservation of 
mineral resources as well as to suggest ways and means to control the adverse impact of 
mining on environment in mining areas under its jurisdiction. This responsibility is discharged 
through regular inspections of working mines, mineral conservation studies, special studies 
and approval of Mining Plan for execution in mines. · 
There are 1138 working mines under South Zonal Office of I.B.M. which are being 
inspected at regular intervals by the officers of four Regional Offices. Suggestions are issued 
thereafter for improvement in respect of:-
(i) Mining and geological problems to maximise extraction. 
(ii) Prospecting, exploration and preparation of geological plaqs. 
(iii) Systematic development of mineral deposits. 
(iv) Working of low gnade ores and their beneficiation for consumption in mineral based 
industries and for export. 
(v) Stacking of low grade ore as well as mineral rejects separately for future use. 
(vi) Maximising the use of low grade material by blending and upgrading the same. 
Out Qf the 1138 working mines, 266 mines are in Bangalore region, 209 mines are in Goa 
region,·473 in Hyderabad region and 190 in Chennai region. In South Zone the annual ore 
production of iron ore is 39.5 to 40 million tonnes; lime stone 31.5 million tonnes; Bauxite 6.6 
lakh tonnes; Manganese 4.6 to 5.2lakh tonnes; gold ore. 4.2 to 4.6lakh-.tonnes; Barytes 3.5 to 
4lakh tonnes; Dolomite 2.5 to 3lakh tonnes; Magnesite '2.5 to 2.6lakh t(,)nnes; clay 4.7_to 5.7 
lakh tonnes; Silica sand 4.5 to 4.8 lakh tonnes. The major activities_of Southern Zone include 
examination of Mining Plans and Mining Schemes, furnishing cimunents to the· Ministry on 
lease renewals and on irrigation projects in the mineralised areas-arid advising the Ministry of 
Mines on mines and minerals as and when such cases are referred to this office. Processing 
and approval of Mining plans and Mining schemes have become the top priority work for all 
the Regional Offices of I.B.M. while processing mining plans, more emphasis is given at the 
planning stage itself for scientific development of mine and protection of environment in close 
proximity of the mines by proposing appropriate measures in the EMP in-built in the Mining 
Plans. Scientifically well conceived mining plans are approval by I.B.M. for implementation in 
the field generally within the stipulated period of 90 days. 
Upto December, 1996 l.B.M. have approved 2415 Mining Plans and 119 Mining 
Schemes. For timely redressal of grievances in respect of disposal of mining plans Mining Plan 
Grievance Committees have been formulated in each region of I.B.M. which take cognizance 
of written complaints received from the mine owners against approving authorities of I.B.M. 
and examine the grievances for suitable redressal. 

148 
In order to enhance awareness amonpt the mine owners and mine employees towards 
environmental protection in . the minirtg areas· each Regional Office plans and organises 
Mines Environment and Mineral Conservation Week (ME & MCW) in. their respective 
regions so that environmental degradation due to mining is controlled and minimised to 
permissible levels. 
On a query from an hon'ble Member Shri Sangode informed that on receipt of any plan 
first of all after examination the plan. is approved and a plan of the ore body is prepared for 
ore realisation. For a viable project the I.B.M. takes three to six months for according its 
final approval. 
He also informed the Sub-Committee that they have very old technology. No new 
technology has been made available to them for Kolar and Hatti gold mines. According to a 
decision of October, 1996 the limit of mining areas has been extended to 5000 Km. from 25 
Km. with the condition that nobody should have the monopoly. The representative of the 
Central Government informed that as per the latest decision an area of 10,000 Km. can be 
given to a Company only for aerial survey which.has to be surveyed within three years. They 
have to invest money and according to the new guidelines not more than 50% of equity has 
been allowed. Shri Satish Chander further informed that there is a clear guideline for 
maintaining environmental norms as well as the welfare activities of the villages situated 
within a radius of S Km. in the mining activities. Barring a few important metals the rest 
have been placed in the charge of State Government concerned. On a specific question from 
the Convenor about the possibility of reviving work at level 32 in Kolar Gold Mines it was 
informed that the mines have been flooded with water. However, after dewatering the mines 
can be worked for further prOduction of gold but· this is subject to further survey. 
Shri Sangode informed that there is a number of posts lying vacant for quite a long time 
and this is affecting the efficient working of I.B.M. He requested that these may be filled up 
and chances of promotion should be provided I enhanced. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that:-
(i) AU vacant posts may be filled up expeditiously. 
· (ii) Promotional avenues may be provided in I.B.M. 
(iii) Latest scientific technology may be made available to I.B.M.

REPRESENTATIVES OF GEOLOGICAL SURVEY OF INDIA, BANGALORE

REPRESENTATIVE OF MINISTRY OF MINES, GOVERNMENT OF INDIA 
Shri Satish Chander, Director 
The Convenor introduced the Members and officers of the Sub-Committee and 
explained the purpose of the visit. 
Shri Devapriya welcomed the Sub-Committee and informed that Geological Survey of 
India was established in 1851 for coal exploration and gradually expanded as responsibility/ 
needs arose and thus grown into a multidisciplinary scientific organisation with a strength of 
18,000 employees. A full fledged separate department of airborne surveys mainly for 
deciphering hidden mineral resources was set up during 1965. In 1970 it was merged with 
Geological Survey of India. Initial surveys were carried with foreign collaboration during 
1968-71 and later on with the indigenous knowhow /facility. 
The Headquarter at Bangalore has all specialised divisions--Geophysics, 
Instrumentation, G.M.C. drilling etc. The Zonal officers do the ground evaluation of 
acquired data and detailed integrated studies inCluding exploratory drilling. The total 
strength of employees in Bangalore is 750. Under the Canadian International Development 
Agency's aid programme during 1986 the twin Offer Airborne Survey System was acquired. 
AMSE wing's programmes are drawn up after considering the recommendations of Sub­
Committees and Government directions and plan priorities. Searches were made for 
nonferrous metal deposits in Singhbhum Copper belt, Ranchi Plateau, Cuddapah basin in 
over 90,400 Km. area with a total of 1,44,460 line Kms. traversed. 
The airborne geophysical data-both in analog form and digital tapes are available to 
the interested parties at nominal cost. These are made available after obtaining necessary 
clearances and permissions of Ministry of Defence/Survey of India. There is a lot of 
demand for these outputs. 
Through the sales of maps and publications about Rs.11.70 lakhs have been earned and 
remitted to the Consolidated Fund of India. 
About 36 mineral investigation reports of AMSE· wing were cost estimated and are 
made available to the interested parties. The cost works out to be around 3.3% of the 
operational cost. The rates of charges for sponsored surveys are under active consideration 
of the Ministry for appr.oval. 
For the further improvement it requires special permission for low altitude flight 
surveys; more fluent and quicker clearances from M.C.A. for routine matters; delegation of 
necessary administrative and financial powers for effective operations to Head of 

150 
Dq>artment-more autonomous if needed to compete commercially; provision for incentives 
to the scientists involved in the . commercial I sponsored items for motivation I effective 
involvement, flight allowance to the scientists on board, and all these will go a. long way in 
improving the quality of surveys in a cost effective time }?~und . scheme with a sense of 
involvement among personnel. Sbri S.S. Murthy informed the Sub-Committee that in the 
southern region G .S.I. has been working successfully in the field of airborne surveys. It has 
done a good job in the Indo-Gangetic areas for oil. Further surveys are being conducted by 
.oil authorities separately.The main survey of G.S.I. is fpr minerals and metals. Oil industry 
has been desiring that we should survey for them but G.S.I. has not sufficient manpower 
and whatever staff they have they can survey for hydrocarbons. Reclaiming the abandoned 
mines is also being done by G.S.I. No expansion of staff has taken place after .the Seventh 
Five Year Plan. The D.G.C.A. ,permits drilling over 120 meters but G.S.I. requires drilling 
at 60 meters in which a man goe~ empty stomach which is a very difficult task. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that in the airborne survey there is a lacunae. In the 
first year it is for 5000 Km. and in the second year it is restricted to 2500 Km. only and in 
the third year only 25 Km. is allowed. But in the. airborne survey it is not at all possible tc> 
come to 25 Km. It should remain SOOO Km. which is the only solution.

should be allowed to indiJenous companies so that the nation may benefit from

154 
Governments perception regarding performance of Indian Steel Sector 
3. Ministry of Steel vide their letter No. H-11-14(7)'96 dated 6th December, 1996 informed 
.the Sub-Committee that the steel industry in general responded to the policy changes 
enthusiastically. This was evident from the performance of different segments of the industry 
some highlights of which were as under: 
(a) Compared to the production level of 14.33 million tonnes in 1991-92, the production 
of finished steel in 1995-96 had been 21.40 million tonnes, · thereby registering a 
growth of over 49% and an average ·annual growth of 12.33%. In the current year, 
10.66 million tonnes of finished steel has been produced upto September, 1996 
which is 13.3% higher than the production during the corresponding period last 
year. 
(b) Pig iron production which was 1.59 million tonnes in 1991-92, increased to 2.79 
million tonnes during 1995-96, registering an overall growth of over 75%. During 
the current year 1.42 million tonnes of pig iron has been produced upto September, 
1996 which is 7.1% higher than the production during the corresponding period last 
year. From being an importer of pig iron till.1991-92, India has emerged as an 
exporter. The exports were 5.04 lakh tonnes. during 1995-96, earning valuable 
foreign exchange of Rs. 243 crores approximaJely. 
(c) Sponge iron production which was 1.3 million tonnes in 1991-92 increased to 4.4 
million tonnes 1995-96, registering a phenomen;ll growth of over 238%. This year 
till September 1996, 2.313 million tonnes of sponge iron has been produced India is 
already the second largest producer of sponge iron in the world. Export of sponge 
iron tou~hed 0. 79 million tonnes during 1995-96 fetching foreign exchange earnings 
of Rs. 350 crores approximately. Besides, production of sponge iron in the country 
has resulted in considerable savings on import of metallics for the secondary steel 
sector. 
(d) Export of iron and steel, which was mere 3.73 lakh tonnes in 1991-92 reached 20.06 
lakh tonnes in 1995-96 registering a growth of 438%. 
(e) After delicensing of iron & steel industry and as a result of the steps taken for 
creation of additional capacity in the private sector, as on 1.8.96, All India Financial 
Institutions have clearcd20' projects involving an annual capacity of 11.26 million 
tonnes of saleable st~el and investment of Rs. 20.095 crores. Out of these, seven 
projects involving instrument of about Rs. 6238 crores have already been 
commissioned with an annual capacity of 3.91 million tonnes. Eight more projects 
with an estimated capacity of 3.98 million tonnes per annum and involving 
investment of Rs. 8926 crores are under appraisal. 
Central Marketing Organisations (CMO), SAIL 
. 4. Central Marketing Organisations was established in 1963 to handle the marketing 
operation for all the steel plants of the then Hindustan Steel Limited. It has since grown into 
a vast distribution network of 42 Branch Sales Offices, 38 Departmental Stock Yards. 140 
Consignment Stock Yards and 8 Authorised Distributors£xtention Counters. CMO accounts 
for 70% of total sale of saleable steel through its wide net work whereas 30% of steel is sold 
by the plants to customers against orders booked by CMO. The Sub-Committee was 
informed by the·representatives of CMO that in the wake of decontrol of pricing/distribution 
of steel w.e.f. January, 1992 Central Marketing Organisation responded to the needs of the 
challenging market by modifying its systems, procedures and policies. There has been a 
gradual shift from volume orientation to market oriented production and marketing with an 
emphasis on value. 
5. The Sub-Committee expressed their concern over the present stock position of Steel 

155 
which is stated to be three times more than the normal level despite the efforts made by the 
Central Marketing Organisation. The representatives of CMO stated that presently the total 
stocks in the stock yards and plants of SAIL put together exceeded one million tonnes, the 
value thereof corning to Rs. 1,500 crores and the monthly co,st of this inventory is about Rs. 
19 crores. SAIL has however taken a number of steps to increase the sale of steel and check 
the trends of rising inventory viz., (i) vigorous customer contact and follow up of individual 
customer orders for affecting speedier delivery; (ii) orienting product mix to suit market 
needs; (iii) adjusting production as per market requirements; (iv) adopting strategic pricing 
depending upon market segment; (v) competition and quantity linked bulk purchases; and 
(vi) maximising exports of some categories to bring down the stock levels. With regard to 
export potential it was clarified that it depended among other factors on the demands and 
supply in the international market. 
6. On being asked whether new areas were being explored by CMO to promote 
consumption of steel in domestic market particularly in agricultural sector, the Sub­
Committee was informed that promotion and consumption of steel in agricultural sector and 
small scale industries was very much part of SAIL's marketing strategy. 
The Indian Iron and Steel Company Ltd. (liSCO) 
7. liSCO, one of the oldest integrated Steel Plant in India still operates with outmoded 
technology for making of steel mainly due to inadequate investment in updating its 
technology resulting in high cost of production and it is, therefore, unable to compete in the 
open market. The Sub-Committee was informed that cost of production had further 
escalated by the spiralling hike of coal prices, railway freight, D.V.C., power tariff and 
expenditure on petro fuel. The Sub-Committee was informed that the losses of the Company 
have come down ever since its take over by SAIL. Last year the Company suffered badly on 
account of wage revision. 
8. On the proposal for revival.tehabilitation of liSCO the Sub-Committee was informed 
that a proposal in that regard was submitted by SAIL and liSCO to I.D.B.I on the ·2nd 
November, 1994 seeking a budgetary support of Rs. 1100 crores from Government of India. 
The Government expressing its inability to provide this budgetary support suggested for a 
joint venture with a private partner for revival of the Company. Consequent to the SAIL's 
global advertisements seeking joint venture participation, only two parties viz. TPE of 
Russia and MITSUI of Japan submitted their offers. MITSUI later declined to participate in 
the equity. TPE has requested for an extension of time for finalising the issue. 
9. As regards the constraints being faced by the Company on marketing side, the .Sub­
Committee was informed by the Management that liSCO depended heavily on pig iron sale, 
steel production being costlier due to outdated technology of Open Hearth Route. Of late, 
pig iron market of the Company has been seriously affected by the competition in the 
market from the secondary producers which have increased in number and also due to the 
heavy imports from outside. The Company is also facing problems as the market is presently 
continuing to show sluggish trend from the last few months. · 
Hindustan Steel Works Construction Ltd. (HSCL) 
10. HSCL was incroporated in 1964 with the primary objective of undertaking complete 
construction of modern integrated Steel Plants right from the stage of site investigation to 
the stage of commissioning of the plant. The Company today has a wide range of expertise 
in steel sector, power plants, dam construction, bridges, coal handling plants, underground 
communication and transport system, industrial and town ship complexes, etc. The 
476/RS F-ll·A 

156 
Company's turnover crossed over Rs. 400 '-Tore mark in 1995-96. The operational loss of Rs. 
6 crore in 1991-92 changed over to operational profit of Its. 3 crore in 1994-95 and Rs. 5 
crore in 1995-96. The accumulated losses of the Company as on 31st March,.1996 are to the 
tune to Rs. 644 crores. When asked about the reasons for losses the Sub-Committee was 
informed that payments to HSCL were stopped in Libya and loan liability of the Company 
became enormous. The Company also suffers from surplus manpower, Wage liability of the 
Company came to Rs. 170 crores in the 1995-96. Moreover, after the completion of Steel 
Plants in the Country, the Company does not regularly get the jobs in Steel Sector. At the 
same time company is facing Competition from the private sector and lags behind owing to 
its overhead cost. Efforts are being made to increase the turnover position of the. Company 
as also to generate sufficient margin to meet salaries and wages and the fixed costs. The 
Company has diversified its activities into other core sectors, such as coal, power and other 
infrastructural facility projects; real estate business; and seeking joint venture for getting the 
technology for slag reduction and processing including marketing of by products. 
11. The Management of the Company informed the Sub-Committee that the Company 
was a viable and profitable unit withou~ having any past lia~iJity. The main problem the 
Company is facing is on account of finances and efforts are being made to get partner(s) 
who could give loans on moratorium basis on ~e partner with sound balance sheet so that 
financial institution could invest. 
Metal Scrap Trading Corporation Limited (MSTC) 
12. MSTC Ltd. incorporated in 1964 was the canalising agent for import of (i) carbon 
steel melting scrap; (ii) sponge iron/hot briquetted iron; and (iii) re-rollable scrap till 
decanalisation in February, 1992. Presently, the Company's status is the same as of any 
other importer and its operation in the area of foreign trade has suffered. However, the 
efforts were being made to compensate through domestic trade operation by acting as selling 
Agency to more than 200 units in the Government and Public Sector. As a result, there has 
been 90% growth over past three years. 
13. The profits of the Company declined during 1995-96 and 1996-97 mainly due to the 
factors affecting foreign trade. The Company has been declaring dividends ranging from 
20% to 40% and has not sought any budgetary support. 
14. The management of Company further informed the Sub-Committee that they were 
looking forward for assistance of Government for issue of Notification defining the physical 
dimension of Heavy Melting Scrap (HMS) and Re-rollable Scrap including these items, in 
customs classification; and providing a differential duty structure for HMS and re-rollable 
scrap. Apart from this, it also solicits Government's assistance in providing funds for 
working capital; in routing of all· advance licences from all organisations under Ministry of 
Steel through MSTC; and in ensuring aJl organisations under Ministry of Steel to utilise 
Company's services for meeting their import requirements as far as feasible instead of using 
other intermediaries. 
Office of the Development Commissioner, Iron and Steel at Calcutta •

units in the secondary steel sectors. This office has the responsibility to see the health and 
growth of this sector from time to time by providing necessary assistance for making 
available inpute materials like Coal, Coke, Furnance oil, power and allotment of Railway 
wagons ~nd other materials. The Sub-Committee was informed thut this office called a 
periodical meetings with the representatives of Central Electricity Authority, R-ailway Board, 
Central Pollution Control Board, etc. to review and sort out the problems being faced by the

secondary sector units in developing their units. This encourages the development of new

16. The Development Commissioner, Iron and Steel further informed the Sub­

members under the Chairmanship of Development Commissioner, Iron and Steel. The JPC 
also managed the funds like the Steel Development Fund, Engineering Goods Export 
Assistance Fund so as to develop the steel industry. 
Bird Group of Companies 
17. Presently, seven companies are working under th~ umbrella of Bird Groups of 
Companies viz., the Orissa Minerals Development Company Ltd. (OMDC), the Karanpura 
Development Company Ltd. (KDCL), the Bisra Stone Lime Company (BSLC), the Slotty

and actions have been taken to improve the m?rketability of products through better product 
mix and- enrichment of quality. · 
Durgapur Steel Plant (DSJ•) 
18. The Sub-Committee visited Durgapur Steel Plant at Durgapur and held discussions 
with the representatives of plant. The Sub-Committee was informed that plant was 
producing both primary and secondary products viz. pig iron, billets, slabs, TMT bars, angle, 
railway products like wheel axle, BG sleepers in the primary sector and iron scrap, steel 
scrap, molted slag, grenulated slag in the secondary sector. 
19. On being asked about the marketing of steel produced by the plant, the Sub­
Committee was informed that due to slump for the last 3 months in the steel market, the 
demand of steel has come down and profit ratio has gone down. Domestic demand in the 
steel sector grew at a very low rate. Keeping in view the present competition in .the market 
DSP has changed its policy and customer satisfaction has become the main feature of this 
policy. The plant has started quick service scheme for its customers. On the issue of cost of 
production, it was informed that its cost of production was the lowest in the country and its 
quality had also improved. 
20. The Sub-Committee was also informed that the plant was facing problem on 
account of poor quality of coal. They urged before the Sub-Committee to consider this 
problem and stop the monopoly of coal supplier. The Sub-Committee was further informed 
that the plan~ was in crisis because of market position being very dull and cash loss of the 
plant was on·· the increasing side. 
Manganese Ore India Limited (MOIL) 
21. MOIL is the single largest producer of manganese ore in the country. The 
Company, apart from producing and marketing manganese ore has identified the need to 
expand and diversify its activities by producing value added products based on manganese 
ore. The Company has already put up an Electrolytic Manganese Dioxide (EMD) plant with 
an installed capacity of 600 tonnes per annum. The Company is contemplating to put up 
another EMD plant having capacity of 1200-1500 tonnes per annum at an estimated cost of 
Rs. 10 crore. 
22. The Sub-Committee was informed by the management that during 1995-96 the 
or; 
. )' 
• • ~1'· 

158 
Company crossed Rs. 100 crore business and recorded 8% growth rate in production as 
compared to the previous year. The Company is continuously signing the MOU with 
Government of India and in 1995-96 its performance had been rated 'excellent'. The 
Company earned profit (after tax) of Rs. 10.78 crore in 1995-96 and paid dividend to 
Government at the rate of 20% for the last three years. 
23. Presently, the Company is facing constraints on account of restriction for 
deployment of contractors for production of manganese ore. Earlier the Company was 
engaging petty contractors for extraction of saleable grades of ore from the old dumps. The 
work being of temporary nature was not economically viable for departmental exploitation. 
The management of the Company desired that engagement of contractors for dump mining 
may be allowed which would go a long way in conservation and optimum utilisation of this 
valuable mineral resource. The attention of the Sub-Committee was also drawn to the 
presentation made by MOIL before the Committee on Industry on 15th November, 1995 at 
Bombay. Pursuant to the recommendations of the Committee in its 17th Report presented to 
Parliament on the 7th March, 1996, the Government had taken up the matter with Central 
Advisory Contract Labour Board for grant of exemption to MOIL which is still awaited. The 
Sub-Committee impress upon the Government to expedite the exemption to MOIL from the 
operation of the Government's Notification NO. U. 23013/123/056-LW dated 23rd March, 
1993 prohibiting engagement of contract labour in respect of manganese ore mines. 
Bhilai Steel Plant 
24. BSP, set up in the 2nd Five Year Plan for production of one million tonne of ingot 
steel annually has now took up an ambitious programme to raise its annual production 
capacity of raw steel to 4 million tonnes. After the expansion, the thrust was on removal of 
bottlenecks and investing in addition, in modification and replacement schemes so that the 
capacity utilisation could be increased without investing in new capacity. In the year 1995-96 
the plant produced 4.07 million tonnes of crude steel, 4.38 million tonnes of hot metals and 
3.5 million tonnes of saleable steel. Primarily being an ore based plant, coking coal is 
transported by rail from Bengal and Bihar, Limestone from Nandini and dolomite from Hiri. 
In addition, the plant also gets supply of coal imported from Australia as the domestic coal 
supplied by Coal India Ltd. has more ash content thereby affecting the quality of steel 
produced in the plant. The domestic coal also suffers from frequent price hikes. It was 
suggested by the management in this regard that there should be a check or regulation on 
the frequent price hikes of coal and there should also be an improvement in its quality so 
that additional burden on production of steel is reduced and the dependence upon imported 
coal is also minimised. · 
25. In the course of discussion the Sub-Committee was informed that iron ore 
exploration from Rajhara mines were becoming gradually uneconomic as the ore was 
explored from a very low level and its quality was also low due to less lump content. To 
meet the requirements of plant Raughat mines have been identified which is yet to be 
connected by railways. State Government of Madhya Pradesh have conditionally cleared the 
project on the ground that rail line be extended to Jagadalpur. 
26. With regard to huge build up inventories lying in the stockyards of SAIL and its 
plants, the Sub-Committee was informed that at present BSP was also facing this problem. It 
was hoped that the downward trend in consumption of steel in domestic as well as 
international market would be checked and demand would pick up. The projections for the 
9th Plan revealed that there would be a shortage of steel. BSP is making all out efforts to 
meet out the challenges by according top priority to quality improvement, cost re~uction arid 
better customer service. The Sub-Committee was further informed thut there was a need of 

159 
restructuring of PSUs in the steel sector so as to give ore autonomy to each production unit 
and accordingly the control of holding Company i.e. SAIL needed to be reduced. 
Sponge Iron India Ltd. (SilL) 
27. SilL was incorporated in 1975 with the primary objective of setting up of a 
demonstration project of 30,000 tpa capacity to establish techno economic feasibility of 
manufacturing sponge iron suitable for steel making, using indigenously available iron ores 
and non-coking coals. The demonstration sponge iron plant was commissioned in 1980. 
Thereafter the Company demonstrated the use of sponge iron as substitute to scrap by 
rendering assistance to various steel making units in the country. Presently, the Company 
has reached the optimum level of operations with a production level of 50,000 tonnes of 
sponge iron. There is also a proposal to take up the production of ferro alloys through the 
existing Sub-merged Aic Furnace (SAF) plant which is expected to go for commercial 
production in 1997. With the present operations, the Company is showing a loss of Rs. 50 
lakhs per aunum. Losses are mainly attributable to increase in input costs particularly of coal 
and iron ore, and heavy competition in the market. According to the management, the only 
solution to this problem was to reduce cost of production and it could be done only if the 
Company goes for expansion of the sponge iron capacity from present 60,000 to 120,000 
tonnes. It would result in reduction in the overall cost of the total production by Rs. 500/­
per tonne and achieve overall profit of Rs. 4.5 crore per annum. As per the Company's 
proposal which is presently before the Planning Commission, the expansion would cost Rs. 
50 crore including the captive power plant. 
28. The other area where Company feels handicapped is the availability of cheaper 
imported scrap in comparison to sponge iron for use in the furnaces. This could be possible 
due to reduction of import duties from 45% in 1991 to 5% in October, 1994. The 
management of Company pleaded before the Sub-Committee for increase of import duty by 
10% so as to help the sponge iron industry in general and SilL in particular. The Sub­
Committee desires that Government should look into the matter • 
. 
National Mineral Development Corporation (NMDC) 
29. NMDC was established in 1958 as a public sector company to undertake 
exploitation and development of all minerals other than coal, oil and atomic minerals. 
NMDC had developed several important mines and deposits of iron ore like Kiriburu and 
Meghatuburu iron ore mines in Bihar, Kudrehmukh iron ore deposit in Karnataka, and also 
other mineral deposits .like Khetri copper deposit in Rajasthan, phosphate deposit in 
Mussorie, etc. Some of these were later on handed over to other public sectors and some of 
them later became separate public sector companies. The Sub-Committee was informed that 
as on 31st March, 1996, the authorised share capital and paid up capital of the Company was 
Rs. 150 and 132.16 crores, for respectively, and there is no outstanding loan due to be paid 
to the Government. The profits of NMDC had been around Rs. 100 crores for the last few 
years and the highest ever profit of Rs. 150.46 crores was achieved in 1995-96. NMDC has 
been paying 20% dividend for the last five years amounting to Rs. 26.43 crores each year. 
30. Management of the Company informed the Sub~Committee that full requirements 
of Visakhapatnam Steel Plaut and substantial portion of the requirements of major units that 
have come up in sponge iron sector like Essar Gujarat, Nippon Denro Ispat Ltd. and other 
new iron and steel projects ln und around Karnataka are being met by the Company. It was 
also informed that the Company has got a great potential for supply of iron to steel industry 

160 
and it needs to be further strengthened. It has already started preparing plan for expansion 
of its mining capacity by developing Kumaraswamy B & C block in Karnataka and Bailadila 
10:11 a project in M.P. out of its own resources, without any budgetary support. The Sub· 
Committee desires that Government should encourage NMDC to expeditiously develop mines 
referred to above. • 
31. The Sub-Committee expresses its happiness on the overall performance of NMDC in 
terms of production and profitabUity. The Sub-Committee feels that in view of good technical 
& financial position and experience in exploration and development of many other minerals, 
NMDC is capable of playing a leading role in the development of minerals like diamond, gold 
etc. apart from iron ore. The Government should also take necessary steps for strengthening 
the position of NMDC. 
Confederation of Indian Industries (CII) 
32. The representatives of CII stated that in 1995-96 there has been 13% growth in the 
demand of steel in the country whereas the growth of demand in current year has come 
down to 8-9%. The Sub-Committee was informed that it was_ expected that demands would 
reach 24 million tonne in 1996-97 as against 20 million tonnes in 1995-9~ Besides, there was 
surplus availability of steel due to imports. Import has been on an increase due to decline in 
international prices of steel during the last one , and half year.: Continuous decline in 
international market has been due to excess capacity and depressed demand in international 
market and an early recovery was not likely in international market. 
33. When asked about the competitiveness of domestic production of iron and steel, the 
Committee was informed that in our country the input cost is very high in comparison to the 
developed countries. It comes. to 33--35% in India whereas the same costs 2% in other 
countries. With regard to custom duties, the Sub-Committee was told that custom duties at 
1996-97 level be retained. It was further suggested that there is a need to impose non tariff 
barriers such as fixation quota, local content rule and other quantitative restrictions on the 
line of steps taken by other steel producing countries. High regulatory duty of 10% above 
than that on primary products may be imposed on the imports of secondary and defectives. 
On the issue of. excise duties the Sub-Committee was informed that it should be reduced on 
steel products to 10%; on constructional steel/GC sheet to 5%; and freight and distribution 
charges may be exempted from calculation of assessable value for excise duty. 
Federation of Indian Chambers of Commerce and Industries (FICCI) 
34. The Sub-Committee has been informed by the FICCI that there are positive signs of 
revival of Steel industry all over the world. The increase in demand is significant in 
developing countries especially in South East Asia and Pacific basin. In the developed 
countries it is likely that there will be future shortage of metallics aad coke in particular. It 
thus provides a g;)od opportunity for India to become a global player in the induslry. There 
will also be a fundamental change in the demand for steel in India in the long run. India has 
a comparative advantage in the production of steel in terms of raw material cost and labour 
cost but these factors are nullified because of technology handicap. There is a strong need 
for restructuring of Indian steel industry in terms of modernisation and technology 
upgradation. In this context adoption of new technology like COREX in new plants and 
modernisation of existing plants is essential to provide a competitive edge to the Indian steel 
producers. The performance of steel sector depends upon the efficiency of infrastructural 
sector particularly power and transportation net work. 
35. The representative of FICCI who appeared before the Sub-Committee stressed 

161 
upon the need for heavy investment in steel sector and also in infrastructural sector and 
suggested that it could come through Foreign Direct Investment (FDI). At the same time 
the representatives pleaded for Government's strict regulatory control on this issue. They 
felt that there was need for intensive· efforts to popularise consumption of steel with a view 
to augment its demand in the country particularly, in agricultural and construction sector. 
36. With regard to present rates of custom duties on steel products it was suggested that 
·import duty on finished steel items (Semis and finished pr9ducts) should not be reduced for 
a period of 2 years i.e. till 1999. Import duty on essential inputs needed by the domestic 
steel industry should be reduced substantially. As regards Central Excise Duty on 
domestically produced steel the Sub-Committee was informed that it should be lowered from 
15% to 10%. 
OBSERVATIONS 
Marketing and Consumption of Steel 
37. Having gone through the information supplied by the Ministry of Steel, and 
discussions with the Managements and Workers' representatives of the Organisations/ 
Companies referred to in para 2 above and evidence of representatives of CII and FICCI, 
the Sub-Committee expresses its concern on the down ward trend in the entire steel sector, 
despite the appreciable growth in the production of steel in the country. The slump in steel 
sector has not only adversely affected the steel plants but also the industries connected with 
production of iron and steel. It is an indicative of lack of coordination between 'the 
production of steel in the country on the one hand and marketing thereof on the other. Jt 
also indicates that growth in production of the steel in the country has not resulted in 
corresponding growth of consumption of steel. Consequent to the industrial liberalisation 
policy of Government the public sector steel producing units witnessed a different era which 
they were not used to. Now there is a shift from production oriented units to market 
oriented units. Import duties came down drastically resulting in import of steel and leading 
to heavy competition in domestic market. The steel industry in the country is now facing 
problem on account of huge inventories build up in SAIL stockyards and steel plants worth 
Rs. 1800 crores. 
38. The Sub-Committee has been assured by the Ministry of Steel, Central Marketing 
Organisation, SAIL and steel plants visited by the Sub-Committee that this situation is for a 
very short period and that steel industry would pick up and future projection reveal that 
there would be global shortage of steel and the fact that about 30 new projects are coming 
up in the country is also an indication of future consumption trends. The Sub-Committee is, 
however, not convinced by the plea of the Government. 
Impact of revised customs and excise duties 
39. After liberalisation, import duty rates on iron and steel items have been gradually 
reduced over the years. Changes brought about in customs duties in .respect of some of the 
items may be seen from table given below:-

Relaxation of import duties has resulted not only in opening up of domestic iron and 
steel market to international competitors but it has also made the domestic market heavily 
competitive for the domestic producers. Apart from this the domestic producers also 
suffered from the increase of excise duties that has registered growth in respect of the items 
mentioned ranging above from 12.5% in 1993-94 to 15% in 1996-97. 
Export 
40. Presently, as per the policy of Government, the export of iron and steel is freely 
allowed. According to Ministry of Steel, in the last five years the export of saleable steel 
registered a growth of 383% in quantitative terms and 837% in value terms. However, the 
information supplied by C.M.O., SAIL reveal that there has been 24% decline in 1995-96 
compared to 1994-95 in value terms. The export, from the country includes semis, hot rolled 
coils, cold rolled coils, pig iron and sponge iron in addition to the traditional items like 
plate, bars and rods, and structurals. In future export is expected to be of higher value 
added items. The Sub-Committee was informed by the Ministry of Steel that an Expert 
Group has been constituted to evolve a strategy to give impetus to the export of iron and 
steel. 
41. In the process of examination, the Sub-Committee also went into the comparative 
cost of steel produced in India and in other countries. The Sub-Committee felt it necessary 
in view of lifting of international trade barriers where only economic consideration had to 
determine both the domestic and external trade hence the ability of the industry would be 
judged by the lower operation costs and better product quality. 
42. According to the Bureau of Industrial Costs and Prices (BICP) tour report on cost 
study of steel industry of 1995 report, the cost of production of saleable steel in Bokaro and 
Bhilai Steel plants of SAIL during 1994-95 was less then the cost of production in Korea 
(Pohang plant of POSCO) and Japan (Kimitsu plant of Nippon). The cost of production of 
saH~able steel of SAIL during 1994 was less than the cost of production in USA, UK, France 
and Germany. The Sub-Committee is constrained to note that despite the above stated cost 
advantages, the domestic steel producers are not getting the desired markets outside and.are 
also facing grave competition in the domestic market. The Sub-Committee impress upon the 
Government to take appropriate measures to come out of this situation. · 
Partial withdrawal of freight equalisation Policy 
43. After deregulation of pricing and distribution of Steel, Government has. paTtially 
withdrawn the freight equalisation policy removing the freight disadvantages to states located 
near plants. According to present policy the main producer of steel are charging ;.actual 
freight upto the stockyards or freight element as existed earlier under the earlier freight 
equalisation scheme whichever is less. In doing so the freight disadvantage to the states/areas 
located near the steel plants of the main producer~ has been removed and at the same time 
advantage of lower frej$ht equlisation scheme to the distant areas/states has been protected. 
The extra burden on Wtis account is borne by the main producers themselves. There is no 
Government subsidy for this purpose. The annual burden on SAIL alone on this account is 
Rs. 200 crore. Government have also received representations from the states like Tamil 
Nadu, Punjab and Assam for re-introduction of freight equalisation scheme. The Economic 
Research Unit of Joint Plant Committee is presently conducting a study on the impact of 
partial withdrawal of freight equalisation policy. 

163

According to Ministry of Steel, the projected gap in finished steel will be met by 
imports. There is no problem anticipated in the availability mat~ing the projected demand 
for alloy and special steel. As regards the sponge iron the MilJistry have stated that there 
would be a net short fall attributable to many of the existing Direct Reduced Iron (DRI) 
units going in for forward integration thereby reducing the availability of merchant sponge 
iron. The Government have adopted a two pronged strategy for increasing steel production 
in the country i.e. through (i) modernisation. and expansion of steel plants in the public 
sector; and (ii) creation of additional capacity in the private sector. 
45. In pursuance of this strategy, SAIL has undertaken a massive modernisation 
programmes of its plants at Durgapur, Rourkela and Bokaro primarily with the objective of 
updating the technology for reducing the cost of production and for improving the quality of 
the products. As regards the creation of additional capacity in private sector, the Ministry of 
Steel informed the Sub-Committee that as on 1st August, 1996 all Indian Financial 
Institutions have cleared 20 projects involving an annual capacity of 11.26 million tonnes of 
Saleable steel and investment of Rs. 20.095 crores. 
RECOMMENDATIONS 
46. The Sub-Committee feels that it is the high time .for the Government to review the ~: 
objectives and functioning steel plants not only in terms of:,mechanism but also the business 
orientation of the management. The Sub-Committee suggests that Government should direct 
the concerned authorities like CMO, SAIL and office of .·DCIS to undertake an exhaustive 
market survey to assess the emerging market dimensions in view of the consumption pattern 
in the country in the coming years. 
47. The Sub-Committee also recommends to the Government to encourage consumption 
of steel in·· rural and agricultural sector. The Sub-Committee bas reasons to believe that this is 
the area.·which has almost been neglected in so far as the consumption of steel is concerned. 
Concerted efforts from the marketing agencies. of the Government are need of the hour for 

164 
promotion of consumption of steel in this sector. The Sub-Committee recommends that direct 
contact be made with institutions such as Agricultural Universities and authorities such as Block 
Development Officers, Industrial Development Officers of the State Governments to assess the 
requirement of the agro processing industries and if necessary, SAIL may set up its own institute 
specifically for promotion of consumption of steel in this sector. 
48~-..he other areas where there is ample scope for consumption of steel is the SSis. In this 
area too the Sub-Committee feels that there should be direct interaction with the entrepreneurs 
with a view to provide them facilities so as to ensure easy process for purchase of steel. This may 
call for an amendment in the present policy of the Government whereby the entrepreneurs have 
to approach the Director of Cottage and Small Scale Industries for the purchase of steel. The Sub· 
Commit~ proposes that entrepreneurs should approach the SAIL directly and if necessary, the 
Central Marketing Organisation may open more branches. It is also pertinent to add here that 
SAIL should encourage the SSI units to manufacture goods which are essential for common 
people. 
49. The Sub-Committee does not find it out place to suggest that Government should also 
explore the possibilities whether there a a need to patronise the engineering industry including 
SSis, and cottage industries with the objective of augmentation of consumption of steel. 
50. Consumption of steel can not substantially grow unless Government comes out in a big 
way for the investment in infrastructural sector. The Sub-Committee appreciates Government's 
policies providing more allocation for the rural development which is essential for the benefit of 
larger section of people. At the same time Sub-Committee also feels equally essential for 
Government to increase investment in urban areas so as to promote the use of steel particularly in 
construction industry. The Sub-Committee, therefore, impress upon the Government to 
encourage private sector to invest in the infrastructural sector and allow them to set up 
engineering and other steel based industries. The Sub-Committee also appeals to the State 
Government to come out with specific schemes I projects promoting steel based industries and if 
necessary, regulations I guidelines be framed so as to change the approach of the entreprenurs as 
well as their own. 
51. The Sub-Committee feels that changes in customs and excise duties have adversely 
affected the domestic producers of iron. and steel as the consumers now have the option to pay 
lower prices on the pretext of availability of cheaper imports. The role of Government in this area 
is very important. The Sub-Committee, therefore, impress upon the Government to rationalise 
the duty structure with a view to protect domestic producers of steel and iron and also ensure that 
the level of duties do not give unnecessary price advantage to the steel producers of other 
countries. The Sub-Committee strongly feels against the reduction of customs duties •.. 
52. The Sub-Committee further desires that Government should also strictly regulate 
imposition of non tariff barriers like fixation . of quota and putting quantitative restrictions on 
import on the Hoes of other steel producing countries. In order to check I reduce the import of 
secondary I defectives of ~R, CR and plates leading to quality deterioration, Government may 
increase import duties on such items and thus accord preference to primary products. 
53. As regards the central excise duties, the Sub-Committee impress upon the Government to 
reduce I exempt the excise duty on freight and distribution from stockyards so as to protect small 
and medium units. 
54. The Sub-Committee impress upon the Government to take necessary steps for 
exploration of markets in other countries and sort out the problems I bottlenecks related .to 
e~ports. 

165 
55. The Sub-Committee feels that extra burdea oa SAIL to the tune of Rs. 200 crore 
mauaUy would adversely affect the maila producers of the Steel Ia publle sector. The Sub­
Committee suggests that the subsidy oa this accouat should be bonae by . the Govenuneat 
itself. 
56. The Sub-Committee feels that after baviaa the experieace of coatiauiaa dowaward 
tread Ia the steel sector both at domestle aad global level tbe Govermneat have to have a very 
cautious approach as il calli for enormou1 lnvestmeal bolla ia public and private Bedor.
